total number of events is held constant at 100000 per predicate, so wider relations mean fewer
time points.

**Two things were wrong with the original log, not one.** The obvious one is the width: 50
tuples is below the size at which a hash table beats a comparison-based tree, which the
micro-benchmark of Section 10 put at about a thousand. The second one only showed up when the
width was raised with the domain scaled along with it (`d = 4n`, chosen to keep the join
selectivity constant): `since` then measures nothing at all, because two operands drawn from
`d² = 16n²` possible pairs almost never share a tuple, so the mapping from tuples to time
stamps that the whole Since structure exists to maintain stays nearly empty. Widening the
relations while keeping the domain wide makes the tables bigger and the structures emptier.

The second family of logs therefore keeps `d² ≈ 2n`, so that the operands overlap heavily and
the structures fill. Median of three, `h/p` is the heap-resident kernel over the pure one:

| width | `once` sparse | `once` dense | `since` dense | `and` dense | `complex` dense |
|------:|--------------:|-------------:|--------------:|------------:|----------------:|
|    50 |         0.80x |        0.96x |         0.97x |       1.03x |           1.03x |
|   250 |         0.80x |        0.97x |         0.93x |       0.98x |           1.01x |
|   500 |         0.86x |        0.97x |         0.94x |       0.98x |           1.00x |
|  1000 |         0.70x |        0.95x |         0.94x |       0.94x |           1.03x |
|  2000 |         0.79x |        0.96x |         0.96x |       1.04x |           1.04x |

So the answer is a qualified yes, and less interesting than it looked. On the sparse-domain
logs `once` gains 20-30%, but that is the heap-resident Since structure of Section 12 doing
what it already did on the original log, not a width effect: the gain is the same at 50 tuples
as at 2000. On the dense logs, where the structures actually fill, everything sits within a few
percent of the pure kernel at every width. The width was never what held the heap maps back --
and it was certainly not what held the join back, which was flat at every width for a reason
that has nothing to do with the log.

### 14.1 The binary join was never reached

`bin_join_j` (Section 8's imperative probe, lifted into the heap monad) showed exactly no
effect at any width because a conjunction is not evaluated by `bin_join`. `convert_multiway`
compiles conjunctions to `MAnds`, which `meval` evaluates with `mmulti_join`; `bin_join` is
reached only from `MAnd`, which survives only for the few shapes multi-way conversion leaves
alone. The left-deep sequence of binary joins and the anti-joins that follow it
(`mmulti_join_pos`, `mmulti_join_neg`, `mmulti_join_pw`) are therefore lifted into the heap
monad as well, and the `MAnds` case of the evaluation function goes through them.

Making the imperative join reachable is what turned it from a no-op into a measurable effect --
in both directions. Three things had to be right, and each was found by measuring the wrong
one:

1. **Do not take the equal-column case from the tree.** When both operands have the same
   columns the join is `t ∩ t'` or `t - t'`. On two red-black trees over the same order that is
   a linear merge exploiting their shared structure, not a lookup per tuple. Indexing instead
   made P2 eleven times slower (1.3 s to 14.3 s).
2. **Do not take the containment cases either.** When one column set contains the other, which
   side is indexed is forced by the shape of the join. The index is then frequently the larger
   table, built to be probed a few times -- the anti-joins of P2 and P3 are exactly this shape.
   Routing them through an index made P3 fifty times slower (1.1 s to 50.7 s), and restricting
   it to calls whose index is no longer than the probing side still left P2 60% slower.
3. **Index the smaller side.** In the remaining case either table may be indexed, and the pure
   code picks the smaller (`if card t ≤ card t' then probe_join_n n K t t' else ... t' t`).
   Indexing `t'` unconditionally, as the first version did, builds a big index and reads it a
   few times whenever the operands differ in size. On the standard `complex` benchmark that is
   the difference between a 27% loss and a 16% gain.

What is left for the index is the case the pure code has no good algorithm for: neither column
set contains the other, so it either builds a pure index or, when the operands are not complete
on the common columns, degenerates to a nested loop. There the heap index wins, and a size
threshold for it (exposed as `-heap_join_min`) turned out to be unnecessary once the case and
the side were right -- an index of any size was at least as good as the pure code on every
benchmark, so the flag defaults to zero.

Standard suite, median of five, all four configurations timed in one interleaved pass so that
they share the state of the machine. "heap-old" is the kernel of Section 13, in which
`bin_join_j` existed but was unreachable; "heap-new" is this section's.

| formula      | MonPoly | VeriMon | heap-old | heap-new |
|--------------|--------:|--------:|---------:|---------:|
| P2           |   0.44s |   1.22s |    1.27s |    1.34s |
| P3           |   0.48s |   1.15s |    1.09s |    1.10s |
| `and`        |   0.16s |   0.25s |    0.26s |    0.23s |
| `andnot`     |   0.15s |   0.17s |    0.17s |    0.17s |
| `once`       |   0.72s |   1.03s |    0.87s |    0.84s |
| `since`      |   0.24s |   0.34s |    0.37s |    0.34s |
| `eventually` |   0.47s |   0.81s |    0.81s |    0.80s |
| `until`      |   0.24s |   0.40s |    0.41s |    0.40s |
| `agg`        |   0.17s |   0.30s |    0.30s |    0.30s |
| `complex`    |   0.82s |   1.92s |    1.70s |    1.58s |

Reaching the multi-way join is worth 7% on `complex` (1.70 s to 1.58 s) and 12% on `and`
(0.26 s to 0.23 s) on top of what the heap-resident Since structures already gave, and costs 6%
on P2, whose conjunctions are the equal-column and containment shapes that stay with the pure
code and now pay for the case analysis that decides so. Against MonPoly the heap kernel stands
at 1.35x on `and`, 1.17x on `once`, 1.42x on `since`, 1.93x on `complex` and 3.0x on P2; the
pure kernel is at 1.56x, 1.43x, 1.42x, 2.34x and 2.8x.

### 14.2 Memoizing the hash

Hashing a tuple walks its whole representation and is the most expensive step of every map
operation, so the bucket entry now carries the hash next to the key and the value. Growing a
table redistributes its entries from the stored hashes without looking at a key again; a bucket
scan rejects a colliding entry by comparing two machine integers before it compares two tuples;
capacities are powers of two, so the bucket index is a mask rather than a division; and every
operation has a variant taking the hash as an argument, so the loops of the Since structure, the
Until structure and the join index -- which look a key up and then update or delete it -- hash
it once instead of twice.

It is a correct optimization and it is worth almost nothing here: 0.3% on `once` and 0.24% on
`since` (exact instruction counts, `callgrind`). The reason is that the earlier estimate came
from the profile of the heap-resident *Until* structure, where rehashing during repeated
forward merging accounted for 7.6 million hash calls; that structure is disabled (Section 13.1
explains why it cannot work). In the Since structures that remain, `caml_hash` is 4.5% of the
run, so removing part of it cannot show up. The profile of the heap path is now flat and led by
the collector (about 18%: `caml_oldify_one`, `do_some_marking`, `caml_modify`, `sweep_slice`,
`bf_allocate`), the tree comparisons of the tables themselves (about 11%: `do_compare_val`,
`compare_tuple`, `comparator_list`, `compare_event_data`) and parsing the log (about 8%).

**What this says about where to go next.** The tables are still Containers red-black sets;
only the mappings inside the auxiliary structures moved to the heap. The comparisons and a
large part of the allocation are in the tables, not in the structures, and no amount of work on
the maps will reach them.

## 15. Where the allocation goes (measurements)

Section 14 ended by pointing at the collector and the tables. This section measures both, and
finds that the gap to MonPoly is one shape of allocation repeated everywhere.

### 15.1 The gap is in the operators

Instruction counts (`callgrind`) and allocation counts (`OCAMLRUNPARAM=v=0x400`) on P2:

| | MonPoly | VeriMon | ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| instructions | 3474M | 9257M | 2.7x |
| minor words | 69.4M | 504.8M | **7.3x** |
| major (promoted) words | 3.96M | 28.7M | 7.2x |

Decomposing the instruction gap by category: the collector is 36% of it and VeriMon's own code
55%; everything else rounds to noise. Since most of that own code is the code that allocates,
the gap is an allocation problem.

Running the same log under a trivial formula separates the front end from the operators. The
front end is shared -- both monitors use MonPoly's parser -- so what differs there is the
adapter that converts each event into a tuple and builds the table:

| | MonPoly | VeriMon |
|---|---:|---:|
| trivial formula | 48.9M | 141.9M |
| P2 | 69.4M | 504.8M |
| difference (the operators) | **20.5M** | **362.9M** |

So the operators allocate 17.7 times as much, and the adapter a further 93M.

### 15.2 Where it goes, and a correction

`Gc.Memprof` samples allocations and records their call stacks; `src/memprof_hook.ml` turns
that into a table of allocation by site, enabled with `MONPOLY_MEMPROF=<rate>` and costing
nothing when the variable is unset.

**The first ranking this produced was wrong, and the way it was wrong is worth recording.**
Memprof samples each allocated *word* with probability `rate`, so a block of `n` words yields
`Binomial(n, rate)` samples and the number of samples at a site, divided by the rate, estimates
the words allocated there. The hook instead weighted each sample by the size of its block, which
estimates `n²` and ranks a handful of large blocks above very many small ones. Under that
estimator the red-black tree insertions dominated every benchmark and the conclusion was that
nearly all of the allocation is one shape. Under the correct one they do not, and it is not.

Corrected, the profile is flat. No site is above 6% of P2:

| P2, pure kernel | share |
|---|---:|
| `safe_hd_t` rebuilding a queue it does not change | 6.1% |
| the verdict printer's per-time-point buffer | 5.2% |
| `plus_nata` under `suc` (boxed `nat` arithmetic) | 2.8% |
| the log lexer | 2.6% |
| `append_queue` | 1.9% |
| `rep_isom` (the `code_dt` wrapper around `safe_hd`) | 1.8% |
| `rev` under `fold` | 1.6% |

For `once` the ranking is different again -- `folda`, the fold by which `union_comp` inserts a
small set into a large one, is 8.5%; the adapter's tuple conversion is 7.7% and 6.2%; `balance`
inside `rbt_comp_ins` is 3.5% and 3.2%. So repeated tree insertion *is* the largest category
there, at around 18% of allocation rather than nearly all of it.

The lesson is not only about the estimator. Allocation volume is not by itself the cost: OCaml's
minor heap allocates by bumping a pointer, about two instructions a word, and collects
short-lived garbage for nothing. What costs is promotion -- and P2 promotes 28.7M words against
MonPoly's 3.96M. A change that removes minor garbage without removing work can therefore be a
regression, and Section 15.4 contains one.

### 15.3 What the heap-resident maps did and did not do

The heap-resident maps of Sections 12 and 14 do not touch this. Allocation with and without
`-heap_maps` on the standard log:

| formula | MonPoly | VeriMon | `-heap_maps` |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `and` | 28.4M | 95.9M | 103.4M |
| `once` | 93.4M | 257.2M | 222.4M |
| `since` | 34.1M | 124.9M | 126.4M |
| `until` | 32.3M | 138.5M | 139.0M |
| `complex` | 132.9M | 540.6M | 567.9M |

Only `once` improves; `and` and `complex` get worse, because the heap index allocates a bucket
array per call while the result table is still built by repeated insertion. Those sections moved
the *mappings inside the auxiliary structures* to the heap and left the *tables flowing between
the operators* where they were, which is why they were worth single-digit percentages: they
changed what a lookup costs, not what a table costs to build.

### 15.4 Three changes measured against that diagnosis

Wall-clock differences of a few percent are below the noise of this machine, so the numbers
below are exact instruction counts (`callgrind`) and exact allocation counts.

**The deque reads index 0 after dropping the head (Section 13.1's open item).**
`eval_step_dq` took the head mapping and the one behind it, combined them and dropped the head.
`dq_norm` only rebalances when the front list is empty, so when the front holds exactly one
element the access to index 1 falls into the back list at its far end, and `dq_nth` and `dq_upd`
both walk it. Dropping the head first and reading and writing index 0 -- which `dq_norm`
guarantees to be at the front -- makes both constant time.

`rel_a2` sees a deque only through `dq_inv`, `dq_len` and `dq_nth`, so the new form relates the
refined structure to the same abstract one. The first attempt stated that as a congruence and
rewrote the goal with it; the congruence proves but the rewrite did not fire, so the lemma is
instead proved by mirroring `rel_a2_step`'s own proof with `dq_nth_upd_tl` in place of
`dq_nth_tl_upd`. `Code_Opt.thy` restates `eval_step_dq` as a code equation and had to be updated
alongside the definition.

| | before | after | |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| P2, pure kernel | 9257.0M | 9019.3M | **-2.6%** |
| P2, `-heap_maps` | 9853.5M | 9590.8M | **-2.7%** |

P3 is unchanged, as expected: it has no Until, so `eval_step_dq` never runs.

**Tables built in one pass.** An operator that builds its result table by inserting tuples one
at a time into a persistent red-black tree copies the root-to-leaf path it descends per tuple,
so an `n`-tuple table costs about `n log n` nodes where one pass costs `n`. This is not the
whole of the allocation -- see the correction in 15.2 -- but it is the largest single category
for `once`, at around 18%. `table_of_unsorted` sorts the tuples with a bottom-up merge sort over
`ed_comparator` that drops duplicates as it merges -- exactly the form `rbt_of_sorted_list`
checks for -- and then builds the tree in one pass. The imperative join now collects its tuples
in a list and builds the table once instead of inserting into it.

Sorting only pays once the table is big enough, because the sort costs `n log n` comparisons on
top of the ones the insertions would have done. Unthresholded it gains on `complex` and loses on
`and`, whose join result is about a dozen tuples per time point:

| `-heap_maps` | before | `bulk_min=0` | `bulk_min=64` |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `complex` | 11848.6M | 11701.2M | **11658.6M (-1.6%)** |
| `and` | 2154.3M | 2210.9M | 2170.3M (+0.7%) |
| `complex` minor words | 567.9M | | **531.7M (-6.4%)** |

The threshold is a flag (`-bulk_min`, default 64) so it can be moved without regenerating the
kernel. The residual 0.7% on `and` is the list detour itself, taken and then discarded when the
result turns out to be short.

**The fused tuple comparator is still not reached.** `ed_comparator` (Section 7) is proved equal
to `comparator_list (comparator_option comparator_event_data)`, and the profile says the generic
chain costs about 2.6% of P2: 5.88M indirect calls from `comparator_list` to its element
comparator, 2.21M more from `comparator_option`, and 2.2M from `rbt_comp_lookup` and
`rbt_comp_ins`, each going through `caml_apply2`. Yet before this section `ed_comparator` did not
appear in `verified.ml` at all.

The reason is that the dictionary at a composite type cannot be specialised: `ccompare` for
`event_data option list` is `ccompare_lista (ccompare_option ccompare_event_data)`, whose body
binds the element comparator at run time, so no code equation can see through it. What *can*
reach it is monomorphisation: instantiating `Set.member` and the RBT operations below it at
`event_data tuple` resolves `ID CCOMPARE(event_data tuple)` to `Some ed_comparator` by
`etuple_dict_simps`. A probe session confirms the chain builds -- but its generated code still
reads

    let rec probe1 xa x = unbox ed_member_v xa x;;

because `Monomorphize.box` has already registered `Set.member = unbox ed_member_v` as a
preprocessor rule and it shadows the monomorphisation redirect. Routing the comparator through
therefore means replacing the boxed treatment of the hot set and mapping operations by
monomorphisation, not adding to it -- a well-specified change with a measured ceiling of about
2.6% of P2, weighed against whatever the boxes themselves are worth (Section 7).

`ed_comparator` does now appear in the generated kernel, called by the merge sort above. It is
in the code; it is the trees that do not use it.

### 15.5 The queue and the printer

The two largest sites of 15.2 are both cases of doing work whose result is thrown away.

**The queue rebuilds queues it does not change.** `Queue.thy` writes every case of every
operation so that it names its result, which reads well and generates badly:

    safe_hd_t (f # fs, l # ls) = (Some f, (f # fs, l # ls))

allocates two cons cells and a pair equal to the ones it was given, and `safe_hd` runs once per
time point per queue. Code equations for `safe_hd_t`, `safe_last_t`, `prepend_queue_t`,
`replace_hd_t` and `tl_queue_t` return the argument wherever the result *is* the argument; each
is one `cases` and an `auto`, and nothing else about the queue changes.

**The printer built a header it usually threw away.** `show_results` created a 1KB buffer and
formatted `"@<ts> (time point <i>): "` with `Printf.sprintf` at every time point, then discarded
both unless that time point had a verdict -- which most do not. It now writes nothing until the
first tuple arrives, and reuses one buffer across the run.

A first attempt at this reused the buffer but kept the header. That cut allocation by 5.4% and
made P2 **1.35% slower**: the discarded buffers were minor garbage that cost almost nothing,
while the shared buffer sits in the major heap and is a worse write target. What was actually
expensive was the format interpretation, which the first attempt left in place.

Exact counts, the two changes composed, on the kernel as of Section 15.4:

| | instructions | | minor words | |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| P2 before | 9000.9M | | 497.0M | |
| P2 + printer | 8559.8M | -4.9% | 458.3M | -7.8% |
| P2 + printer + queue | **8229.8M** | **-8.6%** | **435.0M** | **-12.5%** |
| P3 before | 7067.6M | | 495.3M | |
| P3 + printer | 6497.5M | -8.1% | 456.0M | -7.9% |
| P3 + printer + queue | **6390.8M** | **-9.6%** | **440.1M** | **-11.1%** |

Both are verified-kernel changes and apply with or without `-heap_maps`; the queue equations are
proved, and the printer is checked by the cram suites, which compare output byte for byte. The
differential suite agrees on 678 verified invocations with 0 differing.

### 15.6 What this says about closing the gap

The corrected profile changes the priorities. The trees are one category among several, and the
categories above them were not algorithmic at all -- they were work whose result was discarded.
What remains, in the order the measurements support:

* **Finish the queue.** `safe_hd` still goes through the `code_dt` isomorphism: `safe_hd_aux`
  wraps the result in a datatype and `rep_isom` unpacks it into a fresh pair, so a call that now
  allocates nothing in `safe_hd_t` still allocates two blocks around it (1.8% of P2 before these
  changes). Removing that means giving `safe_hd` a code equation that does not go through the
  lifted product, which is a question about `lift_definition(code_dt)` rather than about queues.
* **One-pass construction in the pure kernel** was tried for the probe join and does not work.
  Section 15.7 records what was measured and why.
* **Boxed `nat`** is 2.8% of P2 in allocation alone and more in arithmetic, and is the next
  largest category after the two fixed here. It was excluded from this round by choice, not by
  measurement.
* **Heap-resident tables do not address any of this.** They change what a lookup costs, not what
  a table costs to build; Section 15.3 measures `-heap_maps` allocation as unchanged or worse.

### 15.7 One-pass construction for the probe join: a negative result

The shape that 15.2 identifies -- an operator building its result table by inserting tuples one
at a time -- suggests collecting the tuples in a list and building the tree once, which costs
`n` nodes instead of about `n log n`. That was done for the probe join, in both kernels, and
measured. It is slower, and the reason is worth recording.

**The first attempt was not reached at all.** A code equation for `probe_join` measured as
*exactly* no change: allocation identical to the word on both benchmarks and both logs,
instructions within 0.002%. `probe_join_n` has its own code equation for the `RBT_set` case --
the masked probe join of Section 7f -- which bypasses `probe_join` for the only representation
that ever occurs. An identical-to-the-word measurement is not a small effect; it says the code
is unreachable. The equation was moved to `probe_join_n`.

**Reached, it loses.** Exact instruction counts for `complex`, one-pass against repeated
insertion, with the threshold flag switching between them:

| | sparse log (50 tuples/tp) | dense log (1000 tuples/tp, domain 45) |
|---|---:|---:|
| pure kernel, repeated insertion | 14038.8M | 42010.8M |
| pure kernel, one pass | 13985.1M (-0.4%) | **55521.1M (+32%)** |
| `-heap_maps`, repeated insertion | 11780.4M | 41416.3M |
| `-heap_maps`, one pass | 11305.2M (-4.0%) | **52823.9M (+27%)** |

Allocation does fall as predicted -- 655.9M to 358.8M words on `and` over the dense log, a 45%
cut -- and the run still gets a third slower. This is the same lesson as the printer buffer in
15.5, in a sharper form: allocation is not the cost.

**Why.** Inserting into a set deduplicates as it goes. A join that produces `n` tuples of which
`k` are distinct costs `n log k` comparisons and keeps a tree of `k` nodes. Collecting the
tuples in a list defers the deduplication to the sort, which then does `n log n` comparisons on
the undeduplicated count. The dense log draws 1000 events per time point from a domain of 45,
so its joins produce many more tuples than they have distinct results, and `n log n` against
`n log k` is exactly the factor measured.

No threshold on the length of the list can separate the two cases, because `k` is not known
until the work is done. Both code equations were therefore reverted; the lemmas are kept, proved
and unused, next to this explanation.

**What would work.** The construction is right for tuples one already has in a list and wrong
for tuples one is generating. To get both, the deduplication has to happen during generation but
more cheaply than a tree insertion -- a hash set, which the imperative kernel already has as
`itbl`: `n` hashes, then `k log k` comparisons to sort the survivors and `k` nodes to build. For
`n` much larger than `k` that beats both. That is the shape to try next, and it only exists in
the imperative kernel.

### 15.8 Comparing black heights without computing them

`bheight` walks a tree's entire left spine and allocates a boxed `nat` per level. Every set
union calls it three times: `small_rbt t = bheight t < 6` decides whether to bulk-build or
insert, and `flip_rbt t1 t2 = bheight t2 < bheight t1` decides which side to iterate over.
`rbt_join` and its two helpers compare two black heights at every step of the join. On P2,
`bheight` accounted for 3.6M of the monitor's 6.7M calls to `suc`.

All of these are comparisons, and a comparison does not need the numbers. `bh_le t1 t2`
descends the two left spines in lockstep and answers as soon as one of them runs out;
`bh_lt k t` counts a constant down and stops as soon as the tree exceeds it. Neither
allocates, and both stop earlier than `bheight` could.

The five constants get code equations phrased in the new tests, proved equal to the originals
via `bh_le_correct` and `bh_lt_correct` (`thys/MFODL_Monitor_Devel/RBT_set_opt.thy`).

| | before | after |
|---|---:|---:|
| P2 instructions, default GC | 8249.2M | 8296.9M (+0.6%) |
| P2 instructions, compaction off | 7993.1M | 7898.7M (**-1.2%**) |
| P3 instructions, default GC | 6381.0M | 6279.0M (**-1.6%**) |
| P2 minor words | 434.9M | 431.3M (-0.8%) |

The first row is the interesting one, and it is not a regression. Per-function costs show the
change doing exactly what it was meant to: `bheight` -37.9M, `suc` -24.1M, `Z.add` -33.3M,
a total of 95M fewer instructions in the monitor's own code. What moved the other way is the
major collector -- `do_compaction` +46.1M, `do_some_marking` +43.3M, `invert_pointer_at`
+14.4M -- because the slightly different heap crossed a fragmentation threshold and compacted
one more time. Compaction is a discrete event triggered by a ratio, not a cost proportional to
anything the change touches; running both binaries with compaction disabled isolates the real
effect, and it is the -1.2% the function-level counts predict.

This is worth stating as a rule for the rest of this document: a difference of well under a
percent in a whole-program instruction count can be a single major-heap event rather than a
property of the code. Where such a number matters, it needs the per-function breakdown or a
run with compaction off before it is read as a result.

### 15.9 Finishing the queue

Section 15.6 put `safe_hd` first on the list, and the buffer operations around it turned out to
be the same problem three times over.

`safe_hd` returns a pair whose second component is a queue. The code generator cannot state a
code equation for that directly, so `lift_definition (code_dt)` introduces an intermediate
datatype: the generated `safe_hd` builds a value of it and `rep_isom` takes it apart into a
fresh pair. Two blocks allocated and two projections executed around a function that, since
the code equations of Section 15.5, allocates nothing in the common case. Splitting `safe_hd`
into `hd_queue` and `norm_queue` gives each half a result type the generator handles on its
own, and the pair is built once.

The head has to be read off the *normalised* queue, not off the argument: on a normalised
queue it is the first element of the front list, while on the argument it can be the last
element of the back list. Reading it off the argument -- the first version measured here -- put
a second pass over the back list next to the reversal that was already there, and `last`
appeared in the profile at 12.6M instructions. `safe_hd q = (let q' = norm_queue q in
(hd_queue q', q'))` keeps the single pass.

`mbuf_t_cases` asked `safe_hd` for a pair it immediately split, then called `tl_queue` on the
queue `safe_hd` had just rebuilt -- two rebuilds for one pop. `mbuf_t_hd` and `mbuf_t_tl` ask
for the two halves separately and `pop_queue` normalises and drops in a single step. In the
empty case the buffer is returned unchanged, so nothing is allocated at all.

`mbuf_t_append` wrapped and unwrapped the queue once per appended element; `append_queue_list`
conses the whole list onto the back in one pass.

P2, cumulative over Sections 15.8 and 15.9:

| | before 15.8 | after 15.8 | after 15.9 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| instructions, compaction off | 7993.1M | 7898.7M | **7863.1M** (-1.63%) |
| instructions, default GC | 8249.2M | 8296.9M | 8214.0M (-0.43%) |
| minor words | 434.9M | 431.3M | **403.8M** (-7.1%) |
| promoted words | 27.03M | 27.27M | 27.03M |

P3 instructions fall from 6381.0M to 6240.0M (-2.21%). All 678 differential tests agree and
the cram suite passes.

**The ratio between the two rows is the lesson.** 31M fewer words allocated bought 130M
instructions -- about four instructions per word. That is the cost of bumping the allocation
pointer and of the minor collector walking a slightly smaller live set; it is *not* the 6.8
instructions per word that dividing the collector's total by the word count suggests. Promoted
words did not move at all, and promotion is what the major collector charges for. Sections
15.5 and 15.7 each recorded a version of this; three measurements in, the shape is clear:
**removing minor allocation that was already dying young pays about four instructions a word,
and nothing more.** Allocation counts are a good way to find code that does unnecessary work,
and a bad way to predict how much removing it will save.

What did pay here was the work removed alongside the allocation: `rep_isom` -50.9M,
`safe_hd` -47.5M against its own previous version, `append_queue` -25.2M, `tl_queue_t` -20.5M,
`norm_queue_t` -16.2M.

Wall clock against MonPoly on the standard suite is now 2.52x on P2 and 2.23x on P3 (median of
five runs; the machine's run-to-run spread is about 5%), against instruction-count ratios of
2.36x and 2.16x.

### 15.10 The generic application path

Four of the ten most expensive symbols on P2 belong to neither the monitor nor the collector:
anonymous closures at 3.37%, `fold` at 1.91%, `caml_apply2` at 1.79% and `caml_tuplify2` at
1.27% -- 8.3% of the run spent passing functions to functions. Two library code equations turn
a direct recursion into exactly that.

`rev` is generated as a fold over `Cons`, so reversing a list allocates nothing extra but
applies a closure through OCaml's generic application path once per element. `rev_prepend` is
the same two-argument recursion written directly. `RBT_Set2.fold` discards each entry's value
by wrapping the element function in a closure: one allocation per fold and two indirect calls
per element instead of one. `rbt_fold_key` takes the element function directly.

| P2 | before | after |
|---|---:|---:|
| `fold` | 156.8M | 76.2M |
| anonymous closures | 276.7M | 234.9M |
| `caml_apply2` | 61.2M | 38.8M |
| `do_some_marking` | 571.6M | 530.3M |
| instructions, compaction off | 7863.1M | **7750.1M** (-1.44%) |
| instructions, default GC | 8214.0M | 8061.4M (-1.86%) |

P3 falls 1.35% with compaction off. This is the best return per line of proof in this section:
two code equations, no change to any data structure, and the effect is in the monitor's own
instruction count rather than in allocation, which barely moved (403.8M to 400.3M words).

What is left of the category is a long tail -- the largest remaining anonymous function is
0.44% of P2 -- so there is no second round of this.

Cumulative over Sections 15.8 to 15.10, against the binary that opened Section 15.8:

| | before | after |
|---|---:|---:|
| P2 instructions, compaction off | 7993.1M | **7750.1M** (-3.04%) |
| P2 instructions, default GC | 8249.2M | 8061.4M (-2.28%) |
| P2 minor words | 434.9M | 400.3M (-8.0%) |
| P3 instructions, default GC | 6381.0M | 6223.6M (-2.47%) |

### 15.11 The last composite dictionaries

Section 7 boxed the container operations at the tuple type so their dictionaries are built once
when the module starts. Four places were left out, and the profile names them precisely: on P2,
`ccompare_lista` and `ccompare_optiona` are each entered 748k times and `ccompare_proda`
603k times, and every entry allocates a record, an option and a partially applied comparator.
The callers are

* `table_of_list`, the bulk construction of a table (201k) -- it calls `rbt_of_sorted_list`,
  which is defined after the first round of boxes and so was never in it;
* `rbt_fold`, the tree traversal the output printer uses (198k) -- its accumulator type is
  polymorphic, so the operation itself cannot be boxed, but the projection it needs out of the
  tree can be;
* `meeval`'s predicate lookup (402k) and `insert_table_into_db` (201k) -- the database is keyed
  by a name and an arity, so its dictionary is a `ccompare_prod` rather than a tuple
  dictionary, and no rule covered it;
* `until_step_dq` and `until_elem_step_dq` (150k), which are polymorphic and build the tuple
  dictionaries from their own dictionary arguments, like the joins before Section 7.

The first four become boxes, the last two monomorphic copies. One further rule was needed:
the redirections rewrite *an operation applied to an argument*, so an operation written
point-free is out of their reach -- `fold (∪) (map fst taken) {}` in the deque step kept the
polymorphic union. `fold_map_unfold` applies the two functions in one step, which removes the
intermediate list as well.

| P2 | before | after |
|---|---:|---:|
| `ccompare_lista` | 19.5M | 6.5M |
| `ccompare_optiona` | 19.5M | 6.5M |
| `meeval` | 99.2M | 89.2M |
| minor words | 400.3M | **374.2M** (-6.5%) |
| instructions, compaction off | 7750.1M | **7630.6M** (-1.54%) |

P3 falls from 6155.8M to 6098.1M with compaction off, and from 6223.6M to 6098.1M with the
default settings.

### 15.12 Two warnings about how to measure this

**Compaction makes whole-program instruction counts unreliable below a few percent.** With the
default settings P2 *rises* 2.0% across the change of Section 15.11 while every symbol the
change touches falls: `do_compaction` +87.4M, `do_some_marking` +68.6M, `invert_pointer_at`
+27.7M, `sweep_slice` +20.0M. VeriMon's default `space_overhead` of 500 leaves the P2 run
sitting on a compaction threshold, so a change of a few million words flips an expensive
compaction on or off. Section 15.8 saw the same thing in the other direction. Every instruction
count in Sections 15.8 to 15.11 is therefore reported with compaction disabled as well, and the
compaction-off number is the one that tracks the per-symbol counts.

**Instruction counts and wall clock diverge once the collector's behaviour changes.** Disabling
compaction is worth 7.2% of P2's instructions and between -2.9% and +1.1% of its wall clock:
compaction spends instructions to buy locality, and on this workload the trade is roughly
even. So it was not made the default. The same gap appears in `space_overhead`: raising it from
500 to 4000 is worth 10.1% of P2's instructions but 3.8% of its wall clock, and costs four
times the peak memory (155MB against 38MB on P2, 177MB against 62MB on the dense `complex`
benchmark). At 2000 it is worth about 4% of wall clock for twice the memory. The flag
`-gc_space_overhead` exists for users who want to make that trade; the default stays at 500,
because `space_overhead` is a ratio of live data and VeriMon's workloads are the ones with
large live state.

The rule that follows: **judge a change that only moves code by instruction counts, and a change
that moves the collector by wall clock.**

### 15.13 Pairs taken apart in the parameter

A function whose parameter is a pattern -- `let rec fst (a, b) = a` -- is compiled by OCaml as a
function of the pattern's *components*, and an application of it to a pair that is not written
out at the call site goes through `caml_tuplify2`, an adapter that rebuilds the pair and takes
it apart again. On P2 that adapter is entered 11.8 million times, and it costs 104.3M
instructions, 1.27% of the run. The callers are ordinary: `fst` 3.67M, `hd_queue_t` 2.99M,
`snd` 1.68M, `pop_queue_t` 1.40M.

Matching on the argument inside the body instead gives an ordinary one-argument function. An
`export_code` probe confirms the shape:

```
  lemma "fst p = (case p of (a, b) => a)"   -->  let rec fst p = (let (a, _) = p in a);;
  lemma "fst (a, b) = a"                    -->  let rec fst (a, b) = a;;
```

`fst`, `snd`, `hd_queue_t` and `pop_queue_t` get the first form.

| P2 | before | after |
|---|---:|---:|
| `caml_tuplify2` | 104.3M | **16.7M** (-84%) |
| `mbuf_t_cases` | 46.5M | 33.6M |
| instructions, compaction off | 7630.6M | **7544.2M** (-1.13%) |

P3 falls from 6098.1M to 5981.7M (-1.91%) -- and here the default-GC count agrees, because
the change does not move allocation.

**This is worth remembering as a shape, not just as a fix.** Isabelle writes pattern-matching
definitions because that is how the functions are defined; OCaml reads a pattern in the
parameter as a calling convention. Nothing in the Isabelle source suggests that `fst` costs
more than a field load. The only way to see it was to count entries into a runtime symbol that
belongs to neither the monitor nor the collector, and then ask what the code generator had
produced. Section 15.10 found the same thing one level up, in `caml_apply2`.

| P3 instructions, default GC | 6381.0M | **5981.7M** (-6.26%) |
| P2 minor words | 434.9M | 374.2M (-14.0%) |

| P3 | 0.44s | 0.92s | 0.82s | 2.09x | **1.86x** |

### 15.15 Comparing without a dictionary where the type is known

`caml_apply2` -- OCaml's path for applying a closure of unknown arity to two arguments -- is
entered 22.2 million times on P2. Following it to its callers turns up something that has
nothing to do with sets or tuples: `max` and `min`.

They are not class parameters but plain overloaded definitions, so the generated code has one
copy, `max _A a b = (if less_eq _A a b then b else a)`, that calls the ordering through a
dictionary. `nat_of_integer k = Nat (max 0 k)` and subtraction on `nat`, which goes through the
same, account for six million of those entries -- each an indirect call into `Z.leq` and from
there into `ml_z_compare`, three levels to decide whether a machine integer is negative.

`Monomorphize.monomorphize_cmd` at `integer`, `nat` and `event_data` gives
`ed_max_integer a b = (if Z.leq a b then b else a)`: a direct call.

| P2 | before | after |
|---|---:|---:|
| `caml_apply2` (both entry points) | 182.6M | 134.4M |
| `Z.leq` | 65.7M | 0 |
| `minus_nata` | 95.2M | 75.4M |
| `nat_of_integer` | 42.2M | 32.1M |
| instructions, compaction off | 7544.2M | **7400.0M** (-1.91%) |

P3 falls from 5981.7M to 5885.4M (-1.61%).

This is the same shape as Sections 15.10 and 15.13 for the third time: the Isabelle source says
`max 0 k`, and what that costs depends on a code-generator decision -- here, that an overloaded
constant which is not a class parameter gets one dictionary-passing copy -- that is invisible
from the source. All three were found by counting entries into runtime symbols that belong to
neither the monitor nor the collector, and none of them would have been found by reading the
formalisation.

### 15.17 One more closure, and where P2's gap actually is

`RBT_Mapping2.lookup` is stated point-free, so the generated code applies it to the tree, gets a
closure back, and applies that to the key: one closure allocated per lookup, 1.3% of everything
the monitor allocates on P2. Eta-expanding the code equation removes it -- P2 and P3 each fall
0.36% with compaction off, minor allocation 0.55%.

That is the end of what the flat profile has to give: after nine changes the largest single
identifiable site left is under 2%, and the next thing worth doing is not another one of these.

**Section 15 in total**, against the binary that opened Section 15.8:

| | before | after |
|---|---:|---:|
| P2 instructions, compaction off | 7993.1M | **7373.1M** (-7.76%) |
| P3 instructions, compaction off | ~6381M | **5864.4M** (-8.1%) |
| P2 minor words | 434.9M | 372.2M (-14.4%) |

### 15.18 Where P2's gap is

P2 is the benchmark that has not moved: 2.41x where the single-operator benchmarks are between
1.07x and 1.75x. Two facts about its log explain why it is different from everything else in
Section 14's suite. It has **201157 time points but only 106 distinct timestamps** -- about 1900
time points per timestamp -- so a bounded-future interval of six time units spans some eleven
thousand time points; and it carries about one event per time point, so nothing in it is
dominated by the size of a table.

Evaluating the formula piece by piece on that log localises the gap exactly:

| | MonPoly | VeriMon | ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `trans(c,t,a)` | 0.37s | 0.36s | 0.97x |
| `trans(c,t,a) AND 2000 < a` | 0.23s | 0.27s | 1.17x |
| `trans(c,t,a) AND report(t)` | 0.22s | 0.29s | 1.32x |
| `trans(c,t,a) AND NOT report(t)` | 0.42s | 0.42s | **1.00x** |
| `trans(c,t,a) AND NOT ONCE[0,6) report(t)` | 0.55s | 0.97s | 1.76x |
| `trans(c,t,a) AND NOT EVENTUALLY[0,6) report(t)` | 0.59s | 1.22s | **2.07x** |
| the whole of P2 | 0.41s | 0.99s | 2.41x |

Predicates, filters, joins and anti-joins are at or near parity. The temporal operators are the
gap, and the Until structure is the worse of the two: the Eventually costs MonPoly 0.17s and
VeriMon 0.80s, a factor of 4.7.

What that 0.80s consists of is worth recording, because it is not what one would guess.
`eval_step_dq` is entered exactly once per time point and `max_tstp` only 25632 times in the
whole run, so the structure is not doing work proportional to the window at every step. The cost
is allocation: the Eventually adds 271.2M minor words and 31.7M promoted words to the same
formula without it, against MonPoly's 20.1M and 6.0M -- 13.5x the minor allocation and 5.3x the
promotion, for the same operator on the same log. Essentially all of P2's major-collector work
belongs to this one structure, and it is spread over dozens of sites of one to three percent
each, in exactly the shape Section 15 has been grinding down everywhere else.

Two ways out are already closed:

* **An imperative Until structure is a measured negative result**, recorded in
  `MFODL_Monitor_Imp/Until_Imp2.thy`: sixty times slower than the pure deque, because every step
  merges the expiring time point's mapping into the next one, and a hash table has to read and
  re-insert every entry where a persistent tree shares the parts that do not change.
* **The join is not the problem here.** On a log with many time points and few tuples the join
  *is* the outlier -- `and` is 2.06x where `once` is 0.88x and `eventually` 1.04x, because the
  column-set analysis in `bin_join` is redone at every call and `sorted_list_subset_fusion` and
  its iterator cost 2.2% of that run. On P2 the same tests cost 0.2%, because P2's joins take
  the subset branch instead.

The remaining lever on P2 is the structure itself. `EVENTUALLY I phi` is rewritten by the
adapter into `Until TT I phi` (`verified_adapter.ml`), so it is evaluated by the general Until
machinery: a tuple-to-timestamp mapping per time point in the window. When the left operand is
closed -- which is what `args_L args = {}` says, and what every `EVENTUALLY`, `ALWAYS`, `ONCE`
and `HISTORICALLY` produces -- "the left operand held throughout `[i,j)`" is a predicate on time
points alone, not on tuples, and the answer at `i` is the union of the right operand's tables
over a contiguous run whose two ends both move right monotonically. That is a sliding-window
union, and it needs no per-time-point tuple mapping at all.

## 16. An Until structure for a closed left operand

Section 15.18 left P2's gap in one place, and this is the structure that closes most of it.

### 16.1 What the general structure is asked to do

`EVENTUALLY I phi` is not an operator of the monitor: the front end rewrites it into
`Until TT I phi` (`verified_adapter.ml`), and `ALWAYS`, `ONCE` and `HISTORICALLY` likewise turn
into a temporal operator whose left operand is closed. The general Until structure therefore
carries, for every time point in the window, a mapping from tuples to the last time point at
which the left operand held -- machinery for a dependence on the valuation that a closed left
operand does not have.

A closed left operand holds either for every valuation or for none. So "the left operand held
throughout `[i, j)`" is a property of time points alone, and the answer at `i` is

  the union of the right operand's tables over `j` in `[i, e(i)]`,

where `e(i)` is the last time point still within the interval, or the first one after which the
left operand fails, whichever comes first. Both bounds move to the right as `i` advances -- the
first because a later `i` has a later timestamp, the second because the first failure of the
left operand can only move forward -- so this is a sliding window whose two ends both advance.

### 16.2 The structure

`Until_Imp2.thy` keeps the union of that window as a count for every tuple and the answer itself
as a set. Adding a table to the window costs one map update per tuple of *that table*; removing
one costs the same; and the answer changes only where a count reaches or leaves zero. Where most
time points contribute nothing -- which is what a bounded-future interval over a coarse timestamp
sequence looks like -- most steps then touch nothing at all. The window is extended one time
point at a time, so over a run each time point enters it once.

Two details cost more than they look:

* `takeWhile_queue` returns the queue it normalised on the way. Dropping that second component
  and shifting the original queue instead re-reverses the whole window at every step: `rev` alone
  was 16.6% of the run, and P2 was *slower* than with the general structure.
* The structure was carrying a time point index nothing read, and the shift built a queue and a
  list only to take their length. Both are per-step allocation on a structure whose point is to
  allocate nothing per step.

### 16.3 Measurements

P2, with heap-resident maps:

| | before | after |
|---|---:|---:|
| instructions | 8250.4M | **5650.4M** (-31.5%) |
| minor words | 469.5M | 344.0M (-26.7%) |
| promoted words | 27.33M | 16.16M (-40.9%) |
| wall clock | 1.05s | **0.73s** |

Wall clock against MonPoly over the standard suite, median of seven runs:

| | MonPoly | pure | heap | h/mp before | h/mp after |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| P2 | 0.41s | 1.01s | 0.73s | 2.56x | **1.78x** |
| P3 | 0.44s | 0.89s | 0.80s | 1.84x | 1.82x |
| and | 0.16s | 0.25s | 0.24s | 1.44x | 1.50x |
| andnot | 0.16s | 0.16s | 0.16s | 1.07x | **1.00x** |
| once | 0.72s | 0.90s | 0.72s | 1.01x | **1.00x** |
| since | 0.25s | 0.32s | 0.33s | 1.33x | 1.32x |
| eventually | 0.47s | 0.71s | 0.48s | 1.51x | **1.02x** |
| until | 0.23s | 0.38s | 0.38s | 1.65x | 1.65x |
| agg | 0.17s | 0.28s | 0.29s | 1.75x | 1.71x |
| complex | 0.85s | 1.78s | 1.47s | 1.80x | 1.73x |

Decomposing P2 again: the Eventually now costs VeriMon 0.48s against MonPoly's 0.19s, where
before this structure it cost 0.80s against 0.17s. `until`, whose left operand has free
variables, is untouched at 1.65x -- that is the general case, and it is doing the work the
general structure exists for.

### 16.4 Where it lives, and why

The structure is selected when the left operand is closed (`args_L args = {}`), the interval
starts at zero so that every time point of the window contributes, and there is no aggregation;
the general structure is used otherwise. It lives in `MFODL_Monitor_Imp`, the unproved layer, and
is therefore reached only with `-heap_maps`.

It cannot simply be moved into the pure kernel. `muaux_dq` in
`MFODL_Monitor_Devel.Optimized_Until_Deque` is not just a datatype: `valid_muaux_dq` is a
correctness invariant that the base session's proofs about `meeval` depend on, and a third
constructor would need a validity argument for the new structure -- that is, its correctness
proof -- before `valid_init_muaux_dq` goes through again. The separate session exists exactly so
that a refinement can be written and measured before it is proved. This one is now measured;
proving it is what would let the default path have it.

### 16.5 Re-measuring the imperative Until

Section 16 leans on a claim recorded earlier in this document and in
`MFODL_Monitor_Imp/Until_Imp2.thy`: that the heap-resident Until structure is sixty times slower
than the pure deque. Sixty times is the signature of something quadratic rather than of a
constant factor, so it was worth re-measuring rather than repeating. It was also worth
re-measuring because the same session had just produced a bug of exactly that shape -- a
`shift_evt` that discarded the queue `takeWhile_queue` had normalised, re-reversing the whole
window at every step, which made the specialised structure *slower* than the general one until
one line changed.

The measurement, on prefixes of the P2 log, evaluating
`trans(c,t,a) AND NOT EVENTUALLY[0,6) report(t)` (exact instruction counts):

| time points | pure deque | specialised | imperative |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 4,000 | 84.1M | 76.5M | 122.8M (1.5x) |
| 8,000 | 168.7M | 152.0M | 245.3M (1.5x) |
| 11,000 | 208.0M | -- | 339.9M (1.6x) |
| 12,000 | 271.5M | -- | 4312.4M (16x) |
| 16,000 | 457.4M | 401.5M | 18082.5M (45x) |

**The claim is right and the usual reading of it is wrong.** Below about eleven thousand time
points the imperative structure costs 1.5x the pure deque -- an ordinary hash-map-against-tree
constant. Between 11,000 and 12,000 it jumps by a factor of 12.7 for a 9% larger input. P2's log
carries about 1900 time points per timestamp, so an interval of six time units spans some 11,400
of them: the jump is exactly where the window fills.

Call counts at 16,000 time points name the operation:

| | calls | per `eval_step_dq2` |
|---|---:|---:|
| `eval_step_dq2` | 5,629 | -- |
| `im_merge_idx` | 7,711,229 | 1,370 |
| `im_update_h` | 5,389,627 | 957 |
| `im_fill` (rehash) | 7,653,245 | 1,360 |
| `im_keys_idx` | 95,693 | **17** |

`im_keys_tbl`, which builds a key set from a bucket array and was the obvious suspect on reading
the code, is entered seventeen times per step and is not the problem. `im_merge` is: 957 entries
re-inserted and 1,370 buckets walked per step, once entries merged forward for the length of the
window have accumulated in one mapping.

The asymmetry is specific and worth stating precisely, because "hash tables are slower here" is
not it. `Mapping.combine` on a red-black tree goes through `rbt_join` and splices whole subtrees,
leaving untouched entries alone -- the `bh_le`/`small_rbt` machinery of Section 15.8. A hash map
has no subtree to splice and must read and re-insert every entry of one side. **A merge is the
one operation a hash map cannot do by sharing, and this algorithm merges a window-sized map at
every step.**

One genuine defect turned up alongside, and it does not change the verdict: each step creates the
destination map with `im_new 16` and then merges about 957 entries into it, so it is rehashed
some six times per step -- 1,360 `im_fill` calls. Sizing it to what is about to go in would be
worth perhaps a factor of two or three, against a factor of forty-five.

The useful form of the lesson, which is narrower than the one this document carried before: **an
imperative map wins where entries are written once and read often, and loses where the algorithm
repeatedly merges maps that a persistent structure could share.** That is why the heap-resident
Since structure pays and this one does not, and why the way out for this operator was not a
better map but a better algorithm -- Section 16.1, where a closed left operand removes the
per-time-point mapping altogether.

## 17. The join

Section 16 left the join as the worst single ratio measured anywhere: on
`A(x,y) AND (ONCE[0,20] B(y,z))` over the dense log, evaluating the `ONCE` alone costs MonPoly
0.70s and VeriMon 0.62s, and adding the join takes MonPoly to 0.79s and VeriMon to 1.31s. The
join itself is 0.08s against 0.68s.

Five changes, none of them to the join algorithm. Every one is something the *encoding* was
doing that the join did not ask for.

### 17.1 Hashing a tuple with the type in hand

The index hashed its key with OCaml's polymorphic `Hashtbl.hash`, which walks an unknown
structure with a work queue and a node budget: **378 instructions per call over 2.4 million
calls**. The structure is not unknown -- it is a list of optional event data -- and walking it
directly, with `Z.hash` for the integers, is the same job done with the type known. Only
determinism is required of a hash here, since a bucket entry is confirmed by `teq`.

5168M instructions to 4377M (-15.3%).

### 17.2 Comparing with the type in hand, and not building the projection

`teq` was `x = y`, which is OCaml's polymorphic comparison: 158M instructions. And every probe
built the projection of its tuple, hashed it, compared it once and discarded it -- two million
tuples allocated per run. `teq` is now a structural equality, and `ihash_proj`/`teq_proj` hash
and compare against the mask directly, agreeing with `proj_tuple` clause by clause. The
projection is still built for the hundred thousand tuples that enter the index, where it is the
stored key.

4377M to 4001M.

### 17.3 The dictionary that was rebuilt on every probe

The largest allocation site in the join was not in the join:

```
im_lookup_proj (heap_list (heap_list (heap_option heap_event_data))) q msk x
```

The operations on a heap map take the value type's `heap` dictionary, and the code generator
builds it at the call site. The nested type representations of a list and of an option were
28.5M and 14.4M words, and with the monadic closures around them the dictionary was **42% of
everything the join allocated**. The index's value type is closed, so it is boxed and built once.

4001M to 3774M.

### 17.4 One closure per bind

What remained was the monad itself. Imperative HOL prints `bind` as

```
(fun f_ () -> f_ (m ()) ()) f
```

a two-argument lambda applied to one argument, which OCaml compiles as a partial application:
two closures for every bind, **104M words on this join, a third of everything it allocated**.
The direct form `(fun () -> f (m ()) ())` leaves one. Two things had to be worked around:

* a printing's placeholders are filled in argument order, and the function has to appear before
  the action, so the flipped `bind_flip` carries the printing and a code equation redirects
  `bind` to it;
* the declaration has to sit in the theory that exports the code. Put next to the definition it
  has no effect: that theory's branch is merged with the one carrying Imperative HOL's own
  printing, and the merge keeps the original.

This one is not specific to the join -- it is every bind in the imperative layer.

3774M to 3493M, and the formula's minor allocation from 472M words to 390M.

### 17.5 Where the join is now

| | instructions | vs start |
|---|---:|---:|
| start of Section 17 | 5168M | -- |
| specialised hash | 4377M | -15.3% |
| specialised equality, no projection | 4001M | -22.6% |
| boxed heap dictionary | 3774M | -27.0% |
| one closure per bind | **3493M** | **-32.4%** |

Wall clock against MonPoly, median of seven runs, with heap-resident maps:

| | MonPoly | pure | heap | before Section 17 | now |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| P2 | 0.41s | 0.98s | 0.70s | 1.76x | **1.71x** |
| P3 | 0.45s | 0.89s | 0.65s | 1.55x | **1.44x** |
| and | 0.16s | 0.25s | 0.22s | 1.38x | 1.38x |
| andnot | 0.16s | 0.16s | 0.16s | 1.00x | **1.00x** |
| once | 0.72s | 0.89s | 0.63s | 0.89x | **0.88x** |
| since | 0.24s | 0.31s | 0.31s | 1.29x | 1.29x |
| eventually | 0.48s | 0.69s | 0.47s | 1.02x | **0.98x** |
| until | 0.22s | 0.37s | 0.37s | 1.68x | 1.68x |
| agg | 0.16s | 0.28s | 0.29s | 1.81x | 1.81x |
| complex | 0.83s | 1.76s | 1.28s | 1.65x | **1.54x** |

Three benchmarks are now at or better than MonPoly. **What the join needed was not a better join.**
Of the 1675M instructions removed, none came from changing how tuples are matched: they came from
a polymorphic hash, a polymorphic equality, a projection built to be thrown away, a type-class
dictionary rebuilt per probe, and a monad encoding that allocated twice where once would do.

## 18. The Until, and the things standing next to it

### 18.1 What the operator can be asked to do

`A(x,y) UNTIL[0,20] B(x,y)` was 1.68x MonPoly, the worst of the ten benchmarks, and the obvious
first question is whether it has an algorithmic lever like the one Section 16 found for
`EVENTUALLY`. It does not. Section 16 works because a closed left operand makes the answer the
same for every tuple, so the whole per-time-point mapping collapses into a sliding-window union
with counts. Here the left operand is `A(x,y)` with the same free variables as the right, so
whether a tuple is still alive genuinely differs per tuple, which is exactly what `a1_map` is
for. There is no structure to remove.

So the question becomes what the operator spends its instructions on that is not the algorithm.
The answer, from a profile of the 1779M instructions the operator itself accounted for, was that
**36% of them were tuple comparison** -- and not because tuples are compared too often, but
because each comparison went the long way round.

### 18.2 One call into the arbitrary-precision library instead of two

Containers derives the order on `event_data` from `comparator_of`, which is `if x < y then Lt
else if x = y then Eq else Gt`: for the `EInt` case, two separate calls out of OCaml into zarith
where the C function already returns the sign. `cmp_integer` makes the one call and reads it.

On its own this was worth only 3.0% of the operator, and the profile said why: `ed_comparator`
went from 559 thousand calls to 2.19 million, but the generic `comparator_list` chain was still
entered 3.84 million times. The comparison was cheaper; it was still the wrong comparison.

### 18.3 The dictionary built to be ignored

Section 15.11 monomorphised `Set.member` and `Mapping.lookup` at the tuple type, so that the
comparator is resolved at code-generation time. The profile showed `ccompare_lista` and
`ccompare_optiona` still entered 600,513 times each -- exactly once per lookup. The monomorphic
`ed_rbt_lookup_nat` builds the dictionary for no other reason than to hand it to
`RBT_Mapping2.impl_of`, which projects a tree out of its wrapper and never looks at it.

Boxing `impl_of` at each of the five value types makes the projection a nullary constant. The
dictionary builds halved, 15.6M instructions to 7.85M.

The write side went the same way: `update`, `delete` and `combine`, and the tree primitives
`insert`, `delete` and `join` underneath them, monomorphised at all five value types. Together
with the boxing this was worth 3.0% of P2 -- and only 0.7% of the Until benchmark, because the
Until's remaining chain comparisons turned out not to come from the write side at all (18.6).

### 18.4 The batch table in the adapter

The tuples of a time point are collected in a table keyed by the predicate's name and arity,
and the arity was an arbitrary-precision natural. A generic `Hashtbl` hashes such a key
structurally and then runs the polymorphic comparison down the bucket chain, so every event paid
a `caml_hash` over a string and a custom block and about three `do_compare_val`s. That was
**5.5% of the run in `do_compare_val` and a further 1.6% in `caml_hash`**, none of it doing any
work the monitor cares about.

Hashing the name alone, comparing the two components directly, and keeping the arity a machine
integer until the flush removes all of it. Events also arrive grouped by predicate, so
remembering the list the previous event went into consults the table once per predicate per time
point rather than once per event; the cache is keyed on the signature's own type list, which
physical equality identifies, except for nullary predicates whose list is the immediate `[]`,
hence a name check as well.

**Until: 3002M to 2840M instructions, -5.4%.** This is the largest single step in Section 18, and
it is not in the verified kernel at all.

### 18.5 Reading zarith's representation

With the batch table gone, `ml_z_compare` was the costliest single symbol of the benchmark at
5.7%, at about fourteen instructions a call for what is usually a comparison of two machine
integers. Zarith keeps every integer that fits in an OCaml integer as an immediate and only
larger ones as a custom block, so two immediates are in the same order as the machine integers
they carry and the two cases are told apart by a tag test. `cmp_integer` now takes that path in
OCaml and falls back to the library for everything else.

**2840M to 2764M, -2.7%.** This reads the representation the library documents rather than its
interface, so it is one more thing to believe about the generated code, alongside the hash
functions of the imperative tables; the theory text says so where the printing is declared.

### 18.6 A nursery three times the default

With the comparison work cut down, the remaining P2 profile is about a third garbage collector
and allocator: marking, promotion, the write barrier, sweeping, compaction. Most of what the
kernel allocates dies before the next time point, and the default 256k-word nursery promotes a
lot of it to the major heap first, so a larger nursery should help.

Instruction counts agree enthusiastically: 4M words removes 13.7% of P2 and 9.9% of the Until
benchmark. **Wall clock says the opposite.** A nursery that does not fit in cache costs more in
misses than it saves in collections, and at 4M words P2 runs a third slower than at the default.

| words | 256k | 512k | 768k | 1M | 2M |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| P2 | 0.68s | 0.68s | **0.67s** | 0.69s | 0.74s |
| P3 | 0.63s | 0.63s | **0.63s** | 0.66s | 0.70s |
| until | 0.34s | 0.34s | **0.32s** | 0.33s | 0.33s |
| complex | 1.16s | 1.18s | **1.05s** | 1.05s | 1.06s |
| once | 0.57s | 0.53s | **0.53s** | 0.52s | 0.57s |
| since | 0.29s | 0.29s | **0.29s** | 0.29s | 0.33s |

768k words: six megabytes, no benchmark slower, the allocation-heavy ones 5-9% faster. This is
the sharpest instance so far of the warning in Section 15.12, and the reason it is worth
restating: **a change that only moves the collector must be judged on wall clock, because the
instruction count is measuring the thing the change is trading away.**

MonPoly's own run gains 5.9% from the same setting; it is not enabled for MonPoly, on the same
grounds as the space overhead of Section 9 -- the flag exists for either monitor, and the
verified kernel is the one whose allocation rate needs it.

### 18.7 Membership, one level up

After all of 18.2 and 18.3 the generic `comparator_list` chain was still entered 3.84 million
times on the Until benchmark, and the write side was not where it came from. `Set.member` is
monomorphised, but its red-black-tree case calls `RBT_Set2.member`, which was not, so the
dictionary was built and the composite comparator reached after all -- once per membership test,
on the one operation the Until does most. The same held for the nested update of the Until
deque, whose keys are tuples and whose values are a time-stamp or a time-point.

Two more entries in the monomorphisation list. `ed_member`'s tree case became
`ed_rbt_member rbt x`, with no dictionary and no chain.

| | pure | heap |
|---|---:|---:|
| until | 2570M | 2551M (-7.1%) |
| complex | 9646M | 7101M (-16.4%) |
| agg | 2078M | 2099M (-4.7%) |
| P2 | 7137M | 5331M (-4.0%) |

### 18.8 Where Section 18 leaves the monitor

Both paths, because they are two different monitors: `pure` is the verified kernel as exported,
`heap` is the same kernel with the unproved imperative layer of Sections 12 to 17 switched in.

Exact instruction counts:

| formula | MonPoly | pure | heap | p/mp | h/mp |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| P2 | 3467M | 7137M | 5331M | 2.06x | 1.54x |
| P3 | 3309M | 5537M | 4539M | 1.67x | 1.37x |
| and | 1436M | 1865M | 1721M | 1.30x | 1.20x |
| andnot | 1468M | 1267M | 1267M | **0.86x** | **0.86x** |
| once | 7096M | 5184M | 3858M | **0.73x** | **0.54x** |
| since | 2192M | 2360M | 2327M | 1.08x | 1.06x |
| eventually | 4585M | 4042M | 2912M | **0.88x** | **0.64x** |
| until | 2053M | 2570M | 2551M | 1.25x | 1.24x |
| agg | 1465M | 2078M | 2099M | 1.42x | 1.43x |
| complex | 6876M | 9646M | 7101M | 1.40x | 1.03x |

Wall clock, median of seven:

| formula | MonPoly | pure | heap | p/mp | h/mp | before Section 18 (heap) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| P2 | 0.42s | 0.96s | 0.68s | 2.29x | **1.62x** | 1.71x |
| P3 | 0.44s | 0.84s | 0.67s | 1.91x | 1.52x | 1.44x |
| and | 0.16s | 0.22s | 0.19s | 1.38x | **1.19x** | 1.38x |
| andnot | 0.16s | 0.14s | 0.14s | **0.88x** | **0.88x** | 1.00x |
| once | 0.74s | 0.77s | 0.54s | 1.04x | **0.73x** | 0.88x |
| since | 0.24s | 0.30s | 0.30s | 1.25x | 1.25x | 1.29x |
| eventually | 0.47s | 0.58s | 0.40s | 1.23x | **0.85x** | 0.98x |
| until | 0.23s | 0.32s | 0.32s | 1.39x | **1.39x** | 1.68x |
| agg | 0.16s | 0.25s | 0.26s | 1.56x | 1.62x | 1.81x |
| complex | 0.84s | 1.46s | 1.08s | 1.74x | **1.29x** | 1.54x |

The Until went from 1.68x to 1.39x without one instruction of its algorithm changing. P3 reads
1.52x against 1.44x before, but both monitors moved a few percent on it between the two runs, so
it is better read as unchanged than as a regression.

Three observations the two columns make that one would not:

**The imperative layer does nothing for the Until or the Since.** `until` is 1.39x either way and
`since` 1.25x either way, because `use_heap_until` is off (Section 16.5: the algorithm merges a
window-sized map at every step, which is the one thing a hash map cannot do by sharing) and
because the specialised Since structure of Section 16 already removed what the heap maps were
there to help with. Where the layer does pay, it pays large: `once` 1.04x to 0.73x, `eventually`
1.23x to 0.85x, `complex` 1.74x to 1.29x.

**Instruction counts and wall clock disagree about which benchmarks are won.** By instructions
the verified kernel beats MonPoly on four of ten; by wall clock on one, and `once` at 0.54x in
instructions is 0.73x in time. The gap is allocation: the counts do not see the cache, and the
kernel's persistent structures touch far more memory per instruction than MonPoly's mutable ones.
Section 18.6 is the same lesson in miniature.

**Where the remaining gap is.** For P2, the largest at 1.62x, the profile is now about a third
garbage collector and allocator and about a tenth lexer -- the latter shared with MonPoly, which
runs the same parser. Comparison, dictionaries and the generic application path, which is what
Sections 15, 17 and 18 have been about, are down to a few percent. **The next thing to attack is
not a comparison but an allocation**, and the largest single candidate is that `nat` is a boxed
constructor around an arbitrary-precision integer, so every subtraction on a time point allocates
twice where MonPoly's machine integer allocates not at all.

## 19. Tuples without their empty columns

### 19.1 What the holes are

A table of the monitor is a set of tuples all of one width, the number of free variables of the
whole formula, and `wf_tuple n V x` says that `x` carries `None` at exactly the positions outside
`V`, the free variables of the subformula that produced the table. Those positions are therefore
the same for every tuple of a table and are known from the formula alone -- which is what makes
deleting them possible, and what makes the column list have to live in the formula.

Deleting a fixed set of positions from every tuple of a table does not change their order. So
everything that only compares or collects tuples is untouched by the change, and in particular
the Since and Until structures of Sections 16 and 17 need no change at all: they read
`args_ivl` and `args_pos` and nothing else. What does need the column list is what reads a tuple
by position -- the join, the projection, the predicate and the constraints.

Instrumenting the comparator said the holes were worth removing: of the positions it walks, 65%
are holes on P2, 64% on P3, 45% on the complex formula, 50% on the aggregation. It also confirmed
the invariant the whole idea rests on, which is worth having as a measurement and not only as a
lemma: **in 45 million comparisons, not once did a comparison meet a hole on one side and a value
on the other.**

### 19.2 The theory

(Section 20 splits this theory: the pure part, with its agreement theorems, is now
`thys/MFODL_Monitor_Imp/Dense.thy`; what stays in `Dense_Imp.thy` is the heap index, the
compilation and the evaluation function.)

`thys/MFODL_Monitor_Imp/Dense_Imp.thy`, a new pure theory in the child session, so the base
session is not rebuilt. It carries the compacting projection and its inverse, the masks, the
merge plan of two column lists, the dense join of two tuples, a binary join with three shapes
(the right operand's columns inside the left's, which is the anti-join and the intersection; the
left's inside the right's; and otherwise an index on the shared columns), and a formula with the
columns at every node.

A constructor the theory does not handle yet is wrapped in `DPure`, which evaluates it with the
existing function and deletes the holes from its result. The two representations therefore mix,
and the fragment can grow one constructor at a time; today it is everything except aggregation,
the multi-way conjunction, the regex operators, `LET`, and the Since and Until outside the
closed-left-operand case. At the root the columns are all of them, so the verdicts have the width
the printer expects and nothing is converted there.

Two cases fall out that are not merely narrower tuples:

- A predicate whose arguments are distinct variables in increasing order needs **no conversion at
  all** -- the database table already is the dense table, where today `simple_match` rebuilds
  every event as a width-`n` tuple.
- An existential quantifier over a variable the body does not mention becomes a **relabelling**
  instead of a rebuild of the whole table.

Behind `-dense`; 678 differential invocations agree with the verified kernel, cram suite green.

### 19.3 A first measurement that measured nothing

The first reading of `-dense` against the plain kernel said 24% on P2 and 17.5% on the complex
formula. Both were wrong, in two different ways, and the way they were wrong is worth recording.

The 24% was an attribution error: `-dense` reaches its Since and Until through `init_j`, so it
also picks up the Section 16 structures, which on the pure path one otherwise only gets with
`-heap_maps`. Comparing against `-heap_maps` instead of against the plain kernel removes it.

The 17.5% was worse. **Nothing in those runs was dense at all.** `bin_join` is almost never
reached from a realistic formula: a conjunction of more than two conjuncts -- and in practice
nearly every conjunction -- is compiled to `MAnds` and evaluated by `mmulti_join`, as
`Join_Imp2` records. `MAnds` was not in the fragment, so every one of these formulas was a
single `DPure` node wrapping the whole formula, and `DPure` runs the old evaluation function
*with the heap index still on by default*. The 17.5% was the imperative join, seen through a
wrapper that did nothing.

Turning the index off on both sides gives the honest figure for the representation as it stood:

| | sparse | dense | |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| P2 | 5344M | 5375M | +0.6% |
| P3 | 4540M | 4573M | +0.7% |
| complex | 8593M | 8610M | +0.2% |
| and | 1951M | 1948M | -0.2% |
| andnot | 1267M | 1268M | +0.1% |

Nothing, plus the cost of a wrapper. The lesson is cheap to state and was expensive to miss: a
representation change behind a fallback needs a counter for how often the fallback fires. There
is one now, `dpure_nodes`, behind `MONPOLY_DENSE_STATS`; it reports 0 for every benchmark here
except the aggregation, which reports 1.

### 19.4 The multi-way conjunction, and the index over short tuples

`MAnds` joins its positive conjuncts left-deep and subtracts the negative ones, and the columns
of every intermediate result are known at build time, so all the join plans are made once. With
that case in place the short tuples finally reach a join.

The heap index of Section 17 is then also worth having over them, which is `-dense -heap_maps`.
Two of the sparse version's worries go away. Its guard requires both operands to be complete on
the common columns, because a hole there means "any value" and a hash index would miss matches;
**a short tuple has no holes by construction, so the guard is vacuous** and the index applies
wherever the shape is right. And the key is the projection onto the shared columns, which here
is as short as those columns rather than as wide as the formula, so it is cheaper to build and
to hash.

### 19.5 Fixing the pure join

The first version of the dense join was worse than the kernel's own pure join, and for two
reasons that had nothing to do with short tuples.

It always indexed the right operand. `bin_join` indexes **the smaller side** and probes with
the larger -- indexing costs a lookup per tuple of the indexed side and probing one per tuple
of the other, so indexing the larger side buys a big index that is read a few times. On the
complex formula the right operand is the sliding window of an `ONCE`, some thousand tuples,
against a fifty-tuple predicate: exactly the wrong way round, and exactly the failure Section
17 documents for the heap index.

And it had no case for equal columns. `bin_join` answers that one with a set intersection or
difference, which on two red-black trees over one order is a merge that shares structure; the
dense join was doing a lookup per tuple instead. A right operand with no columns at all is the
other case worth keeping apart -- one membership test decides the whole join.

With the smaller side indexed and both special cases in, the complex formula goes from 2.07x to
**1.21x**, which is better than the sparse monitor with its heap index (1.25x).

### 19.6 The aggregation

`eval_agg` groups its operand by `drop (length tys)` of each tuple -- the aggregation's bound
variables occupy the bottom of the frame, so the group key is the rest -- and writes the
aggregate into position `y` of the key. Over short tuples it is the same two steps for a reason
worth stating: `{0..<length tys}` is contained in the operand's free variables, so those columns
are the first `length tys` of every short tuple and **the group key is again a suffix**. The
aggregate goes at the one position `y` occupies among the group's columns. The clustering and
the value counting are the kernel's, with the term read at the short tuple's positions.

That removes the last fallback: every benchmark here now reports zero subformulas evaluated by
the old function. It does nothing for the `agg` benchmark, and the reason is the one from 19.3
again -- `s <- SUM y; x A(x,y)` has no empty columns anywhere, so there is nothing to drop.
`B(u,v) AND (s <- SUM y; x A(x,y))`, where the aggregation sits inside a wider formula and its
operand uses two of five columns, is the case that shows it.

### 19.7 Five configurations, by the clock

Median of nine, all outputs identical; `dense+h` is `-dense -heap_maps`. Benchmarks under a
third of a second -- `and`, `andnot`, `since`, `agg`, `until` -- moved by up to a tenth between
runs and should not be read closely.

| formula | MonPoly | pure | dense | **dense+h** | heap |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| P2 | 0.44s | 2.23x | 1.55x | **1.52x** | 1.61x |
| P3 | 0.47s | 1.87x | 1.34x | **1.30x** | 1.40x |
| and | 0.17s | 1.41x | 1.12x | **0.94x** | 1.29x |
| andnot | 0.17s | 0.94x | 0.94x | 0.94x | 0.94x |
| once | 0.76s | 1.03x | 0.75x | 0.72x | **0.71x** |
| since | 0.26s | 1.23x | 1.19x | 1.19x | **1.15x** |
| eventually | 0.50s | 1.20x | **0.84x** | **0.84x** | 0.88x |
| until | 0.24s | **1.42x** | 1.50x | 1.50x | **1.42x** |
| agg | 0.18s | 1.50x | 1.50x | 1.50x | 1.56x |
| complex | 0.89s | 1.72x | 1.21x | **1.03x** | 1.25x |
| aggwide | 3.69s | 0.99x | 0.76x | 0.78x | 0.88x |

Short tuples are now the best or joint-best configuration on nine of eleven, and the two they
lose are `until` and `since`, by a hundredth of a second each on benchmarks that move by more
than that between runs.

**The two ideas are worth different things and both are worth having.** Where the join shape
admits an index -- which is where neither operand's columns contain the other's, and among these
benchmarks that is `complex` and `and` -- the heap index is still worth 15 to 20% on top of the
short tuples. Everywhere else it buys nothing, and the short tuples buy between 10% and 25%
against the plain kernel, with no heap at all: pure Since and Until structures, pure joins,
red-black trees.

Four of eleven benchmarks are now at or faster than MonPoly, and the two worst -- P2 at 1.52x
and `agg` at 1.50x -- are worst for reasons the representation cannot reach. P2 is seven eighths
a three-column predicate with no holes, and its profile is a third garbage collector and a tenth
lexer. `agg` likewise has no holes; at 0.18s against 226,000 events it is mostly parsing.

### 19.8 What is left

A short tuple is still an `event_data option list`, so every value carries a `Some` box: two
words per value that the representation cannot drop without changing the type of every table and
so of every auxiliary structure. Underneath that, `nat` is a boxed constructor around an
arbitrary-precision integer, which Section 18.8 already named as the largest remaining
allocation. Neither is a change to an algorithm; both are changes to what a value costs.

## 20. Verifying the short-tuple representation

Section 19 built a second evaluator over short tuples and tested it differentially against the
kernel. That is evidence, not a proof. The goal of this section is a refinement theorem: that the
dense evaluator simulates the kernel's `meval`, so that running the dense monitor is running the
verified one.

*Work in progress. 20.1-20.10 are done -- the simulation covers every node of the dense fragment
and the kernel's correctness theorems transfer (20.9); 20.11 is what is left, chiefly the rest of
`produces` and the compilation.*

### 20.1 The pair of morphisms

The relation between the two representations is a pair of inverse maps. For a column list `cs` --
strictly sorted, all entries below the frame width `n` -- narrowing deletes the empty columns and
widening puts them back:

```isabelle
definition dtabc     :: "nat ⇒ cols ⇒ 'a table ⇒ 'a table" where "dtabc n cs T = dproj  (wide_mask n cs) ` T"
definition dtabw     :: "nat ⇒ cols ⇒ 'a table ⇒ 'a table" where "dtabw n cs D = dwiden (wide_mask n cs) ` D"
definition dwf_tuple :: "nat ⇒ 'a tuple ⇒ bool"           where "dwf_tuple k d ⟷ length d = k ∧ (∀i<k. d ! i ≠ None)"
definition dtable    :: "cols ⇒ 'a table ⇒ bool"          where "dtable cs D ⟷ (∀d ∈ D. dwf_tuple (length cs) d)"
```

`wide_mask n cs` is what turns "a number and a list of positions" into the mask the two maps take.
`dwf_tuple` is the point of the whole exercise: a short tuple has *no* holes, which is what makes
the pair a bijection rather than merely a surjection, and it is proved as such --

```isabelle
lemma dtable_dtabc : "sorted_wrt (<) cs ⟹ ∀c∈set cs. c<n ⟹ table n (set cs) T ⟹ dtable cs (dtabc n cs T)"
lemma table_dtabw  : "sorted_wrt (<) cs ⟹ ∀c∈set cs. c<n ⟹ dtable cs D ⟹ table n (set cs) (dtabw n cs D)"
lemma dtabw_dtabc  : "sorted_wrt (<) cs ⟹ table n (set cs) T ⟹ dtabw n cs (dtabc n cs T) = T"
lemma dtabc_dtabw  : "sorted_wrt (<) cs ⟹ ∀c∈set cs. c<n ⟹ dtable cs D ⟹ dtabc n cs (dtabw n cs D) = D"
```

-- so that `dtabc n cs` is a bijection from `{T. table n (set cs) T}` onto `{D. dtable cs D}` with
inverse `dtabw n cs`. `wide_mask_eq_join_mask : sorted_wrt (<) cs ⟹ wide_mask n cs = join_mask n (set cs)`
is the bridge that lets every proof below hand a goal back to a kernel lemma about `join_mask`.

Everything else in this section is one statement per operation, all of the same shape:

> *dense operation applied to narrowed arguments = narrowing of the kernel's operation applied to
> the wide arguments*

with the column list transforming alongside the table -- `merge_cols cs1 cs2` for a join,
`merge_cols gcols [y]` for an aggregation, `ex_cols ccs` for the existential. That is what makes
the equations compose: the right-hand side of each is a `dtabc` of a kernel result, ready to be
the left-hand side's argument at the next step.

### 20.2 The table operations (done)

`thys/MFODL_Monitor_Imp/Dense.thy`, 2839 lines and 180 lemmas and theorems, no `sorry`. It sits in
