the child session for now, so that changing it costs a two-minute build rather than a rebuild of
the base session; it moves to the base session once the whole refinement is in place.

| kernel operation | dense operation | theorem |
|---|---|---|
| `join T pos T'`, equal columns | `A ∩ B` / `A - B` | `dtabc_join_eq` |
| `join T pos T'`, right operand closed | membership test | `dtabc_join_no_cols` |
| `join T pos T'`, right columns ⊆ left | `dsemi` | `dsemi_join` |
| `join T True T'`, left columns ⊆ right | filter on a projection | `dsup_join` |
| `join T True T'`, general | `dgen_join` (index) | `dtabc_gen_join` |
| `join T pos T'`, one frame | `join` at the narrow frame | `dtabc_join_frame` |
| `bin_join n A T pos A' T'` | `dbin_join (mk_djoin cs1 cs2)` | `dbin_join_bin_join` |
| `mmulti_join' A_pos A_neg L` | `dands_neg ∘ dands_pos` | `dmulti_join_dtabc` |
| `eval_agg n g0 y ω tys f` | `dagg` | `dagg_eval_agg` |
| `simple_match n 0 tms \` X` (Copy, Simple) | the database table itself | `dtabc_simple_match` |
| `(λf. tabulate f 0 n) \` these (match tms \` X)` | `dmatch_table` | `dmatch_table_dtabc` |
| `tl \` X` (the existential) | `tl` or nothing | `dtabc_exists` |
| narrowing at the boundary | `dnarrow` | `dnarrow_dtabc` |

Three are worth a word.

**The general join.** `dgen_join` indexes one side by the shared columns and probes it with the
other, choosing which side by `card A ≤ card B`. Two things had to be proved that the sparse
kernel never needs. First, that a probe that hits can merge without a test: `djoin1_dmerge` says
two short tuples join exactly when they agree on the shared columns, and that the result is then
`dmerge`, which walks the plan and copies. Second, that the size heuristic is transparent:
`dgen_join_eq` computes both branches of `card A ≤ card B` and shows they are the same set, so the
choice cannot be observed. Underneath, `dindex_lookup` says the fold that builds the index maps a
key to exactly the tuples with that key, and `dprobe_fold` / `dprobe_l_fold` say the probe folds
accumulate the merges.

**The multi-way join.** The kernel's `mmulti_join'` picks a join order at run time from the
variable sets; the dense evaluator folds binary joins left to right against a plan list computed
once when the formula is compiled. Proving these equal did not need a new argument about the
generic join: the kernel already carries `mmulti_join'_opt_True` and `mmulti_join'_opt_False`,
which merge two adjacent positive operands into their join and discharge a negative operand by an
anti-join. Iterating them (`mmulti_join'_fold_pos`, `mmulti_join'_fold_neg`) folds the whole thing
down to the one-operand case `mmulti_join'_single`, which is the left-to-right fold. The dense
half (`dands_pos_dtabc`, `dands_neg_dtabc`) is then a straight induction on `dbin_join_bin_join`.

**The aggregation.** The kernel groups by `drop (length tys)` of the wide tuple and writes the
aggregate at position `y`. Over short tuples the group key is still a suffix -- `drop_dproj_agg`,
which needs the operand's columns to be `[0..<m]` followed by the group's columns shifted up by
`m`, which is exactly what the aggregation's frame gives -- but the aggregate goes at the one
position `y` occupies among the group's columns (`dproj_list_update`, `ins_pos`). The values
aggregated do not change (`agg_values_dtabc`): the re-indexed term reads the short tuple where the
original reads the wide one (`meval_trm_reindex`), and the multiset of values is the same because
narrowing is injective on a group (`ecard_image`).

The proofs found no defect. That is worth recording next to Section 19.5, where testing found two
in the same code: the two bugs -- indexing the wrong side, and a missing equal-columns case -- were
found by measurement before the proof was attempted, and the proof would have found both. The
general-join theorem `dtabc_gen_join` is exactly the statement the first bug violated, and
`dbin_join0_join`'s case analysis is exactly the case the second was missing.

### 20.3 The Since and Until need no new structure (done)

Section 19 left the temporal operators alone, and Section 20.3 of the first draft of this section
named them as the biggest obstacle: `DSince` and `DUntil` fire only when
`sevt_applies args ⟷ args_agg args = None ∧ args_L args = {}` -- that is, only for `ONCE` and
`EVENTUALLY` -- and they use the `sevtaux` / `evtaux` structures of Section 16, which have no
correctness proof of their own.

Reading the `msaux` and `muaux` locales dissolved the obstacle. Their entire specification is
stated over an abstract auxiliary list of timestamped tables, and everything the operations do to
that list is a join, a union, or a filter on timestamps:

```isabelle
valid_join_msaux:          table (args_n args) (args_L args) rel1 ⟹ … (map (λ(t,rel). (t, join rel (args_pos args) rel1)) auxlist)
valid_add_new_table_msaux: table (args_n args) (args_R args) rel2 ⟹ … (… y ∪ rel2 …)
valid_result_msaux:        result_msaux args aux = eval_args_agg args (foldr (∪) [rel. (t,rel) ← auxlist, memL … ] {})
valid_add_new_muaux:       … (update_until args rel1 rel2 nt auxlist)
valid_eval_muaux:          eval_until (args_ivl args) nt auxlist = (res', auxlist') ⟹ … res = map (eval_args_agg args) res'
```

Nothing there reads a tuple position except the join, and the join commutes with narrowing. So the
base session's **verified** `mmasaux` and `muaux_dq` can be run unchanged at a frame whose width is
the number of columns, on narrowed tables. The re-indexing is

```isabelle
definition cpos  :: "cols ⇒ nat set ⇒ nat set" where "cpos cs L = {i. i < length cs ∧ cs ! i ∈ L}"
definition dargs :: "cols ⇒ cols ⇒ args ⇒ args" where
  "dargs cs_R cs_L args = init_args (args_ivl args) (length cs_R) (cpos cs_R (set cs_L)) {0..<length cs_R} (args_pos args) None"
```

-- the width becomes `length cs_R`, the `R` set becomes *all* of the columns, and the `L` set
becomes the positions of the left operand's columns inside the right's, so that `join_mask` at the
new args is exactly the sub-mask of `cs_L` in `cs_R`. Note that `R` being all of the columns makes
the structure's own `proj_tuple` on the right side the identity: the dense frame is not just
smaller for these structures, it is also simpler.

The one new preservation lemma this needs is the join in a single frame,

```isabelle
lemma dtabc_join_frame:
  assumes "sorted_wrt (<) cs" "∀c ∈ set cs. c < n" "L ⊆ A" "A ⊆ set cs"
      and "table n A T" "table n L T'"
  shows "dtabc n cs (join T pos T') = join (dtabc n cs T) pos (dtabc n cs T')"
```

proved through the kernel's own `join_sub` on both sides, plus `proj_tuple_dproj` (masking commutes
with narrowing) and `dproj_inj_sub` (narrowing is injective on `wf_tuple n L` when `L ⊆ set cs`).
On top of it there is one theorem per locale operation, over `dauxlist` and `dauxlist3`, which
narrow every table of the auxiliary list:

| locale operation | theorem |
|---|---|
| `add_new_ts_msaux` | `dauxlist_filter` |
| `join_msaux` | `dauxlist_join` |
| `add_new_table_msaux` | `dauxlist_add_new_table` |
| `result_msaux` | `dtabc_result` (with `eval_args_agg_None`) |
| `add_new_muaux` | `dauxlist3_update_until` |
| `eval_muaux` | `dauxlist3_eval_until` |

with `dauxlist_wf_filter`, `dauxlist_wf_join`, `dauxlist_wf_add_new_table` and
`dauxlist3_wf_update_until` carrying `table n (set cs)` across each step.

So this is a case where verification made the program **better**, not merely checked: the dense
Since and Until will cover the general case rather than `ONCE` and `EVENTUALLY`, and will use a
verified structure rather than an unverified one.

### 20.4 The buffers (done)

A buffer never looks inside a tuple: it lines up the tables the operands produce and hands matching
pairs to the node's operation. So a buffer of narrowed tables behaves as the narrowing of a buffer
of wide ones. These are map-fusion lemmas for the kernel's *list-level* buffers -- the ones its
verified `meval` uses. The queue representation of `Monitor_Impl` is layered on top of that by the
kernel's own code equations (`mebuf2_take_aux` and friends), so the dense side reuses it unchanged
instead of redoing it.

| buffer operation | theorem | used by |
|---|---|---|
| `mbuf2_add` | `mbuf2_add_map` | `MAnd`, `MOr`, `MUntil` |
| `mbuf2_take` | `mbuf2_take_map` | `MAnd` (with `dbin_join_bin_join`), `MOr` (with `dtabc_Un_img`) |
| `mprev_next` | `mprev_next_map` | `MPrev`, `MNext` |
| `mbuf2t_take` | `mbuf2t_take_rel` | `MUntil` (with `dauxlist3_update_until`) |

`mbuf2t_take_rel` is relational rather than an equation, because the accumulator of the `MUntil`
buffer is not a narrowed table but an auxiliary structure related to the wide one; the step
condition is therefore `R z z' ⟹ R (f a b t z) (g (h a) (k b) t z')`.

`mbufn_take_rel` covers the multi-way conjunction, where each slot of the buffer has its own
column list and so its own narrowing: it relates `mbufn_take f z buf` to
`mbufn_take g z' (map2 map hs buf)` for a list `hs` of per-slot narrowings, under a step condition
that only has to hold for tuples drawn from per-slot sets `Ss ! i`.

### 20.5 The simulation

`thys/MFODL_Monitor_Imp/Dense_Eval.thy`, 1479 lines, no `sorry`. This is the part that turns
20.2-20.4 from a list of algebraic identities into a statement about the evaluator.

**Where the kernel's invariant enters, and only there.** Every theorem of 20.2 wants its operand
tables to be finite and well formed over the node's columns. That is not a property of a single
call but of a formula's whole future, so it is isolated into one coinductive predicate:

```isabelle
coinductive produces :: "nat ⇒ nat set ⇒ (mmasaux, muaux_dq, event_data mmtaux) mformula ⇒ bool"
  where "(⋀j ts db xs φ'. meval j n ts db φ = (xs, φ') ⟹
      list_all (λX. table n A X ∧ finite X) xs ∧ produces n A φ') ⟹ produces n A φ"
```

`produces_tables` and `produces_next` are the only two facts the simulation ever uses about the
kernel's own evaluation, and the kernel's well-formedness invariant is what will discharge
`produces` at compile time (20.6 below). Nothing else in the induction touches the trace, the
progress functions, or `qtable`.

The second -- and last -- thing the simulation assumes about its input is that the database is
well formed:

```isabelle
definition wf_db :: "database ⇒ bool" where
  "wf_db db ⟷ (∀e ar X. Mapping.lookup db (e, ar) = Some X ⟶ list_all (λT. table ar {0..<ar} T) X)"
```

That is what the trace's own databases look like, and it is what the predicate nodes need: they
read the database's table and hand it straight through, so its tuples have to be the short ones
already. A let-bound predicate, whose table is *not* of this shape, is always inside a `DPure`
node, where the kernel's own evaluator handles the lookup -- so the condition is never needed
there. `wf_db` is a hypothesis of the simulation theorem, discharged at the top level by
`wf_envs`.

**The dense formula and its evaluator.** `dformula` mirrors the kernel's `mformula` node by node,
but each node carries what the *short* representation needs rather than what the wide one needs: a
join plan instead of two variable sets, a term already reindexed against the operand's column list,
a mask at the boundary. `DPure φ m msk` is the escape hatch -- it runs the kernel's own evaluator
on a subformula and narrows the result -- so a dense formula need not cover every node of the
kernel's language in order to be usable. `deval` is the corresponding evaluator, `j ts db` only.

**The relation.** `dsim n cs tw φd φ` relates a dense formula to a kernel one at a frame of
width `n` whose live columns are `cs`, with every timestamp stored anywhere inside the node at most
`tw` (20.7). Each rule carries `wf_cols n cs` and the node's own `produces`, so
`dsim_wf_cols` and `dsim_produces` hand out at every node exactly the two side conditions the
20.2 theorems ask for. The buffers are related by

```isabelle
definition dbuf2 :: "nat ⇒ cols ⇒ cols ⇒ event_data mbuf2 ⇒ event_data mbuf2 ⇒ bool" where
  "dbuf2 n cs1 cs2 buf dbuf ⟷
     list_all (ftable n (set cs1)) (fst buf) ∧ list_all (ftable n (set cs2)) (snd buf) ∧
     dbuf = (map (dtabc n cs1) (fst buf), map (dtabc n cs2) (snd buf))"
```

-- the two halves may live at different column lists, as they do at an `MAnd`. `dbuf2_add` and
`dbuf2_take` package `mbuf2_add_map`, `mbuf2_take_map` and the new `mbuf2_take_sub` (a take only
removes a prefix, so whatever stays in the buffer was already there, and so is still well formed).
The multi-way conjunction has its own relation, one column list per slot:

```isabelle
definition dbufn :: "nat ⇒ cols list ⇒ event_data mbufn ⇒ event_data mbufn ⇒ bool" where
  "dbufn n css buf dbuf ⟷ length buf = length css ∧
     (∀i < length css. list_all (ftable n (set (css ! i))) (buf ! i)) ∧
     dbuf = map2 map (map (dtabc n) css) buf"
```

with `dbufn_add` and `dbufn_take` packaging `mbufn_add_map2`, `mbufn_take_rel` and the new
`mbufn_take_sub`.

**The theorem.**

```isabelle
theorem dsim_deval:
  "dsim n cs tw φd φ ⟹ wf_db db ⟹ sorted ts ⟹ (∀t ∈ set ts. tw ≤ t) ⟹
   meval j n ts db φ = (xs, φ') ⟹
   ∃φd'. deval j ts db φd = (map (dtabc n cs) xs, φd') ∧ dsim n cs (dnext tw ts) φd' φ'"
```

One step of the dense evaluator produces the narrowing of what one step of the kernel's produces,
and the relation is preserved, so by coinduction the two agree on the whole stream. It is proved by
induction on `dsim`; each case is one theorem of 20.2 applied pointwise to the operand's output
list, with `produces_next` re-establishing the invariant for the successor:

| node | uses |
|---|---|
| `DPure` | `dnarrow_dtabc` |
| `DRel` | `map_replicate` |
| `DPredS`, Copy mode | `dtabc_upt` (new: a frame with no empty columns, so narrowing is the identity) |
| `DPredS`, Simple mode | `dtabc_simple_match` + `wf_db` for the tuple widths |
| `DPredM`, Match mode | `dmatch_table_dtabc` |
| `DAndRel` | `eval_constraint_reindex` (new, see below) |
| `DNeg` | `dtabc_neg` (new: `dtabc n [] (unit_table n) = unit_table 0`, and `dtabc_empty_iff`) |
| `DExists` | `dtabc_exists` |
| `DOr` | `dtabc_Un_img` + `dbuf2_add` / `dbuf2_take` |
| `DAnd` | `dbin_join_bin_join` + `dbuf2_add` / `dbuf2_take` |
| `DAgg` | `dagg_eval_agg` |
| `DPrev`, `DNext` | `mprev_next_map` + `mprev_next_sub` (new, the leftover-buffer analogue of `mbuf2_take_sub`) |
| `DAnds` | `dmulti_join_eq` (new) + `dbufn_add` / `dbufn_take` |

`eval_constraint_reindex` is the one algebraic law 20.2 was missing. `reindex_constraint` moves
into `Dense.thy` (it was unverified in `Dense_Imp.thy`) and

```isabelle
lemma eval_constraint_reindex:
  assumes "sorted_wrt (<) cs" "∀c ∈ set cs. c < n"
    "Formula.fv_trm t1 ⊆ set cs" "Formula.fv_trm t2 ⊆ set cs" "table n (set cs) T"
  shows "dtabc n cs (Set.filter (eval_constraint (t1, p, c, t2)) T)
       = Set.filter (eval_constraint (reindex_constraint cs (t1, p, c, t2))) (dtabc n cs T)"
```

-- the reindexed terms read the short tuple at the position the column occupies there, which by
`meval_trm_reindex` is the same value, and filtering commutes with an image without needing any
injectivity.

`dmulti_join_eq` is the multi-way join in the shape the evaluator calls it -- one plan list for the
positive operands, one for the negative ones, and the operand tables as a single list -- against
the `mmulti_join` the monitor actually runs. Two steps: `mmulti_join_eq_mmulti_join'` says that
whichever of the generic and the pairwise join the monitor picks at run time, it agrees with the
`mmulti_join'` that 20.2's `dmulti_join_dtabc` is stated over (both have the same membership
characterisation; the pairwise one additionally needs the negated conjuncts' columns covered, which
holds for every safe formula), and then `dmulti_join_dtabc` itself.

The `DAnds` node is where `deval` becomes mutually recursive with `deval_list`, and the relation
takes its operand list slot by slot: `∀i < length L. dsim n ((c0 # css @ nss) ! i) (Ld ! i) (L ! i)`.
That indexed form rather than a `list_all2` is deliberate -- the inductive package derives the
induction hypothesis through `∀`/`⟶` but not through a `list_all2` of a pair-splitting lambda.

### 20.6 The verified Since structure at the dense frame, and one firing of it

20.3 argued that the `msaux` specification transports to the dense frame. That argument is now
carried out against the *verified* structure, `mmasaux`, which is what the monitor actually runs:

```isabelle
definition dsaux :: "nat ⇒ cols ⇒ cols ⇒ args ⇒ ts ⇒ mmasaux ⇒ (ts × event_data table) list ⇒ bool" where
  "dsaux n cs_R cs_L args cur daux auxlist ⟷
     (∀(t, rel) ∈ set auxlist. table n (set cs_R) rel) ∧
     valid_mmasaux (dargs cs_R cs_L args) cur daux (dauxlist n cs_R auxlist)"
```

-- the dense structure, run at `dargs`, is valid for the narrowing of the auxiliary list the wide
one is valid for. `dsaux_init`, `dsaux_add_new_ts`, `dsaux_join` and `dsaux_add_new_table` say each
of the four operations preserves it, and `dsaux_result` says the result the dense structure computes
is the narrowing of the one the wide structure computes. Two things the argument in 20.3 had not
accounted for:

**The left operand has to be embedded.** `join_msaux` joins its argument into the running tables, so
the argument must live in the *same* frame as they do: at the dense frame, tuples of width
`length cs_R` with holes outside `cpos cs_R (set cs_L)`. The left operand's own narrowing is at its
own column list `cs_L`, of width `length cs_L`. So a new operation is needed, with the plan computed
once at compile time:

```isabelle
definition emb_plan :: "cols ⇒ cols ⇒ nat option list" where
  "emb_plan cs_R cs_L = map (λc. if c ∈ set cs_L then Some (col_pos cs_L c) else None) cs_R"
definition dembed1 :: "nat option list ⇒ 'a tuple ⇒ 'a tuple" where
  "dembed1 p d = map (λq. case q of None ⇒ None | Some i ⇒ d ! i) p"
definition dembed :: "cols ⇒ cols ⇒ 'a table ⇒ 'a table" where
  "dembed cs_R cs_L D = dembed1 (emb_plan cs_R cs_L) ` D"

theorem dembed_dtabc: "… ⟹ table n (set cs_L) T ⟹
  dembed cs_R cs_L (dtabc n cs_L T) = dtabc n cs_R T"
```

This is a real cost the dense representation pays that the wide one does not -- one pass over the
left operand's table per time point -- but it is the same cost class as the join it feeds.

**The aggregation does not transport.** `dargs` has `args_agg = None`, so `result_msaux` at the dense
frame is the plain union while the wide one applies the aggregation. A Since with an aggregation
pushed into it therefore stays a wide `DPure` node.

**One firing of the Since evaluator.** The node relation puts the two structures and the two
buffers together, sharing the current timestamp -- the structure's, which is also the lower bound
on the timestamps still queued:

```isabelle
definition dsince_rel :: "nat ⇒ cols ⇒ cols ⇒ args ⇒ ts ⇒ mmasaux ⇒ mmasaux ⇒
    event_data mbuf2S ⇒ event_data mbuf2S ⇒ bool" where
  "dsince_rel n cs_R cs_L args tw aux daux buf dbuf ⟷
     (∃cur auxlist. valid_mmasaux args cur aux auxlist ∧
        dsaux n cs_R cs_L args cur daux auxlist ∧ cur ≤ tw ∧
        (case buf of (xs, ys, tss, sk) ⇒
           list_all (ftable n (set cs_L)) xs ∧ list_all (ftable n (set cs_R)) ys ∧
           sorted tss ∧ (∀t ∈ set tss. cur ≤ t ∧ t ≤ tw) ∧
           dbuf = (map (dtabc n cs_R) xs, map (dtabc n cs_R) ys, tss, sk)))"

lemma eval_since_rel:
  assumes "sorted_wrt (<) cs_R" "∀c ∈ set cs_R. c < n" "set cs_L ⊆ set cs_R"
    "args_agg args = None" "args_n args = n" "args_L args = set cs_L" "args_R args = set cs_R"
  shows "meval_since args rs buf aux = (zs, buf', aux') ⟹
    meval_since (dargs cs_R cs_L args) (map (dtabc n cs_R) rs) dbuf daux = (dzs, dbuf', daux') ⟹
    dsince_rel n cs_R cs_L args tw aux daux buf dbuf ⟹
    dzs = map (dtabc n cs_R) zs ∧ dsince_rel n cs_R cs_L args tw aux' daux' buf' dbuf'"
```

The proof is an induction over the kernel's own recursion (`eval_since.induct`, taken at the wide
`args`): the recursion is driven by the buffer alone, so the two sides step in lockstep, and each
of the three stepping equations is one of 20.6's four preservation lemmas plus `dsaux_result`. The
pattern-completion cases, where the buffer has run dry, are the ones where neither side steps.

Stating the dense result as a *given* rather than an existential -- the shape
`mbuf2t_take_rel` already used -- is what makes the proof tractable: the eight remaining
pattern-completion cases then close uniformly, where an existential conclusion had the automation
guessing witnesses inside the verified structure's 11-tuple.

### 20.7 The Since node

Two things were missing between 20.6 and a `dsim` rule for `MSince`.

**A timestamp bound on the relation.** `valid_add_new_ts_msaux` needs `nt ≥ cur`: the structure
only accepts a timestamp at or after the one it is already at. `dsince_rel` carries that for the
timestamps already queued, but a *new* batch arrives at the node as `meval`'s `ts` argument, and
nothing inside the relation can bound it, because `cur` is internal to the node. So `dsim` carries
a timestamp parameter -- `dsim n cs tw φd φ`, read as *every timestamp stored anywhere inside is at
most `tw`* -- and `dsim_deval` assumes `sorted ts` and `∀t ∈ set ts. tw ≤ t`, concluding at the new
bound `dnext tw ts = (if ts = [] then tw else last ts)`. Every rule but `DsSince` (and `DsUntil` to
come) carries `tw` without looking at it. Like `produces` and `wf_db`, the hypothesis is discharged
once, at the top, from the trace's monotonicity -- these three are the whole interface between this
development and the kernel's.

Inside `dsince_rel` the bound reads `cur ≤ tw` and `∀t ∈ set tss. t ≤ tw`, and one lemma does the
transition:

```isabelle
lemma dsince_rel_add:
  assumes "dsince_rel n cs_R cs_L args tw aux daux buf dbuf"
    "list_all (ftable n (set cs_L)) xs" "list_all (ftable n (set cs_R)) ys"
    "sorted ts" "∀t ∈ set ts. tw ≤ t"
  shows "dsince_rel n cs_R cs_L args (dnext tw ts) aux daux (mbuf2S_add xs ys ts buf)
     (mbuf2S_add (map (dtabc n cs_R) xs) (map (dtabc n cs_R) ys) ts dbuf)"
```

The batch's timestamps are all at or after the old bound and the buffer's are all at or before it,
so the concatenation is still sorted; `eval_since_rel` then preserves the bound for free, because
the timestamp it moves `cur` to comes out of the buffer.

**The node.** The dense node stores the embedding plan rather than the two column lists, so
``dembedp p D = dembed1 p ` D`` is the operation the evaluator calls, with `dembedp_dtabc` the
corollary of `dembed_dtabc` at that shape:

```isabelle
| "deval j ts db (DSince dags pl φ ψ buf aux) =
     (let (xs, φ') = deval j ts db φ; (ys, ψ') = deval j ts db ψ;
          (zs, buf', aux') =
            meval_since dags [] (mbuf2S_add (map (dembedp pl) xs) ys ts buf) aux
      in (zs, DSince dags pl φ' ψ' buf' aux'))"

| DsSince: "wf_cols n cs ⟹ produces n (set cs) (mformula.MSince args φ ψ buf aux) ⟹
    dsim n cs_L tw φd φ ⟹ dsim n cs tw ψd ψ ⟹
    wf_cols n cs_L ⟹ set cs_L ⊆ set cs ⟹
    args_agg args = None ⟹ args_n args = n ⟹
    args_L args = set cs_L ⟹ args_R args = set cs ⟹
    dags = dargs cs cs_L args ⟹ pl = emb_plan cs cs_L ⟹
    dsince_rel n cs cs_L args tw aux daux buf dbuf ⟹
    dsim n cs tw (DSince dags pl φd ψd dbuf daux) (mformula.MSince args φ ψ buf aux)"
```

The proof case is three steps: `dembedp_dtabc` pointwise over the left operand's output turns its
own narrowing at `cs_L` into the narrowing at `cs`, which is what `dsince_rel` expects in the
buffer; `dsince_rel_add` puts the batch in; `eval_since_rel` fires the structure. The `DSince` node
is the first one whose *state* is a verified data structure rather than a list of tables, and the
relation between the two states is carried entirely by 20.6's `dsaux`, so nothing about `mmasaux`'s
eleven components appears in the simulation.

### 20.8 The Until node

The Until node needs the same three ingredients, plus one generic buffer lemma.

**The structure.** `duaux` is the Until analogue of `dsaux`: the verified `muaux_dq`, run at
`dargs`, is valid for the narrowing of the auxiliary list the wide one is valid for. An Until entry
carries two tables per time point -- the left operand's accumulated join and the right operand's
accumulated union -- and `dauxlist3` narrows both at the right operand's column list, so the one
new fact needed from 20.2 was already there (`dauxlist3_update_until`, `dauxlist3_eval_until`).
`duaux_init`, `duaux_add_new` and `duaux_eval` are the three preservation lemmas; as with Since the
aggregation does not transport, so `duaux_eval` needs `args_agg args = None` to conclude that the
result list is the plain narrowing.

**The buffer traversal, with timestamps.** The Until node steps its structure from *inside* a
buffer traversal, and `add_new_muaux` accepts a new timestamp only at or after the one the
structure is already at. `mbuf2t_take_rel` cannot express that -- its step condition has to hold
for every timestamp -- so it gets a timestamp-aware sibling:

```isabelle
lemma mbuf2t_take_rel_ts:
  "(⋀a b t u za z'a. a ∈ set xs ⟹ b ∈ set ys ⟹ R u za z'a ⟹ u ≤ t ⟹
      R t (f a b t za) (g (h a) (k b) t z'a)) ⟹
   R u0 z z' ⟹ u0 ≤ t0 ⟹ sorted ts ⟹ (∀t ∈ set ts. u0 ≤ t) ⟹
   mbuf2t_take f z (xs, ys) t0 ts = (w, (xs', ys'), nt, ts') ⟹
   mbuf2t_take g z' (map h xs, map k ys) t0 ts = (w2, buf2, nt2, ts2) ⟹
   (∃u. R u w w2 ∧ u ≤ nt ∧ (∀t ∈ set ts'. u ≤ t)) ∧ sorted ts' ∧
     (nt = t0 ∨ nt ∈ set ts) ∧ set ts' ⊆ set ts ∧
     buf2 = (map h xs', map k ys') ∧ nt2 = nt ∧ ts2 = ts'"
```

The accumulator relation is indexed by the structure's current timestamp; the step may assume the
new timestamp is no smaller; and the conclusion says where the accumulator ended up -- at or before
the timestamp the traversal reports and at or before every timestamp it did not consume. The two
extra conjuncts (`nt = t0 ∨ nt ∈ set ts`, `set ts' ⊆ set ts`) are what lets the node re-establish
its *upper* bound afterwards: whatever the traversal reports came either from the node's own stored
timestamp or from the batch. `mbuf2t_take_sub` is the leftover-buffer analogue of
`mbuf2_take_sub`.

**The node.** `duntil_rel n cs_R cs_L args tw t aux daux buf dbuf nts` holds when the structure's
current timestamp `cur` satisfies `cur ≤ t ≤ tw`, the two structures are related at `cur`
(`duaux_rel`, the existential over the shared auxiliary list, factored out because the traversal
steps it on its own), the buffer's two halves are well formed at their own column lists and the
dense buffer is their narrowing, and every queued timestamp lies in `[cur, tw]`. `until_step_rel`
is the whole firing -- traversal then evaluation -- and `DsUntil` is the rule, with the same twelve
side conditions as `DsSince` plus the node relation.

`Dense_Eval.thy` is 1479 lines, 2355 commands, no `sorry`; `Dense.thy` is 3017 lines, 5359
commands, 6 benign warnings. `dsim_deval` now covers every node of the dense fragment.

### 20.9 The dense monitor inherits the kernel's correctness theorems

`thys/MFODL_Monitor_Imp/Dense_Correct.thy`, 265 lines, 323 commands, no `sorry`. This is the point
of the whole exercise: the simulation carries the kernel's collected correctness results across to
the dense algorithm, with nothing about the dense representation left in the statements.

**The dense monitor.** Its online interface mirrors the kernel's field for field, with
`deval` in place of `meval`, plus the column list its verdicts are narrowed at:

```isabelle
record dmstate = dmstate_i :: nat  dmstate_j :: nat  dmstate_m :: dformula
  dmstate_n :: nat  dmstate_cs :: cols  dmstate_t :: "ts list"

definition dmstep :: "database × ts ⇒ dmstate ⇒ (nat × ts × event_data table) list × dmstate"
definition dmonitor :: "dmstate ⇒ Formula.prefix ⇒ (nat × ts × event_data table) list"
```

**The state relation.**

```isabelle
definition dmsim_state where
  "dmsim_state n cs tw st dst ⟷
     dmstate_i dst = mstate_i st ∧ dmstate_j dst = mstate_j st ∧
     dmstate_t dst = mstate_t st ∧ mstate_n st = n ∧ dmstate_n dst = n ∧
     dmstate_cs dst = cs ∧ dsim n cs tw (dmstate_m dst) (mstate_m st)"
```

`dmstep_mstep` is one step. `dmsteps0_stateless_rel` is the whole prefix, and it is where two of
`dsim_deval`'s three input hypotheses are discharged once and for all: the timestamp bound, from
the prefix's own sortedness (each step re-establishes the bound at the timestamp just consumed, and
the next one is no smaller), and the database condition, from `wf_db_mk_db`.

**The verdicts.** `dmonitor_monitor` says the dense verdicts are the narrowing of the kernel's, and
`dmonitor_widen` says widening them back recovers the kernel's *exactly* -- the narrowing is a
bijection on the tables of the frame (`dtabw_dtabc`) and the kernel's verdict tables live there.
Then, inside `verimon_spec` (which now carries the concrete monitor as a `verimon` interpretation,
by a `sublocale` whose `maux` part the existing `default_maux` interpretation discharges):

```isabelle
theorem (in verimon_spec) dmonitor_mverdicts:
  assumes "dmsim_state n cs 0 (minit_safe φ) dst" and "sorted_wrt (<) cs"
    and "pfinite π" and "wty_prefix SIG π"
  shows "flatten_verdicts (map (λ(i, t, X). (i, t, dtabw n cs X)) (dmonitor dst π)) = Mt π"

theorem (in verimon_spec) dmstep_mverdicts: ...
```

These are the kernel's own `monitor_mverdicts` and `mstep_mverdicts` with the dense monitor in
place of the kernel's. The one thing they still ask of the caller is a related initial state; that
is the compilation, 20.11 below.

**`wf_db` needs finiteness, and it is an input property.** The dense multi-way join and the dense
aggregation need finite tables, so `produces` carries finiteness -- and there is nowhere else it
can come from. The kernel's own invariant does not have it (`qtable n A P Q X ⟷ table n A X ∧ …`,
no finiteness) and neither does the trace (a trace is a sorted stream of arbitrary event sets; the
kernel is correct for infinite databases because `Formula.sat`'s aggregation degenerates the same
way `eval_agg` does). Without it `produces` is not provable even for a predicate node, whose
output is the database's table verbatim. So `wf_db` now says the database's tables are *finite*
tables of full-width tuples, `wf_db_mk_db` asks its event set to be finite, `pfinite` lifts that to
a prefix, and the correctness theorems carry it -- which is what a log gives them.

### 20.10 Discharging `produces`

`thys/MFODL_Monitor_Imp/Dense_Produces.thy` (1069 lines, 3223 commands),
`Dense_Match.thy` (730 lines, 1345 commands) and `Dense_Let.thy` (268 lines, 463 commands); no
`sorry` in any of them. **All 23 constructors of the kernel's formula datatype are done.**

`produces` is the last of the simulation's three input hypotheses, and unlike the other two it is
not a property of the input but of the kernel's evaluation:

```isabelle
coinductive produces :: "nat ⇒ nat set ⇒ ts ⇒ (mmasaux, muaux_dq, event_data mmtaux) mformula ⇒ bool"
  where "(⋀j ts db xs φ'. wf_db db ⟹ sorted ts ⟹ (∀t ∈ set ts. tw ≤ t) ⟹
      meval j n ts db φ = (xs, φ') ⟹
      list_all (λX. table n A X ∧ finite X) xs ∧ produces n A (dnext tw ts) φ')
    ⟹ produces n A tw φ"
```

It quantifies over *every* well-formed database, not just the ones a particular trace produces, so
it cannot be read off the kernel's `meval_correct` (which needs `wf_envs`, hence a trace and the
progress bookkeeping). It is a kernel-side obligation of its own: *the executable monitor's tables
are finite tables over the free variables, for any finite well-formed database*.

The timestamp lower bound `tw` is not decoration. The verified Since and Until structures accept a
new timestamp only at or after the one they are already at (`valid_add_new_ts_msaux`,
`valid_add_new_muaux`), and a fresh batch of timestamps arrives at a node from outside, where no
per-node invariant can see it. So the invariant reads *every timestamp in the batch is at least
`tw`*, and re-establishes itself at the bound `dnext tw ts` the batch leaves behind -- exactly the
bound `dsim` already carried for the same reason. The non-temporal nodes carry it without using it.
`produces_mono` says raising the bound is harmless, which is what the recursive let needs when it
rejects a round.

It is discharged **constructor by constructor** rather than through one monolithic invariant: for
each node of the kernel's formula, a lemma saying that if the operands produce finite tables over
their columns then so does the node. Each is a coinduction with an invariant that says only what
that node needs, so the lemmas compose by structural induction at the end and could be done
independently. Four are packaged:

- `producesI` -- the coinduction with the trivial equations of its conclusion already solved, so a
  node's lemma only has to name its own invariant;
- `produces_fix` -- a node unchanged by evaluation (`MRel`, `MPred`, `MTS`);
- `produces_unop` -- one operand, output mapped pointwise, which covers every unary node; the
  table-and-finiteness obligation is the only thing that varies from one to the next;
- `produces_binop` -- two operands and a two-way buffer, where the invariant additionally carries
  the buffer's own well-formedness; it survives because a take only removes a prefix
  (`mbuf2_take_sub`) and the outputs are what the step function built (`mbuf2_take_out`).

**The simple nodes.** `MRel`; `MNeg` (unconditionally -- its output is the unit or the empty table
either way); `MAndRel`, `MAndAssign`, `MExists`, `MAgg` through `produces_unop`; all three modes of
`MPred` (`wf_tuple_simple_match` and `match_wf_tuple` from the kernel's own correctness development
give the two matching modes their shape); `MTP`, `MTS`; `MOr` and `MAnd` through `produces_binop`
(`ftable_bin_join`: at tables of the operands' frames `bin_join` is the plain join, so `join_table`
and `finite_join` give the result's shape); `MPrev` and `MNext`, whose outputs come from the buffer
or are the empty table (`mprev_next_out`). For `MAgg`, `ftable_eval_agg` is the one with content:
the operand's columns sit above the aggregation's group-by prefix, so dropping that prefix lands the
tuple on the group key and writing the aggregate into position `y` widens it by exactly `{y}`.

`MAnds` is the glue over `mmulti_join`'s shape: at well-formed operands it is the left-to-right
fold of the positive tables followed by the anti-joins against the negative ones -- the same fold
`dmulti_join_dtabc` uses -- and `kands_pos_ftable` / `kands_neg_ftable` say each step of both folds
keeps the table over the accumulated columns and keeps it finite. `mbufn_take_out` is the
multi-way analogue of `mbuf2_take_out`.

**The temporal nodes.** `MSince`, `MUntil` and `MTrigger` each carry the *wide half* of the
relation 20.6-20.8 already built for the dense side: the verified structure is valid for an
abstract auxiliary list whose tables are finite tables over the node's columns, at a current
timestamp that the buffer's timestamps -- and the batch still to come -- are at or after.

- `saux_inv` / `eval_since_ftable`: one firing of the kernel's own `eval_since` recursion, the same
  induction `eval_since_rel` follows, with `ftable_result_mmasaux` reading the structure's result
  off `valid_result_msaux` as a union of the auxiliary list's tables. The three auxiliary-list
  steps -- filter, join, `add_new_table` -- got finiteness alongside the well-formedness Dense.thy
  already had for them.
- `uaux_inv` / `until_step_ftable`: the Until structure is stepped from inside a buffer traversal,
  so the traversal gets its own invariant lemma (`mbuf2t_take_inv`), which reports the timestamp
  the traversal stops at and that every timestamp it leaves queued is at or after the one the
  accumulator reached.
- `taux_inv` / `trigger_step_ftable`: the trigger structure's result is genuinely *not* a table
  over a fixed column set -- with an empty window it reports the unit table, and the column set it
  returns alongside says so. But the kernel only builds a trigger node for an interval containing
  zero, and then the entry the update has just appended is itself in the interval, so the window is
  never empty: the result is a table over the right operand's columns, and it is finite because
  that entry's own table bounds it (the last index has nowhere later to send its disjunct).
- `MAndTrigger` is the fused conjunction-with-a-trigger, and there the trigger's varying column set
  is not discarded: the buffer holds the pairs the structure reports and the join reads each pair's
  column set off the pair. With an interval containing zero that set is always the right operand's,
  so the join is the ordinary one and the result stays over the conjunction's columns.

**The match nodes** (`Dense_Match.thy`) need a toolkit of their own: the kernel's derivative
operations on marked regular expressions. Finiteness of those is purely structural -- every one of
them builds unions and joins of the operand tables -- but well-formedness over the node's column
set is not, because `rε_strict` returns the unit table at a `Skip 0`, and that is a table over the
node's columns only because the safety condition forces them to be empty there. So `ε_lax` and the
two derivatives (`ftable_rdelta`, `ftable_ldelta`) are proved directly, while the two strict
epsilons borrow the kernel's own `qtable_rε_strict` and `qtable_lε_strict`: instantiated at the
empty restriction, where `qtable` degenerates to `table` (`qtable_mem_restr_empty`), they give
exactly what is needed without redoing their inductions over the safety predicate. On top of that:
`mbufnt_take_out` for the n-ary buffer traversal, `maux_inv` (a mapping per timestamp) for the past
node, `mfaux_inv` (the operands plus the accumulated table) for the future one, and the two node
lemmas. Neither match node needs the timestamp bound -- their updates accept any timestamp.

**The let nodes** (`Dense_Let.thy`). This is where 20.10's earlier reading was wrong: `MLet`,
`MLetPast` and `MFrz` need **no** predicate-environment generalisation of `produces`. Because
`produces` quantifies over every well-formed database, the body's own invariant applies to the
extended one as soon as the extension is well formed -- and `wf_db_update` says it is, since the
bound formula is evaluated at the full frame `{0..<m}`, which is exactly the shape `wf_db` asks of
a stored relation of arity `m`. `wf_db_map_values_empty` covers the emptied databases the other two
build.

- `MLet`: one extension, one evaluation.
- `MLetPast`: `letpast_meval0_ftable` carries the invariant through the fixpoint iteration. Each
  round evaluates against a database in which the bound predicate holds the previous round's output
  and -- from the second round on -- everything else is empty, at an empty batch of timestamps, so
  the bound does not move after the first round. The guard that rejects a round in which the
  formula's size changed keeps the old state, which the invariant still covers by `produces_mono`.
- `MFrz`: `freeze_meval0_ftable`. The body is evaluated once against the database with the bound
  predicate empty, and then once more per stored operand table against a database holding just that
  table; the states those repeated evaluations produce are discarded rather than threaded.

### 20.11 The dense monitor, measured again

The proofs of 20.1-20.10 did not change a line of the running program -- `src/` is unchanged since
the commit 19.7 was measured on -- so this is a confirmation run, on a machine that is faster in
absolute terms than 19.7's (MonPoly's P2 is 0.37s here against 0.44s there). Compare within a row.

Median of nine, all five configurations byte-identical on every formula (55 runs, one output hash
per row); `dense+h` is `-dense -heap_maps`. Benchmarks under a quarter of a second -- `and`,
`andnot`, `since`, `until`, `agg` -- move by a tenth between runs and should not be read closely.

| formula | MonPoly | pure | dense | dense+h | heap |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| P2 | 0.37s | 2.50x | **1.71x** | 1.72x | 1.85x |
| P3 | 0.43s | 1.91x | 1.35x | **1.34x** | 1.39x |
| and | 0.14s | 1.31x | 1.05x | **0.90x** | 1.24x |
| andnot | 0.13s | 0.93x | 0.97x | **0.90x** | 0.95x |
| once | 0.58s | 1.20x | 0.79x | **0.77x** | 0.83x |
| since | 0.22s | **1.17x** | 1.22x | 1.23x | 1.28x |
| eventually | 0.39s | 1.29x | 0.92x | 0.88x | **0.86x** |
| until | 0.20s | 1.45x | 1.54x | **1.39x** | 1.42x |
| agg | 0.15s | 1.57x | 1.52x | **1.50x** | 1.53x |
| complex | 0.70s | 2.03x | 1.34x | **1.13x** | 1.39x |
| aggwide | 2.97s | 1.18x | 0.87x | **0.84x** | 1.04x |

Short tuples are the best or joint-best configuration on nine of eleven, four are at or faster
than MonPoly, and 19.7's conclusions all hold. Differentially, `-dense` and `-dense -heap_maps`
agree with the plain verified kernel on all 537 `-verified` invocations of the two cram suites.

**What the short tuples alone buy**, `pure / dense`, is the number this section is really about:

| P2 | P3 | and | andnot | once | since | eventually | until | agg | complex | aggwide |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1.46x | 1.41x | 1.24x | 0.96x | 1.52x | 0.96x | 1.40x | 0.94x | 1.03x | 1.52x | 1.36x |

**And the three benchmarks that get nothing name exactly what is left to do.** `agg` (1.03x) is
19.6's case: `s <- SUM y; x A(x,y)` has no empty columns anywhere, so there is nothing to drop --
which is why `aggwide` is in the table at all, and it gets 1.36x. But `since` (0.96x) and `until`
(0.94x) are a different matter, and the dense node counter says why:

```
$ MONPOLY_DENSE_STATS=1 monpoly -verified -dense …
[dense] subformulas still evaluated by the sparse function: 1   (since, until)
                                                            0   (all nine others)
```

The running dense fragment takes a Since only when `sevt_applies args`, that is
`args_agg args = None ∧ args_L args = {}` -- the left operand has no free variables, so the node is
an `ONCE` -- and an Until only under the analogous `evt_applies`. A genuine two-sided
`B(x,y) SINCE[0,50] A(x,y)` fails that test, becomes a single `DPure` node covering the whole
formula, and `-dense` then only pays the narrowing at the boundary and gains nothing. That is
precisely the 4% it loses. `once` and `eventually`, which do pass, get 1.52x and 1.40x.

This is the gap 20.6-20.8 close on paper: `dsaux` and `duaux` are stated over the *general*
`mmasaux` and `muaux_dq`, with no `args_L = {}` restriction, and `DSince` and `DUntil` are proved
against them. Rebuilding the running fragment on those (20.12 item 3) is therefore not only what
makes `-dense` a refinement of the verified kernel rather than a parallel implementation of it --
it is also the one change these measurements say is still worth wall clock, on the two benchmarks
that currently get none.

### 20.12 What is left

1. **The compilation.** Done: 20.13 does the `produces` half and closes the chain through the
   escape hatch `DPure`, down to the kernel's own initial state, and 20.14 replaces that escape
   hatch by a descending compiler.
2. **The Since and Until cases.** Done: 20.15 rebuilds the running fragment on 20.6-20.8, so a
   genuine two-sided Since or Until is a dense node rather than a `DPure` one, and re-measures.
3. **The heap index.** Done: 20.17. `dbin_join_h` and the `didx_ins` / `dprobe_r` / `dprobe_l`
   it calls were Imperative HOL with no Hoare triples, and unlike `hprobe_join`, which Section 8
   could prove because it was built on the AFP's verified `Hash_Map`, they are built on the
   bespoke table of `Table_Imp`, which had to be verified first. `Imap_Correct.thy` does that and
   `Dense_Join_Correct.thy` the join on top of it, up to `dmulti_join`, which is the shape the
   evaluator calls.
4. **The session layout.** Settled, the other way round: 20.16 says why the Dense theories stay
   in `MFODL_Monitor_Imp`.

### 20.13 Closing the chain: the criterion and the initial state

The transferred theorems of 20.9 all ask the caller for a related initial state,
`dmsim_state n cs 0 (minit_safe φ) dst`, and until one is produced they say nothing about any
concrete monitor. `Dense_Compile.thy` produces one.

**The criterion.** The node lemmas of 20.10 each say: if a node's operands produce finite tables
over their columns, so does the node. They compose by structural induction, and

```isabelle
inductive mok :: "nat ⇒ nat set ⇒ ts ⇒ (mmasaux, muaux_dq, event_data mmtaux) mformula ⇒ bool"
```

is what they compose into -- one rule per node, carrying exactly that node's lemma's hypotheses
with the operands' `produces` replaced by `mok` itself, and

```isabelle
theorem mok_produces: "mok n A tw φ ⟹ produces n A tw φ"
```

discharges them all by `mok.induct`, one node lemma per case. `mok` is inductive rather than a
recursive function because the two match rules quantify over the atom list the marked regular
expression was compiled from: `safe_regex` is a property of the source regex and is not
recoverable from the `mformula` alone.

**Two node lemmas had to be corrected or extended first.**

*The aggregated Since and Until.* `produces_MSince` and `produces_MUntil` were proved for
`args_agg args = None`. The kernel also fuses an aggregation into these two nodes: for
`Agg y ω tys f (Since φ I ψ)` it builds a single `MSince` whose `args` carry `Some aggargs`,
evaluating the operands at the inner frame `length tys + n` and emitting tables at the outer frame
`n`. Only the output step of each proof looks at the aggregation, so each is generalised over what
that step delivers -- a parameter `Res` on the structure's result for Since, an output frame
`(nr, Ar)` for both -- and the aggregated instances follow. This is `Dense_Agg.thy`.

*The aggregation's columns.* `produces_MAgg` asked for `∀i ∈ G. length (aggargs_tys args) ≤ i` of
the operand's columns `G`. In the kernel's own initial state that hypothesis cannot hold:
`safe_formula` for `Agg y ω tys f φ` requires `{0..<length tys} ⊆ fv φ`, so `G` always contains the
bound columns, and the lemma was only about aggregations that bind nothing. `eval_agg` drops the
first `length tys` entries of each tuple and groups by what is left, so the node's columns are

```isabelle
Set.insert y ((λi. i - length tys) ` (G - {0..<length tys}))
```

and `ftable_eval_agg`, `produces_MAgg` and the aggregated Since and Until now say that.

**The state.** With the criterion in hand,

```isabelle
definition dpure_state :: "nat ⇒ cols ⇒ mstate ⇒ dmstate"
lemma dmsim_state_dpure_state_mok:
  "wf_cols n cs ⟹ mstate_n st = n ⟹ mok n (set cs) tw (mstate_m st)
     ⟹ dmsim_state n cs tw st (dpure_state n cs st)"
theorem dmonitor_mverdicts_pure (in verimon_spec)
```

produce a related state through the `DsPure` rule of 20.9, whose only content beyond the column
list is `produces`. The monitor built this way runs the kernel's evaluator and narrows its output,
so it is the bottom of the refinement rather than the interesting point of it; what it settles is
that the theorems are about something, and what the descending compiler of 20.12 will replace is
the dense formula, not the chain.

**The initial state.** What `dmonitor_mverdicts_pure` still asks of its caller is

```isabelle
lemma mok_minit0:
  "safe_formula φ ⟹ ∀x ∈ fv φ. x < n ⟹ mok n (fv φ) tw (minit0 n φ)"
```

by induction with `safe_formula_induct`, all thirty-two cases, in `Dense_Init.thy`, which is now
part of the session. The initial state makes the auxiliary invariants cheap -- `saux_inv_init`,
`until_inv_init` and `trigger_inv_init` all rest on the kernel's own `valid_init_*` lemmas at
`cur = 0` -- and the work is in following `minit0` through the rewritings: the release forms, the
fused aggregations, the multi-way conjunction's positive and negative partitions, and the two
match nodes' `to_mregex`.

**A third node lemma had to be weakened: the fused trigger.** `produces_MAndTrigger` assumed
`mem (args_ivl args) 0`, and the `And_Trigger` case of the induction cannot supply it. The kernel
splits on exactly that interval: when it contains `0` the trigger is itself `safe_formula`, so
`minit0` builds an ordinary `MAnd` over the trigger's own `MTrigger` node and the existing rules
suffice; when it does not, `safe_formula` of the trigger is `False` and `minit0` builds the fused
`MAndTrigger`. The assumption was there because the fused node's buffer holds *pairs* -- the
trigger structure reports its own column set alongside its table -- and the old invariant pinned
that set to the right operand's `R`, which holds only while the window is nonempty; on an empty
window the structure reports the empty column set and the unit table. So the buffer invariant

```isabelle
list_all (λp. fst p = R ∧ ftable n R (snd p)) (snd buf1)
```

becomes a bound,

```isabelle
list_all (λp. fst p ⊆ R ∧ ftable n (fst p) (snd p)) (snd buf1)
```

which is all the following join needs: with `R ⊆ A` the join is against a table over a subset of
the conjunction's columns, so its result is still a table over `A`. The four weakened lemmas
`ftable_trigger_results_weak`, `taux_inv_step_weak`, `trigger_pairs_step_weak` and
`produces_MAndTrigger_weak` are in `Dense_Agg.thy`, and `MokAndTrigger` now carries
`mem (args_ivl args) 0 ∨ (args_pos args ∧ L = R)` in place of the interval condition.

Finiteness of the result in the new case is the one point that needs an argument rather than a
weakening. `safe_dual True safe_formula φ' I ψ'` with `¬ mem I 0` forces `safe_formula φ'` and
`fv φ' = fv ψ'`, so `L = R` and the structure is built with `args_pos args`. Every tuple the
trigger reports is then `wf_tuple n R`, so `wf_tuple_proj_idle` makes the join mask the identity
on it and the structure's own characterisation puts each reported tuple in `r i0 ∪ (⋃ j. l j)` for
any in-window index `i0` -- a finite union of finite tables. That is `ftable_trigger_results_weak`.

**The chain, closed.** With the last case in place,

```isabelle
theorem dmonitor_mverdicts_dense (in verimon_spec):
  "pfinite π ⟹ wty_prefix SIG π ⟹
   flatten_verdicts (map (λ(i, t, X). (i, t, dtabw (nfv φ) (set_cols (fv φ)) X))
     (dmonitor (dpure_state (nfv φ) (set_cols (fv φ)) (minit_safe φ)) π)) = Mt π"
```

states the dense monitor's correctness with no hypotheses left for the caller beyond the locale's
own `mmonitorable φ` and the prefix being a finite, well-typed log. `Dense_Init.thy` is in `ROOT`
and the session builds with no `sorry`.

### 20.14 The descending compiler

20.13 relates the compiled state to the kernel's through a single `DPure` node at the root. That
is honest but inert: `DPure` runs the kernel's own evaluator and narrows its output, so none of
the dense representation is used. `Dense_Descend.thy` replaces it by a compiler that descends into
the formula and emits a dense node wherever the simulation has a rule for one.

```isabelle
fun dof :: "nat ⇒ cols ⇒ (mmasaux, muaux_dq, event_data mmtaux) mformula ⇒ dformula"
```

`dof n cs φ` is a plain function of the kernel's formula and the column list its result is to
have. It is total: at every constructor it either builds the dense node or falls back to
`DPure φ n (wide_mask n cs)`, and each dense branch is guarded by a decidable check of exactly
what the corresponding `dsim` rule asks for. That is what makes the correctness proof work case by
case without a separate well-formedness criterion: where a guard fails the fallback is sound
anyway, because `DsPure` needs only `produces`, which 20.13 already supplies for every node of the
initial state.

**No second pass computing a node's columns.** The monadic compiler in `Dense_Imp.thy` carries an
`mcols` that reads a node's column set off it. The pure compiler does not need one. At every
recursive call the operand's column list is read off the node itself -- the two variable sets a
conjunction stores, the argument record a temporal node stores, the operand columns an aggregation
stores -- with one exception, the existential, where it is reconstructed from the node's own
columns: a safe existential binds a variable that does occur, so

```isabelle
dof n cs (mformula.MExists φ) = DExists (dof (Suc n) (0 # map Suc cs) φ) True
```

is right, and `ex_cols (0 # map Suc cs) = cs` closes the circle. The corresponding fact about the
kernel's column sets, `set_cols (fv φ) = 0 # map Suc (set_cols (fvi 1 φ))` for `0 ∈ fv φ`, is the
only place the column arithmetic of the binders shows up.

**The theorem.**

```isabelle
theorem dsim_dof_minit0:
  "safe_formula φ ⟹ ∀x ∈ fv φ. x < n ⟹
   dsim n (set_cols (fv φ)) tw (dof n (set_cols (fv φ)) (minit0 n φ)) (minit0 n φ)"
```

by `safe_formula_induct` again, one lemma per constructor proved beforehand with the induction
hypotheses as assumptions -- so the thirty-two cases of the induction itself are one line each.
Two of the lemmas needed something new. The aggregation's operand columns had to be shown to be
`[0..<k] @ map (+ k) gcols`, which is where a strictly sorted list of naturals containing
`{0..<k}` begins with `0, …, k-1`; `strict_sorted_take_upt` is that fact. The Since and Until
nodes needed the dense structure at time `0`, which is `dsaux_init` and `duaux_init` of 20.6-20.8
paired with the kernel's own `valid_init_*`; `dsince_rel_init` and `duntil_rel_init` package them.

**What compiles densely.** Every constructor the dense fragment covers: relations, the three
prediction modes, the conjunction in its three forms, the multi-way conjunction, negation,
disjunction, the existential, the aggregation, previous and next, and Since and Until. What stays
`DPure` is what `dsim` has no rule for -- `Let`, `LetPast`, `Frz`, the assignment conjunction, the
match nodes, the trigger nodes, the time-point and timestamp nodes, and the Since and Until with a
fused aggregation, whose structure carries the aggregation in its arguments while the dense ones
do not.

**The chain, once more.** `dof_state` puts the compiled formula into the dense monitor's state
record, and

```isabelle
theorem dmonitor_mverdicts_dof (in verimon_spec):
  "pfinite π ⟹ wty_prefix SIG π ⟹
   flatten_verdicts (map (λ(i, t, X). (i, t, dtabw (nfv φ) (set_cols (fv φ)) X))
     (dmonitor (dof_state (nfv φ) (set_cols (fv φ)) (minit_safe φ)) π)) = Mt π"
```

is 20.13's theorem on that state instead of the inert one: the same correctness statement, now
about a monitor that really runs on short tuples.

### 20.15 The general Since and Until, running

The fragment 20.11 measured took a Since only when `sevt_applies args` -- `args_agg args = None`
and `args_L args = {}`, so the node is an `ONCE` -- and an Until only under the analogous
`evt_applies`. Anything else became a single `DPure` node covering the whole formula. 20.6-20.8
lift that restriction on paper: `dsaux` and `duaux` are stated over the general `mmasaux` and
`muaux_dq` at the narrow frame `dargs cs cs_L args`, with the left operand's tables embedded into
the wider frame by `emb_plan`. `Dense_Imp.thy` now does the same:

```isabelle
| DSince args "nat option list option" dformula dformula "event_data mebuf2S" msaux_j
```

The new field is the embedding plan, `None` when none is needed. `dformula_of` builds the dense
node whenever `args_agg args = None`, the node's frame is the formula's, its right columns are the
node's own, and the left columns are contained in them -- and falls back to `DPure` otherwise, as
before. `deval` embeds the left operand's tables through the plan before the buffer.

**`None` when none is needed** is not a detail. Where the two operands have the same columns --
which is exactly the `since` and `until` benchmarks, `B(x,y) SINCE[0,50] A(x,y)` over a
two-column frame -- the plan is the identity, and building every left tuple again through it costs
more than the short tuples save. Emitting `None` there took the two benchmarks from a loss to a
wash, and the wide ones from a wash to a win.

**Measured**, median of seven on a log four times the size of 20.11's (8000 time points, so the
differences are above the noise floor, which the unchanged `once` row calibrates at about three
points):

| formula | before: pure | before: dense | before | after: pure | after: dense | after |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| sincewide | 3.57s | 3.48s | 1.03x | 3.56s | 2.98s | **1.19x** |
| untilwide | 3.84s | 3.55s | 1.08x | 3.71s | 3.18s | **1.17x** |
| once | 2.80s | 1.92s | 1.46x | 2.82s | 1.96s | 1.44x |

where `sincewide` is `C(z) AND (B(x,y) SINCE[0,50] A(x,y))` and `untilwide` is
`C(z) AND (A(x,y) UNTIL[0,20] B(x,y))` -- a two-sided operator inside a wider formula, so its
tuples have a column to drop. `once` is the control: its path did not change, and it did not move.

**On `since` and `until` themselves the change buys nothing, and 20.11's diagnosis of them was
wrong.** Those formulas are `B(x,y) SINCE[0,50] A(x,y)` at a two-column frame: every column is
live at every node, so there are no empty columns to drop and the dense representation is the
sparse one. Their 0.96x and 0.94x were not the `DPure` fallback -- they were 19.3's case, the same
one `agg` is in. The node counter now reports zero subformulas evaluated by the sparse function on
every benchmark in the table, `since` and `until` included, and they sit at a wash.

The cram suites are green and all 537 `-verified` invocations agree between `-dense` and the plain
verified kernel.

### 20.16 Where the Dense theories live

They stay in `MFODL_Monitor_Imp`. Moving the ten pure-HOL ones -- `Dense`, `Dense_Eval`,
`Dense_Produces`, `Dense_Match`, `Dense_Let`, `Dense_Agg`, `Dense_Correct`, `Dense_Compile`,
`Dense_Init` and `Dense_Descend` -- down into `MFODL_Monitor_Devel` is tidy on paper, since none
of them is an imperative refinement and none imports anything from the imperative session, and it
does cut the imperative session from 4:20 to 2:22. It was tried and reverted, because it pays for
that with the wrong thing: every change to a Dense theory then rebuilds the base session, which
is the expensive one, instead of the cheap one. The dense development is still being worked on,
and 20.17's Hoare triples will keep it that way for a while; the split that matters is between
what is finished and what is not, not between what is pure and what is imperative.

The base session's build is unchanged by this, and Section 5's recipe stands as it was.

### 20.17 The table the heap index is built on

Section 8 could prove `hprobe_join` in a page because it was built on `Hash_Map` from
`Separation_Logic_Imperative_HOL`, which comes verified. The dense index is not: it is built on
the table of `Table_Imp`, which Sections 10-13 wrote directly on an array for the reasons that
theory gives -- the hash stored beside the key so that growing redistributes without rehashing, a
power-of-two capacity so that the bucket index is a mask, and a hash-taking variant of every
operation so that a lookup followed by an update hashes once. That table had no correctness
property at all, and neither do the sparse join, the imperative Since and the imperative Until
that also use it. So the triples item 3 asks for need the table verified first, and that is the
bulk of the work.

`Imap_Correct.thy` does it.

```isabelle
definition is_imap :: "(event_data tuple ⇒ 'v option) ⇒ ('v::heap) imap ⇒ assn"
```

relates a table on the heap to a partial function. The invariant is that the array has the
capacity the record carries, that every entry sits in the bucket its stored hash selects and
stores the hash of its own key, and that no key occurs twice. The entry count the record carries
is deliberately left unconstrained: it only decides when the table grows, and correctness does
not depend on it.

**`ihash` and `ibkt` stay folded.** Both are `code_printing`-replaced at code generation -- in HOL
they are the constant `0`, and the OCaml is the hash and the mask `Table_Imp` documents. A proof
that unfolded them would be a proof about a table with a single bucket, and would say nothing
about the program that runs. All the proof uses of them is that `ihash` is a function, which it
is, and that a bucket index is in range; `ibkt_in_range` is the only lemma that looks at `ibkt`,
and it is what the OCaml `h land (n-1)` satisfies for a power-of-two capacity.

**The map is read off the key's own bucket.**

```isabelle
definition imap_map where "imap_map n bs x = hm_look_list (ihash x) x (bs ! ibucket n x)"
```

The first attempt said instead that the map of a bucket list is `map_of` of all its entries. That
is the true statement globally, but every operation changes exactly one bucket, and each change
then had to be re-proved against the whole concatenation. Reading the map off the bucket the key
belongs to -- which is what the lookup does -- makes an update of one bucket a local change of
the map, and of the invariant too: a key can only ever sit in the bucket its hash selects, so
keys of different buckets are automatically distinct and the global distinctness is exactly
distinctness inside each bucket. The two readings are identified once, in `imap_map_global`, and
that one lemma is what lets a map be compared across two different capacities -- which is the
whole of what growth needs.

**What is proved.** `im_new_rule`, `im_lookup_h_rule`, `im_lookup_rule`, `im_update_h_rule` and
`im_update_rule`:

```isabelle
lemma im_update_h_rule:
  "<is_imap m q> im_update_h q (ihash x) x v <λq'. is_imap (m(x ↦ v)) q'>⇩t"
```

with, underneath it, the growth step in full: `im_entries_rule` for the fold over the buckets,
`im_fill_rule` for the re-insertion into the larger array, and `im_grow_rule` tying them
together. Both loops are `partial_function (heap)`, so neither gets a structural induction: the
fold is an induction on how many buckets are left and the fill an induction on the list of
entries still to place. The `⇩t` is what lets the old array be left behind.

Growth does not change the map because it does not change the entries -- only their order and
their buckets -- and the map of a table is the map of its entries. An update puts the new entry
at the head of the key's bucket in both of its branches, and they differ only in what stays
behind it, the bucket as it was when the key is new and the bucket without that key's entry when
it is not, so one lemma about the bucket serves both.

**The join itself**, in `Dense_Join_Correct.thy`, is then what that description promised:
`didx_ins`, `dprobe_r` and `dprobe_l` are list inductions over `is_imap`, each saying that the
loop computes what the pure `dindex`, `dprobe_step` and `dprobe_step_l` compute, and

```isabelle
lemma dbin_join_h_rule:
  "set (csorted_list_of_set A) = A ⟹ set (csorted_list_of_set B) = B ⟹
   <emp> dbin_join_h pl A pos B <λr. ↑(r = dbin_join pl A pos B)>⇩t"
```

is the case distinction its definition already makes. One thing had to be noticed there: the
index picks the side to build on by list length, while `dgen_join` picks by cardinality, and on
a tie they pick differently. It does not matter, because `dgen_join_eq` already says that either
choice computes the pairs that agree on the shared columns, merged; the two loops are proved
against that set, not against each other.

`dands_pos_h`, `dands_neg_h` and `dmulti_join` follow, since nearly every conjunction is
compiled to a multi-way one and the join is reached through them rather than directly; their
loops carry the finiteness `dbin_join_h_rule` asks for from one operand to the next.

```isabelle
theorem dmulti_join_rule:
  "∀u ∈ set us. finite u ⟹
   <emp> Dense_Imp.dmulti_join ps ns us <λr. ↑(r = Dense.dmulti_join ps ns us)>⇩t"
```

So `-dense -heap_maps` now computes what `-dense` computes, which 20.2 in turn relates to the
kernel. `im_delete_h` and `im_filter`, which the Since and Until structures use but the join does
not, are untouched; `Table_Imp` still has no correctness property beyond what the join needed.

The generated OCaml is byte-identical -- the two theories add lemmas and no code equations -- and
both cram suites are green. Differentially, all 537 `-verified` invocations of the suites agree
between `-dense` and the plain verified kernel, and all 537 agree between `-dense` and
`-dense -heap_maps`, which is the branch this section proves.

### 20.18 Timings on the current build

Re-measured after 20.16's revert and 20.17's theories. The exported OCaml is byte-identical to
what 20.15 measured, so this is a re-measurement, not a new measurement; it is here because the
sub-second rows of 20.11's table are too noisy to read and because the wide Since and Until have
never been placed against MonPoly.

Nine repetitions each, medians, on the 8.6 MB synthetic log -- every row runs for seconds, which
is where the numbers mean something. Multipliers are relative to MonPoly, lower is better.

| formula | MonPoly | pure | dense | dense+h | pure/dense |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| sincewide | 3.12s | 1.13x | **0.93x** | 0.95x | 1.21x |
| untilwide | 3.13s | 1.14x | **0.99x** | 1.01x | 1.15x |
| aggwide | 12.51s | 1.06x | **0.80x** | 0.80x | 1.34x |
| once | 2.45s | 1.15x | **0.76x** | 0.77x | 1.51x |
| eventually | 1.64s | 1.27x | **0.88x** | 0.88x | 1.45x |
| complex | 2.96s | 1.81x | 1.25x | **1.10x** | 1.45x |

**The short tuples put the verified monitor ahead of MonPoly on five of these six.** That is the
headline of the whole dense line of work, and it is the first time the wide Since and Until are
in it: 20.15 reported them only as pure-against-dense ratios, so their standing against MonPoly
was not on record. `sincewide` at 0.93x and `untilwide` at 0.99x are small margins on a three
second run, but they are medians of nine, and the direction is consistent with the 1.21x and
1.15x that 20.15's rebuild bought.

**The heap index does not pay on them.** `dense+h` is a hair *behind* `dense` on `sincewide`
(0.95x vs 0.93x) and `untilwide` (1.01x vs 0.99x), and identical on `aggwide`, `once` and
`eventually`; it earns its keep only on `complex` (1.10x vs 1.25x), which is the one row with a
genuine multi-way join on wide operands. So 20.17's triples are worth having for what they
license -- `-dense -heap_maps` is now proved, where before it was the one unproved path -- rather
than for a speed-up that is there in general. The flag stays off by default, which is what
`dense_heap_join` being `False` in HOL and a mutable OCaml flag arranges.

The sub-second table of 20.11 was re-run too and agrees with itself within its own noise; nothing
regressed from the revert or from adding two theories of lemmas.


### 20.19 Profiling the dense code against MonPoly

Callgrind, `complex` on the 2.1 MB synthetic log, output to `/dev/null` in both cases. `perf` is
unavailable for this kernel, so all instruction figures are Valgrind's and therefore exact and
reproducible rather than sampled.

| | MonPoly | dense | ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| instructions | 6.876 G | 7.194 G | 1.05x |
| ... of which output | 817 M (11.9%) | 2 M (0.03%) | |
| ... of which lexing | 125 M | 125 M | 1.00x |
| ... of which GC | 445 M (6.5%) | 789 M (11.0%) | 1.77x |
| ... of which `caml_apply*` | 95 M (1.4%) | 490 M (6.8%) | 5.2x |
| data refs | 2.619 G | 2.861 G | 1.09x |
| **D1 misses** | 40.2 M (1.5%) | **95.1 M (3.3%)** | **2.37x** |
| ... of which writes | 17.8 M | 56.8 M | **3.19x** |
| **minor words allocated** | 132.9 M | **423.8 M** | **3.19x** |
| promoted words | 5.56 M | 13.00 M | 2.34x |
| peak major heap | 188 Kwords | 373 Kwords | 1.98x |

**The instruction counts are nearly equal; the miss counts are not.** Discounting the parts that
are not monitoring -- MonPoly's output costs 817 M because it prints through `printf`, where the
verified path has the direct printer of Section 12 and costs 2 M -- the monitoring work itself is
5.934 G against 7.067 G, or 1.19x. But the dense monitor takes 2.37x the D1 misses, and its
*write* misses are 3.19x, which is exactly its allocation ratio. That equality is the finding:
every allocated word is a write into a cold minor-heap line, so the remaining gap is allocation
traffic and nothing else.

A larger minor heap does not recover it. On the 8.6 MB log, `complex` at 3.589 s runs in 3.752 s
at `s=8M`, 3.673 s at `s=32M`, 3.630 s at `s=64M` and 3.733 s at `s=32M,o=200` -- all within 5%,
none an improvement worth the name. So the cost is not collection work, which a bigger nursery
would defer; it is the allocation stores themselves. That also explains why 20.18 finds the heap
index no faster than the pure dense code: a hash table removes comparisons and indirections,
which are not what is short.

**Where the dense monitor's instructions go** (share of 7.194 G, with call counts):

| function | Ir | share | calls |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `comparator_event_data` | 574 M | 7.98% | 27.1 M |
| `caml_apply2` | 462 M | 6.43% | 57.4 M |
| `comparator_list` (generic) | 354 M | 4.93% | 15.1 M |
| `balance` | 347 M | 4.82% | 5.0 M |
| `rbt_comp_lookup` | 345 M | 4.80% | 2.9 M |
| `ed_comparator` (monomorphic) | 296 M | 4.12% | 8.7 M |
| `rbt_comp_ins` | 239 M | 3.32% | 6.4 M |
| `comparator_option` | 215 M | 2.99% | 15.3 M |
| `gen_keys` | 193 M | 2.68% | 4.5 M |
| GC, all together | 789 M | 11.0% | |

Comparison is 20% and the red-black tree another 13%, which is the same split MonPoly has
(`do_compare_val` + `caml_compare` + `Tuple.compare` + `Tuple.compare_cst` + `ml_z_compare` =
19.5%, `Set.bal` + `Set.add` = 5.5%, and on top of that `caml_hash` + `caml_page_table_lookup` =
15% that the verified monitor does not pay at all). Per operation the verified kernel is not
behind. It just does far more of them against memory.

### 20.20 What is left that does not need imperative code

Four things, in the order the profile sizes them. None of them is an imperative refinement; all
four are representation or code-equation work of the kind Sections 7-19 already did.

**1. The `option` in a dense tuple is dead weight -- but not where this first said.** A dense
tuple has type `event_data option list`, and `Dense.thy` already carries

```isabelle
definition dwf_tuple :: "nat ⇒ 'a tuple ⇒ bool" where
  "dwf_tuple k d ⟷ length d = k ∧ (∀i < k. d ! i ≠ None)"
definition dtable where "dtable cs D ⟷ (∀d ∈ D. dwf_tuple (length cs) d)"
```

as an invariant of every dense table, in every theorem of the development, so every element of
every dense tuple is a `Some`. This section first concluded that the wrapper therefore costs two
words of allocation per element and that changing the representation to `event_data list` was the
way to attack the allocation ratio. **That was wrong, and 20.21 measures why**: those `Some` cells
arrive once with the input event and are then shared -- `dproj` and `dmerge` reuse the elements
and allocate only cons cells -- so building dense tuples at all is 5.5% of allocation, and the
`Some` cells are a fraction of that. The type change would have been weeks of work across eleven
theories for a few per cent.

What the option layer does cost, 23.3% of allocation, is its *dictionary and comparator chain*:
`ccompare_optiona`, `ccompare_lista`, `ceq_optiona`, `ceq_lista`, `equal_option`, `equal_list`,
`comparator_option` and `comparator_list`, rebuilt per call in the dense inner loops. That is a
code-equation problem, not a type problem, and 20.21 fixes it.

**2. The red-black tree primitives take the comparator as an argument, so every use of it is an
indirect call.** Section 8's monomorphisation baked `ed_comparator` into the *call sites* --
`rbt_comp_lookup ed_comparator t k` -- but inside `rbt_comp_lookup` the comparator is a
parameter, so OCaml compiles `c k kk` as `caml_apply2` and cannot inline it. The callers of the
57.4 M `caml_apply2` calls are

| caller | calls |
|---|---:|
| `rbt_comp_lookup` | 15.5 M |
| `comparator_option` | 15.3 M |
| `comparator_list` | 15.3 M |
| `rbt_comp_ins` | 6.5 M |
| `fold` | 2.9 M |
| `rbt_comp_del` | 1.7 M |
| `rbt_fold_key` | 0.5 M |

so 24.2 M of them are the tree calling its comparator. Generating specialised copies of the
primitives -- an `ed_rbt_lookup` whose own recursion calls `ed_comparator` directly -- removes
those and lets the comparator's first cases inline. At the measured 8 instructions per
`caml_apply2` that is about 194 M instructions, 2.7%, before any inlining benefit.

**3. The generic comparator chain is still the one mostly used.** `comparator_list` runs 15.1 M
cons steps against `ed_comparator`'s 8.7 M, so most tuple comparisons still go through
`comparator_list (comparator_option comparator_event_data)`: three frames and two indirect calls
per element where the monomorphic one is a single frame and a direct call. The reason is visible
in the generated code -- `ed_comparator` reached `rbt_comp_lookup`, `rbt_comp_insert`,
`rbt_comp_delete` and `rbt_comp_union_with_key`, but `rbt_comp_union_swap_rec`,
`rbt_comp_inter_swap_rec`, `rbt_comp_minus`, `rbt_comp_bulkload`, `rbt_comp_insert_with_key` and
`rbt_comp_inter_with_key` still receive `the (ccompare _A)`. Those are the set operations, which
is what the join and the window structures spend their time in. Finishing Section 8's job over
them is the same ML pass applied to more constants.

**4. The join materialises both operands as lists.** `dgen_join` takes
`csorted_list_of_set A` and `csorted_list_of_set B`, and `gen_keys` -- the in-order traversal that
builds them -- runs 4.5 M times, allocating a cons per key and a pair-and-cons per branch.
MonPoly folds over its sets in place and allocates neither. A fold-based `dgen_join`, which is
what `dprobe_fold` and `dindex_fold_lookup` are already stated in terms of, would drop that.

**The answer to whether one can get ahead without the imperative refinement is that on five of
the six multi-second benchmarks of 20.18 the dense monitor already is**, and the profile says the
remaining row is an allocation problem rather than a dispatch problem. Imperative structures were
the right tool for the Since and Until windows, where the same map is updated in place many times
and the pure version copied a tree per update. They are the wrong tool for the join, where the
tables are built once and read once, and 20.18's timings bear that out. What is left to win is in
the representation, and items 1 to 4 are all provable in HOL.

### 20.21 The dictionaries and the comparator of the dense inner loops

20.20's items 1 and 2, done. Both turned out to be in the generated code rather than in any
definition, and `Dense_Code_Opt.thy` holds them.

**What the profile found.** Allocation was attributed by sampling the callers of `caml_call_gc`
under a 1 Kword nursery -- with OCaml's inline bump-pointer allocation the collector is entered
once per nursery filled, attributed to whichever frame tripped the limit, which samples frames in
proportion to the words they allocate. 1,265 samples on `complex` gave

| | share of allocation |
|---|---:|
| red-black tree node churn (`balance` alone 21.3%) | 34.0% |
| dictionary and composite comparator construction | 23.3% |
| `csorted_list_of_set` materialisation (`gen_keys`) | 6.7% |
| building dense tuples at all (cons cells) | 5.5% |
| parsing and the log lexer | 4.0% |

and the second row had a single cause, visible in the generated code:

```ocaml
let rec dprobe_step_l p rk m y acc =
  (match lookupa ((ccompare_list (ccompare_option ccompare_event_data)),
                  (equal_list (equal_option equal_event_data)))
           m (dproj rk y)
   with None -> acc | Some l -> fold (fun x -> ed_insert (dmerge p x y)) l acc)
```

Four dictionary records, a `Some` and two closures, built for every probed tuple. Section 8
removed exactly this from the kernel's inner loops, but it was written before the dense
representation existed and its list of mapping value types does not contain the one the dense
join index uses, `event_data tuple list`: a key of shared columns mapped to the tuples that agree
on it. `Set.insert` in the same function was already redirected to `ed_insert`; only the mapping
side was missed. Six monomorphised constants at that type close it, and the probe becomes

```ocaml
let rec dprobe_step_l p rk m y acc =
  (match ed_lookup_tuples m (dproj rk y) with None -> acc
   | Some l -> fold (fun x -> ed_insert (dmerge p x y)) l acc)
```

**And the tree now calls the comparator by name.** The primitives of
`Deriving.RBT_Comparator_Impl` take the comparator as an argument, so inside `rbt_comp_lookup` it
is an unknown function and OCaml compiles `c k x` as a generic two-argument application --
15.5 M `caml_apply2` calls from the lookup, 6.5 M from the insertion, 1.7 M from the deletion, out
of 57.4 M. Specialising each on `ed_comparator` gives

```ocaml
let rec ed_rbt_lookup x0 k = match x0, k with Empty, k -> None
  | Branch (cc, l, x, y, r), k ->
      (match ed_comparator k x with Eqa -> Some y | Lt -> ed_rbt_lookup l k
        | Gt -> ed_rbt_lookup r k)
```

Nothing is proved about the trees: each copy is the partial application by definition and its code
equations are the originals' with the argument substituted, so the `code_unfold` rules only choose
which of two equal functions the generated code names.

**What it costs to run.** Callgrind on `complex`, 2.1 MB log, against the same measurement in
20.19:

| | MonPoly | before | after |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| instructions | 6.876 G | 7.194 G | **6.216 G** |
| `caml_apply*` | 95 M | 490 M | **123 M** |
| `comparator_list` | -- | 354 M | 75 M |
| `comparator_option` | -- | 215 M | 45 M |
| `ed_comparator` | -- | 296 M | 599 M |
| GC | 445 M | 789 M | 726 M |
| D1 misses | 40.2 M | 95.1 M | 87.7 M |
| ... of which writes | 17.8 M | 56.8 M | 50.2 M |
| minor words allocated | 132.9 M | 423.8 M | **372.9 M** |
| promoted words | 5.56 M | 13.00 M | 11.80 M |

Instructions fall 13.6%, of which three quarters is the generic application path collapsing into
`ed_comparator` -- the work does not vanish, it moves into one frame with a direct call, which is
why `ed_comparator` doubles while the two composite frames lose four fifths of their cost.
Allocation falls 12% and the write misses 12%. Discounting the parts that are not monitoring, the
verified monitor now takes 1.03x MonPoly's instructions where 20.19 measured 1.19x.

**Timings**, nine repetitions, medians, 8.6 MB log, with 20.18's figures for comparison:

| formula | MonPoly | pure | dense | dense+h | 20.18 dense | 20.18 dense+h |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| sincewide | 3.17s | 1.09x | **0.92x** | 0.91x | 0.93x | 0.95x |
| untilwide | 3.04s | 1.20x | **0.99x** | 0.99x | 0.99x | 1.01x |
| aggwide | 11.39s | 1.08x | 0.85x | **0.84x** | 0.80x | 0.80x |
| once | 2.37s | 1.17x | 0.78x | **0.77x** | 0.76x | 0.77x |
| eventually | 1.65s | 1.23x | **0.84x** | 0.87x | 0.88x | 0.88x |
| complex | 2.90s | 1.89x | 1.15x | **0.96x** | 1.25x | 1.10x |

The row that moved is `complex`, from 1.25x to 1.15x dense and 1.10x to 0.96x with the heap index.
That is the right row and the only one that should have moved -- it is the one benchmark with a
genuine multi-way join on wide operands, so the only one where `dprobe_step_l` and `dindex_step`
run millions of times.

**These wall-clock figures are not trustworthy to the precision they are quoted at, and 20.23
re-measures them.** They are medians of nine, but each configuration was timed in a block rather
than interleaved with the others, so drift over a block biases the comparison; and this machine's
spread on a single configuration is 9-21%. MonPoly's own absolute time on `complex` moved from
2.90 s to 2.41 s between two runs of the same binary. On the protocol of 20.23 `complex` reads
1.02x rather than 0.96x, so the claim this section originally made -- that the verified monitor is
faster than MonPoly on all six -- was not supported by its own measurement. The instruction
counts above are exact and stand.

The exported OCaml is 26 lines longer, both cram suites are green, and both differential checks
