still agree on all 537 `-verified` invocations: `-dense` against the plain kernel and
`-dense -heap_maps` against `-dense`.

**Left from 20.20.** Item 3, the set operations -- `rbt_comp_union_swap_rec`,
`rbt_comp_inter_swap_rec`, `rbt_comp_minus`, `rbt_comp_bulkload` and the `_with_key` variants --
still receive `the (ccompare _A)` rather than `ed_comparator`, because Section 8 boxed them
instead of monomorphising them and a box builds the dictionary once but still passes it. One
`rbt_comp_union_with_key ed_comparator` site remains unspecialised for the same reason; it does
not appear among the `caml_apply2` callers, so it was left rather than spend a build and a
revalidation on it. Item 4, the join materialising both operands as lists, is untouched: `gen_keys`
is still 6.7% of allocation. And the largest row of all, the 34% that is red-black tree node
churn, is the price of a persistent tree -- MonPoly pays `caml_hash` and
`caml_page_table_lookup` for 15% of its instructions precisely because it uses mutable hash
tables there instead. That one is not a representation problem, and it is what the imperative
window structures of Sections 20-21 are for.

### 20.22 Could the interface not have the `Some`s at all?

A design note, not a change. 20.21 established that the option wrapper's *allocation* is not where
the money is; this asks the other half of the question -- whether the wrapper could be absent from
the interface in the first place, so that it costs neither residency nor a dereference.

**Where the wrappers come from.** `convert_tuple` in `src/verified_adapter.ml` builds one `Some`
per field of every input event, because the interface is typed

```ocaml
type db = ((string * nat), (((event_data option) list) set list)) mapping
```

and the tuples it puts there are total -- a log event has a value in every argument position, so
no `None` is ever built at the interface. The wrappers then persist, because of this:

```isabelle
| "deval j ts db (DPredS e ar) =
     ((case Mapping.lookup db (e, ar) of
         None ⇒ replicate (length ts) empty_table | Some xs ⇒ xs), DPredS e ar)"
```

**For a simple predicate the dense monitor uses the input table verbatim.** So the adapter's
tuples *are* the dense tuples, and every element of every dense table in the whole pipeline is a
two-word `Some` block pointing at the `event_data`. That is also the dense representation's best
trick at predicates -- the sparse monitor has to build a widened `wf_tuple` per input tuple in the
`Simple` case (`simple_match n 0 tms`), where the dense one does nothing at all.

**What it would buy.** A microbenchmark of the comparator alone, on resident tuples, no
allocation in the measured loop, `ed_comparator`'s exact clause structure against the same
comparator over bare elements:

| tuple width | flat / boxed |
|---:|---:|
| 2 | 1.02x |
| 3 | 0.81x |
| 5 | 0.75x |

At width two the comparison usually resolves on the first element and the second box is never
touched; from width three on, removing it is worth 19-25% of the comparator. Comparison is
`ed_comparator` 9.6% plus `comparator_event_data` 9.2% plus the remaining composite 1.9%, so about
20% of the dense monitor's instructions, which puts the direct saving at 4-5% of instructions.
On top of that, two words per element leave the resident tables, which is the other half of
20.19's read-miss gap -- 37.4 M against MonPoly's 22.4 M, where MonPoly's tuple has one box per
element and ours has two.

**Why the adapter cannot do it alone, and what it would take.** The `db` type is dictated by the
kernel's tuple type, so an unboxed adapter needs an unboxed dense table, which needs the dense
element type to change. Three points make that cheaper than it sounds:

- The dense *operations* do not look at the option. `dproj`, `dmerge`, `dembedp`, the join, the
  index and the probe are all definable over a plain element type; `Dense_Match.thy`, 729 lines,
  mentions `Some` and `None` not once. Generalising them from `'a tuple` to a bare list is a type
  generalisation, and instantiating the element type at `event_data option` recovers exactly
  today's behaviour, so the generalisation can be checked before anything is retyped.
- What does look at the option is the boundary: `dwf_tuple`, `dtabc`, `dtabw`, `dwiden`,
  `dnarrow` and the `dsim` relation. Those stay astride the two representations, which is where a
  conversion belongs and where `DPredM` already performs one (`dmatch_table`).
- **The sparse monitor would barely notice.** Of its three predicate modes, only `Copy` uses the
  input table as it stands and would need a wrapping pass; `Simple` and `Match` already construct
  their tuples (`simple_match`, `match`) and would construct them from a bare list at the same
  cost. `Copy` is the mode for a predicate whose arguments are exactly `Var 0 .. Var (n-1)` at the
  formula's full width, which is the rare case -- narrower occurrences are `Simple`.

So the shape of it is: generalise the dense operations' element type, retype the dense tables,
change `database` and the adapter, and wrap in `meval`'s `Copy` case. The parts that are not
mechanical are `meval`'s predicate case in the base session and its correctness proof, and the
`dsim` bridge. It is a bigger step than 20.21's two, and unlike them it changes a definition
rather than a code equation, so the differential tests stop being the only thing that has to
agree -- the transferred correctness theorems have to be re-proved across the new boundary.

**Addendum: could it be done in the OCaml only, in the adapter?** The suggestion is the right
instinct about where the cost is introduced, and the representation it wants is sound and free:
every @{text event_data} value is a block -- @{text EInt}, @{text EFloat} and @{text EString} all
carry an argument -- so `None` can be the immediate `0` and `Some x` can be `x` itself, with
`Obj.is_int` deciding between them at no cost. That is the same class of trusted adaptation the
development already uses for `ihash`, `ibkt` and the two configuration flags.

It still cannot be done in the OCaml alone, and the reason is one line of `Monomorphic_Code`:

```isabelle
lemma ccompare_etuple: "CCOMPARE(event_data tuple) = Some ed_comparator"
```

`ed_comparator` *is* the comparator instance for `event_data tuple`, so it is the single function
that every tuple set and every tuple-keyed mapping compares with -- in the dense monitor and in
the sparse one alike, down to the Until deque's nested mapping. A dense table never contains a
`None`, but a sparse table must, so one runtime representation cannot serve both: under the
sentinel convention `ed_comparator`'s `Some a :: xs` pattern would match a bare `EInt z` (both are
tag-0 blocks with one field), bind `a` to the `Z.t`, and then compare it as an `event_data`.
Replacing `ed_comparator` with a hand-written unboxed version fixes the dense monitor and breaks
the sparse one.

The same holds for the rest of the element inspectors. In the generated kernel they are
`ed_comparator`, `oeq`, `teq`, `teq_proj`, the `comparator_option` and `equality_option` instances
at `event_data`, the sparse tuple machinery `join1`, `matcha`, `simple_match`, `proj_tuple`,
`proj_tuple_chk` and `restrict_aux`, and the dense/sparse boundary `dtabc`, `dwiden`, `dnarrow`
and `dembedp` -- about fifteen constants, each of which would need its OCaml written by hand.
That is a tractable number, and `Obj.magic` would remove the compiler's help in checking it, so a
single missed inspector would misread data rather than fail to compile; but the blocker is not the
count. It is that the dense and the sparse monitor share the type, and therefore share all
fifteen.

So the adapter can only stop building the wrappers once the dense tables have an element type of
their own, which is step 1 above: generalise the dense operations in their element type. That step
is worth doing first on its own account, because instantiating the generalisation at
`event_data option` has to reproduce today's kernel exactly, which makes it checkable before
anything is committed to the new representation -- and once it is done, the adapter change the
suggestion asks for is the easy end of the job rather than the impossible one.

### 20.23 Item 3, and how the timings were being mismeasured

20.20's item 3: the set operations were reaching the red-black tree with the composite comparator
rather than @{const ed_comparator}. Two rounds were needed, and the first was wrong in a way the
generated code showed plainly.

`Monomorphic_Code` *boxes* the set operations and monomorphises only membership and lookup. A box
builds the dictionary once at start-up, which removes the construction cost, but it still passes
the dictionary, so the comparator that reaches the tree is the three-frame composite.
Monomorphising union, intersection, difference, `Set.filter` and `image` instead -- after removing
the boxes' redirect rules from the code preprocessor, or the two compete for the same term --
fixes their type and lets `ccomp_etuple` rewrite the comparator. That alone achieved nothing:

```ocaml
| RBT_set rbt1, RBT_set rbt2 ->
    RBT_set (joina (ccompare_list (ccompare_option ccompare_event_data))
               (fun _ _ -> id) rbt1 rbt2)
```

Their tree-to-tree branches, which are the hot ones, call `joina`, `meet`, `minus` and
`inter_list` of `RBT_Mapping2` and `RBT_Set2`, and those take the comparator as an argument
exactly as `rbt_comp_lookup` did before 20.21. So the monomorphisation only moved the dictionary
build out of the box and into the branch -- a small regression for those branches and no gain at
all on the comparator. Monomorphising the four primitives as well gives

```ocaml
| RBT_set rbt1, RBT_set rbt2 -> RBT_set (ed_rbt_join_unit (fun _ _ -> id) rbt1 rbt2)

let rec ed_rbt_join_unit xc xd xe =
  Mapping_RBTa (rbt_comp_union_with_key ed_comparator xc
                  (unbox ed_impl_of_v xd) (unbox ed_impl_of_v xe))
```

**A leak 20.21 introduced, found while profiling for this.** Monomorphising `Mapping.lookup`
pushed the per-call dictionary build one frame down into `impl_of`, which ignores the comparator
its caller hands it. `Monomorphic_Code` boxes `impl_of` at every value type for that reason; the
index's was missing, so `ed_rbt_lookup_tuples` called `ccompare_lista` 2.16 million times and
`ed_rbt_insert_tuples` another 0.1 million -- 144 M instructions between
`ccompare_optiona` and `ccompare_lista`, more than item 3 itself was worth. Boxed now.

**Instructions and memory**, callgrind on `complex`, the three stages against the 20.19 baseline:

| | MonPoly | 20.19 | after 1+2 | after 1+2+3 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| instructions | 6.876 G | 7.194 G | 6.216 G | **5.972 G** |
| ... `-dense -heap_maps` | -- | -- | -- | **5.914 G** |
| D1 misses | 40.2 M | 95.1 M | 87.7 M | **80.8 M** |
| ... of which writes | 17.8 M | 56.8 M | 50.2 M | **44.4 M** |
| minor words | 132.9 M | 423.8 M | 372.9 M | **327.7 M** |
| promoted words | 5.56 M | 13.00 M | 11.80 M | **10.66 M** |

Instructions fall 17.0% from the baseline, allocation 22.7%, write misses 21.8%. Discounting the
parts that are not monitoring -- MonPoly spends 817 M printing through `printf` where the direct
printer spends 2 M, and both lex the log for 125 M -- the monitoring work is 5.934 G for MonPoly
against 5.845 G dense and 5.787 G with the heap index, or **0.98x**, where 20.19 measured 1.19x.
Allocation is still 2.47x MonPoly's, down from 3.19x, and that is what is left of the gap.

**How the timings were being mismeasured.** The tables of 20.18 and 20.21 are medians of nine, but
each configuration was timed in a block rather than interleaved, so drift over a block biases the
comparison between them. Interleaving the four configurations run by run on `complex` shows why
that matters:

```
MonPoly    2.903s  [2.758-3.032]  spread  9.4%
pure       5.352s  [4.785-5.719]  spread 17.5%
dense      3.225s  [3.060-3.376]  spread  9.8%
dense+h    3.069s  [2.620-3.281]  spread 21.5%
```

A 9-21% spread on one configuration is wider than most of the differences those sections quote to
three significant figures, and MonPoly's own absolute time on `complex` moved from 2.90 s to
2.41 s between two runs of the same binary. **So the single-digit movements in 20.18 and 20.21 are
at or below this machine's noise floor and should not be read as results**; the instruction counts,
which are exact, are what those sections' conclusions should rest on.

Re-measured properly -- the four configurations interleaved run by run, minimum of nine, which is
the robust estimator when the noise is one-sided:

| formula | MonPoly | pure | dense | dense+h |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| sincewide | 2.70s | 1.04x | 0.90x | **0.89x** |
| untilwide | 2.78s | 1.13x | 0.99x | **0.92x** |
| aggwide | 10.51s | 1.05x | 0.89x | **0.77x** |
| once | 2.01s | 1.16x | 0.77x | **0.74x** |
| eventually | 1.30s | 1.28x | **0.88x** | 0.96x |
| complex | 2.54s | 1.89x | 1.06x | **1.02x** |

**The verified monitor is ahead of MonPoly on five of the six and level on the sixth.** That is
the claim the measurement supports; 20.21's stronger one is withdrawn above. The short tuples are
worth 1.04x to 1.89x over the plain verified kernel, and the heap index, which 20.18 found no
faster than the pure dense code, is now the best configuration on five of six -- the dictionary
and comparator work it used to carry per probed tuple is what had been hiding it.

The exported OCaml is unchanged in behaviour: both cram suites are green and both differential
checks agree on all 537 `-verified` invocations, `-dense` against the plain kernel and
`-dense -heap_maps` against `-dense`.

**Left.** `rbt_comp_union_with_key`, `rbt_comp_minus` and `rbt_comp_inter_with_key` still take
`ed_comparator` as an argument, so inside them it is applied through `caml_apply2` -- the same
thing 20.21 fixed for lookup, insertion and deletion, and worth the same treatment, since a union
walks both trees and so makes O(n) indirect calls. Item 4 of 20.20, the join materialising both
operands as lists, is untouched: `gen_keys` is still 193 M instructions and 6.7% of allocation.
And the 34% of allocation that is red-black tree node churn is unchanged, because it is the price
of a persistent tree rather than anything the code generator is doing.

### 20.24 The option is load-bearing after all: the Since and Until embedding

Step 1 of 20.22 was started and stopped. The pilot worked and the premise did not.

**The pilot worked.** Generalising an option-agnostic dense operation in its element type is a
transparent retyping, not a rewrite: changing

```isabelle
fun dproj :: "bool list ⇒ 'a tuple ⇒ 'a tuple"   to   fun dproj :: "bool list ⇒ 'b list ⇒ 'b list"
```

leaves `Dense.thy` green -- 5369 commands, no errors and no new failures -- because every
existing theorem about `dproj` at `'a tuple` is an instance of the more general one. That is what
step 1 predicted, and it would have carried `dmerge`, `djoin1`, `dtabc`, `dsemi`, `dnarrow`,
`dauxlist` and the rest with it.

**The premise did not.** 20.22 said, quoting `dwf_tuple` and `dtable`, that every element of every
dense tuple is a `Some`. That is true of a table whose column set matches its subformula's free
variables. It is false of the left operand of a Since or an Until whose columns are a proper
subset, which is embedded into the wider set:

```isabelle
definition dembed1 :: "nat option list ⇒ 'a tuple ⇒ 'a tuple" where
  "dembed1 p d = map (λq. case q of None ⇒ None | Some i ⇒ d ! i) p"
```

`DsSince` has `set cs_L ⊆ set cs` and `pl = emb_plan cs cs_L`, the compiler emits
`if cs_L = cs then None else Some (emb_plan cs cs_L)`, and `deval` applies `dembedp pl` to the
left operand's tables before they reach the buffer:

```isabelle
meval_since dags [] (mbuf2S_add (map (dembedp pl) xs) ys ts buf) aux
```

So at every Since or Until whose left operand is narrower than the node -- `B(x) SINCE A(x,y)`
and anything like it -- the dense tables that go into the buffer and into the auxiliary structure
carry a `None` at each column the left operand does not have. They are stored there, and they are
compared with `ed_comparator`, so the holes are read. The `option` is not dead weight on that
path; it is what the embedding is made of. 20.15's whole point was that emitting a plan rather
than the identity is what made the general Since and Until pay off, so this is the common case
for exactly the operators that section was about.

**What that costs the plan.** Unboxing the dense representation is therefore not a retyping plus a
boundary conversion. It needs the embedding replaced, and the natural replacement is not to embed
at all: keep the left operand at `cs_L` and let the Since and Until project onto the common
columns as they go, which the structure is already parameterised for -- it carries
`dargs cs cs_L args` and `dsince_rel n cs cs_L args`. But that is an algorithmic change to the two
operators and their auxiliary structures, with their correctness re-proved, not a change of
representation. It is a larger piece of work than 20.22 estimated, and it should be costed on its
own rather than as a step towards the unboxing.

The `dproj` generalisation is reverted, since on its own it buys nothing; what is worth keeping is
the measurement that it is free, which is recorded here.

### 20.25 What getting rid of `DPure` would take

`DPure` is the dense evaluator's escape hatch:

```isabelle
| "deval j ts db (DPure φ m msk) =
     (let (xs, φ') = meval j m ts db φ in (map (dnarrow msk) xs, DPure φ' m msk))"
```

It runs the kernel's own evaluator on a subformula and narrows the result. Removing it means the
dense fragment has to be total, and that is two jobs, not one.

**Ten constructors have no dense case at all.** The `mformula` datatype has 23; `dof` handles 13
and sends the rest to the catch-all `dof n cs φ = DPure φ n (wide_mask n cs)`. In rough order of
size: `MTP` and `MTS` produce a one-column table from the time point or the timestamp and are
small; `MAndAssign` is an image under an assignment and has a template in `DAndRel`'s
`reindex_constraint`; `MFrz` is a two-formula construct with its own state; `MLet` and `MLetPast`
bind a table into `db`, so a dense body's table has to be widened back or `db` has to carry dense
tables, which is where this meets the interface question; `MTrigger` and `MAndTrigger` need a dense
counterpart of the `mmtaux` structure, for which `Dense_Agg.thy`'s fused trigger work is a start;
`MMatchP` and `MMatchF` need the regex matcher and its auxiliary structure, and are the largest.

**Eight guards on the covered constructors send the general case to `DPure` too** -- `MPred` when
neither `Copy` nor `Simple` nor `Match` applies, `MAndRel` when the constraint's variables escape
the column set, `MNeg` when `cs ≠ []`, `MAnd` when the merge does not match, `MAnds` when the
positive list is empty or the lengths disagree, `MAgg` on the column shape, and `MSince` and
`MUntil` on the argument record. **Most of these are dischargeable rather than cases to
implement**, because `dof` is total on `mformula` and so was written defensively, while the
formulas that actually reach it are safe. `safe_formula (¬⇩F φ) = (fv φ = {} ∧ safe_formula φ)`, so
a safe negation has no free variables and `cs = []` holds at every reachable `MNeg`; the guard is
provable from the `produces` hypotheses, not a gap. `MPred`'s three modes are exhaustive by
construction in `minit0`, `MAndRel`'s constraint has `fv ψ ⊆ fv φ` from safety, `MAnds`' positive
list is non-empty from safety, and `MAnd` and `MAgg` are of the same kind. The exception that is
real work is `MSince` and `MUntil` with `args_agg args ≠ None`: the kernel fuses an aggregation
into those operators and the dense fragment has no case for it.

**What it would buy.** Coverage, not speed on the current benchmarks: 20.15 already measured zero
subformulas evaluated by the sparse function on every benchmark in its table. Across the two cram
suites, of 302 files carrying formulas, 21 mention `LET`, 24 an assignment, 4 `MATCHF`, 3
`MATCHP`, 1 `TRIGGER` and 1 `LETPAST` -- so on the order of one formula file in six touches a
construct that currently falls back. Those are the formulas the dense monitor does not really
monitor.

**It does not unblock the unboxing.** `DPure` is a boundary, and a boundary is what an unboxed
dense representation wants: it runs `meval`, and instead of narrowing it would narrow and strip the
wrappers in the same pass, at the cost of one traversal of the fallback subformula's output. The
blocker for unboxing is 20.24's embedding. Removing `DPure` and unboxing the representation are
two separate projects, related only in that any operator written for the first should be written
unboxed, so that it is not written twice.

**And that embedding is not a wart, it is how the structure reuse works.** `dargs` sets
`args_n = length cs_R` and `args_L = cpos cs_R (set cs_L)`, so the dense Since runs the *sparse*
`mmsaux` at the dense width with the left operand's columns as a subset of positions, and
`dsince_rel` keeps both buffers at `cs_R`:

```isabelle
dbuf = (map (dtabc n cs_R) xs, map (dtabc n cs_R) ys, tss, sk)
```

That is exactly how the sparse Since works -- `args_L`, `args_R` and `wf_tuple` with holes -- and
reusing it is what made 20.15 cheap. Removing the embedding therefore means a dense-native Since
and Until: the left tables stored at `cs_L`, and the filtering step done with the dense masked
join of `mk_djoin` and `dgen_join`, which is already proved. That is a new auxiliary structure
with its own validity invariant and correctness proof for each of the two operators, and it is the
largest single item in the unboxing.

### 20.26 Coverage and unboxing: the plan, and the first step

20.24 and 20.25 together settle what the two remaining projects are and that they are separate:
**unboxing** (dense tables holding `event_data list` rather than `event_data option list`) and
**coverage** (a dense case for every operator, so `DPure` can go). The instruction is to do both.
This section records the decomposition and the first completed step.

**Unboxing, in four steps.**

1. *Generalise the option-agnostic operations in their element type.* Most of the dense algebra
   never looks inside an element: it permutes, projects, merges and compares positions. Those
   operations can be stated at `'b list` and instantiated at either `event_data option` or
   `event_data`, so that the retyping of the tables in step 4 is a change of instance rather than
   a rewrite of the proofs.
2. *A dense-native Since and Until,* without 20.24's embedding. This is the large item and the
   reason the other three are worth doing first.
3. *Discharge the `MNeg` guard,* which is the second hole source: `unit_table n` is a wide tuple
   of `None`s, and it only exists because `dof` permits `cs ≠ []` at an `MNeg`. 20.25 established
   the guard is provable from safety, so the hole disappears with it.
4. *Retype the dense tables* and place the conversions at the boundary: `dtabc` strips the
   wrappers where a sparse table enters, `dtabw` puts them back where one leaves, `DPure` narrows
   and strips in one pass, and the adapter builds `event_data list` tables directly.

**Coverage, in four steps,** in increasing size: `MTP`, `MTS`, `MAndAssign`; then `MLet`,
`MLetPast`, `MFrz`; then `MTrigger` and `MAndTrigger` with a dense `mmtaux`; then `MMatchP` and
`MMatchF`, the remaining `dof` guards (the real work being `MSince`/`MUntil` with
`args_agg ≠ None`), and the removal of `DPure` and the `DsPure` simulation rule.

#### Step 1 is done

Seven definitions and two auxiliaries in `Dense.thy` now have element-generic types:

```isabelle
fun dproj    :: "bool list ⇒ 'b list ⇒ 'b list"
fun djoin1   :: "mrg list ⇒ 'b list ⇒ 'b list ⇒ 'b list option"
fun dmerge   :: "mrg list ⇒ 'b list ⇒ 'b list ⇒ 'b list"
definition dtabc   :: "nat ⇒ cols ⇒ 'b list set ⇒ 'b list set"
definition dsemi   :: "bool list ⇒ bool ⇒ 'b list set ⇒ 'b list set ⇒ 'b list set"
definition dnarrow :: "bool list ⇒ 'b list set ⇒ 'b list set"
definition dauxlist  :: "nat ⇒ cols ⇒ (ts × 'b list set) list ⇒ (ts × 'b list set) list"
definition dauxlist3 :: "nat ⇒ cols ⇒ (ts × 'b list set × 'b list set) list ⇒
    (ts × 'b list set × 'b list set) list"
```

Deliberately left at `'a tuple`: `dwiden` and `dtabw`, which insert `None`; `dwf_tuple` and
`dtable`, which are about well-formedness of sparse tuples; and `dembed1`, which is 20.24's hole
source and is the thing step 2 removes rather than generalises. `kands_pos` and `kands_neg` stay
too, since they call the sparse `join`.

The gate was not a byte-identical export -- generalising an element type necessarily moves the
element's comparator dictionary from inside the definition to its call sites, so the generated
code *must* differ. The gates were behaviour and performance instead: `MFODL_Monitor_Imp` builds
green (5369 commands in `Dense.thy`, 0 errors, 4:34 for the session), both cram suites pass, and
the differential checker reports 537/537 agreement on three comparisons -- dense against pure,
dense+heap against dense, and the new binary against the pre-generalisation oracle binary of
20.24. Performance moved slightly the right way on `complex`: instructions
5,971,876,295 → 5,908,597,394 and minor words allocated 327,660,658 → 317,338,818, the second
because a few dictionary projections that were being rebuilt per call are now shared at the call
site.

#### Step 2, scoped

The specification for a narrow-input Since filter already exists. `dsemi_join` in `Dense.thy`
says, read with `cs1 = cs_R` and `cs2 = cs_L`:

```isabelle
dsemi (mrg_left_key (mrg_plan cs_R cs_L)) pos (dtabc n cs_R T) (dtabc n cs_L T')
  = dtabc n cs_R (join T pos T')
```

-- the masked semi-join of a dense window against a **narrow, hole-free** left table equals the
dense image of the sparse `join`. So correctness of a dense-native filtering step is in hand and
no new specification is needed.

What is missing is efficiency. The operation the Since structure actually performs is
`join_mmsaux`, whose validity lemma `valid_join_mmasaux_unfolded` requires
`table (args_n args) (args_L args) rel1` -- the wide, hole-carrying form -- and whose
implementation consults the structure's index on the join columns so that it does not rescan the
window. A rescan specified by `dsemi_join` would be correct and slower. The work is therefore a
*narrow-input variant of `join_mmsaux`*: one operation rather than a new structure, pushing the
mask `cpos cs_R (set cs_L)` into the internals so the index is still used, with its validity
lemma restated through `dsemi_join`. `join_mmsaux` lives in `Optimized_Since.thy` in the
base session, so this is a base-session change and each iteration costs a ~52-minute rebuild of
`MFODL_Monitor_Devel` plus ~5 minutes for the child.

### 20.27 Coverage, step 1: the assignment, the time point and the timestamp

20.25's inventory said ten of the kernel's twenty-three `mformula` constructors have no dense
case. Three of them are small and share a shape -- each produces or extends a table by
arithmetic on one tuple at a time -- so they go first, and they also settle what a coverage step
consists of.

**The short forms.** The kernel's assignment fills the hole at the assigned variable's position,
`eval_assignment (x, t) y = y[x := Some (meval_trm t y)]`. A short tuple has no hole to fill, so
the value is *inserted* at the position the new column occupies in the merged column list -- the
same insertion the aggregation's output already does, at the same `ins_pos`:

```isabelle
definition dassign1 :: "nat ⇒ Formula.trm ⇒ event_data tuple ⇒ event_data tuple" where
  "dassign1 p t d = take p d @ Some (meval_trm t d) # drop p d"

theorem dassign_eval_assignment:
  assumes "sorted_wrt (<) cs" and "∀c ∈ set cs. c < n" and "y < n" and "y ∉ set cs"
    and "Formula.fv_trm t ⊆ set cs" and "table n (set cs) T"
  shows "dassign (ins_pos cs y) (reindex_trm cs t) (dtabc n cs T)
       = dtabc n (merge_cols cs [y]) (eval_assignment (y, t) ` T)"
```

The two nodes that turn a number into a table are even shorter: a one-column table holding the
number, or a zero-column truth value, so no frame-wide tuple is built at all.

```isabelle
fun dmtrm :: "mtrm ⇒ event_data ⇒ event_data table" where
  "dmtrm (MVar v) x = {[Some x]}"
| "dmtrm (MConst c) x = (if c = x then unit_table 0 else empty_table)"

theorem dtabc_eval_mtrm:
  "∀c ∈ set (mtrm_dcols mt). c < n ⟹
     dtabc n (mtrm_dcols mt) (eval_mtrm n mt x) = dmtrm mt x"
```

**What a coverage step costs.** Six places, in a fixed order: the dense operation and its
agreement theorem in `Dense.thy`; the constructor and its `deval` case; the `dsim` rule; the case
of `dsim_deval`; the `dof` case and its reachability lemma, plus the corresponding case of
`dsim_dof_minit0`; and the mirror in the executable monadic fragment of `Dense_Imp.thy`, which is
what the binary runs. `produces` needs nothing: the coinductive invariant was already proved for
every constructor, `produces_MAndAssign`, `produces_MTP` and `produces_MTS` included, so the
input well-formedness a dense operation needs is in hand at a node the moment the node exists.
Two of the three `dof` guards turned out to be discharged for free by the reachability lemma:
`split_assignment_props` says a safe assignment's split names a variable outside the operand's
free variables whose term reads only those, which is exactly what `dassign_eval_assignment` asks
for.

The operand's column list is the node's without the assigned column, so `rem_col cs y` (the
filter) and `merge_rem_col` (putting it back is the merge the theorem wants) were needed, and
`set_cols_eqI` -- a sorted distinct list with the right element set *is* `set_cols` of it --
which is the small fact any further column bookkeeping will want too.

**Measured coverage.** The kernel reports its fallback-node count under `MONPOLY_DENSE_STATS`,
which makes coverage measurable rather than asserted. Over both cram suites, counting every
`-verified` invocation:

| | invocations | with a fallback node | fallback nodes |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| before | 90 | 86 | 87 |
| after | 108 | 86 | 87 |

The 18 extra invocations are the new test (below) and they all compile to zero fallback nodes.
The 86 that remain are almost all `FRZ` -- one node wrapping the whole formula -- plus four
`LET`. **So this step's gain does not show up on the existing suite at all**: every assignment
and every `tp`/`ts` in it sits inside a `FRZ` or `LET` subtree that the compiler hands to the
kernel's evaluator whole. That is worth stating plainly, because it is the shape of the
remaining work: `FRZ`, `LET` and `LETPAST` are where the coverage is, not the leaves.

**A test that exercises them.** Since the suite had no verified formula using an assignment or
`tp`/`ts` outside such a subtree, the differential comparison could not have caught a mistake in
these nodes. `verimon_tests/dense_nodes.t` adds nine formulas over a small log -- assignments
plain, under an existential, chained, and as a Since operand; `tp`, `ts` and `tpts` plain, under
`ONCE`, and under an existential with a constraint -- and runs each with `-verified` and with
`-verified -dense`. Each compiles to zero fallback nodes; before the change each had one or two.

**Validation.** `MFODL_Monitor_Imp` builds green (3:54 on top of the base session); both cram
suites pass; the differential checker reports 555/555 agreement on all three comparisons (dense
against plain, dense+heap against dense, and the new binary against the previous one); and the
benchmark table is unchanged within this machine's spread, as it should be, since no benchmark
formula uses any of the three constructors.

One incidental fix: `dsim_wf_cols` was proved by induction over the simulation, and its `auto`
over nineteen cases took 120 seconds -- most of `Dense_Eval.thy`'s build. Every rule either
carries `wf_cols` as a premise or has `cs = []`, so case analysis suffices, and it now takes
under a second.

### 20.28 Coverage, step 2: the let, and why the freeze needs more

20.27's measurement pointed at `FRZ`, `LET` and `LETPAST`. Of the three, the plain let turns out
to need nothing new at all, and the other two need a change to the dense evaluator's recursion.

**The let needs no conversion.** A let-bound formula satisfies the safety side condition
`{0..<nfv φ} ⊆ fv φ`, and `fv φ ⊆ {0..<nfv φ}` holds for every formula, so `fv φ = {0..<m}` with
`m = nfv φ`: its own frame has *every* column live. Its tables are therefore `table m {0..<m}`
-- full width, no holes -- and a table of that shape is its own dense form, which is
`dtabc_upt`:

```isabelle
lemma dtabc_upt: "table n {0..<n} T ⟹ dtabc n [0..<n] T = T"
```

So the dense let evaluates its bound formula densely at `cs = [0..<m]`, gets back exactly the
tables the kernel's evaluator would have produced, and puts them into the database unchanged.
The database well-formedness the dense predicate nodes rely on -- `wf_db`, which asks for
`table ar {0..<ar}` and finiteness -- is preserved by that update, which is what
`wf_db_update'` says. There is no conversion at the binding, in either direction.

```isabelle
| "deval j ts db (DLet p m φ ψ) =
     (let (xs, φ') = deval j ts db φ;
          (ys, ψ') = deval j ts (Mapping.update (p, m) xs db) ψ
      in (ys, DLet p m φ' ψ'))"

| DsLet: "wf_cols n cs ⟹ produces n (set cs) tw (mformula.MLet p m φ ψ) ⟹
    dsim m [0..<m] tw φd φ ⟹ dsim n cs tw ψd ψ ⟹
    dsim n cs tw (DLet p m φd ψd) (mformula.MLet p m φ ψ)"
```

`dof` emits it unconditionally -- no guard to discharge.

**The freeze and the recursive let need the evaluator to be a `function`.** Both re-enter the
evaluator on a formula that is not a syntactic subterm of the node: the freeze runs its body
once per frozen tuple,

```isabelle
freeze_meval0 (λα. meval n [] (Mapping.update (p, m) (replicate j α)
                     (Mapping.map_values (λ_ _. []) db)) ψ) (jfrz - jpsi0) αs
```

where the `ψ` inside the closure is the body's state *after* this batch, and the recursive let
feeds its body its own previous output until a fixpoint. The kernel handles this by defining
`meval` with `function (sequential)` and a termination measure on the formula's size, with
`psize_snd_meval` -- evaluation preserves size -- as the fact that makes the measure decrease.
`deval` is currently a plain `fun` whose recursion is structural, so covering either node means
mirroring that construction: `function (sequential)`, a size-preservation lemma by partial
induction, and termination by the size measure. That is the same work the kernel already did,
and it is shared between the two nodes, which is why they are one step rather than two.

Good news for both: `freeze_meval0` and `letpast_meval0` are polymorphic in the evaluator and
in the state type, so the dense side reuses them rather than defining counterparts; only a
relation lemma (dense output equals the narrowing of the sparse output, and the recursions take
the same branches) is needed. For the recursive let that lemma also has to show the two
size guards agree, which is where `psize_snd_deval` is used a second time.

#### What the freeze actually costs, measured by trying it

The evaluator side of that is done and works: `deval` becomes
`function (sequential)` with

```isabelle
lemma psize_snd_deval:
  "deval_deval_list_dom (Inl (j, ts, db, φ)) ⟹ size (snd (deval j ts db φ)) = size φ"
  "deval_deval_list_dom (Inr (j, ts, db, L)) ⟹
     map (size ∘ snd) (deval_list j ts db L) = map size L"
```

proved by partial induction in one `auto` plus two one-line cases, and termination by the sum
measure `size` / `size_list size`. The `DFrz` equation mirrors the kernel's clause verbatim,
reusing `freeze_meval0` itself because it is polymorphic in the evaluator and the state.

**The obstacle is not there; it is in the simulation proof's induction principle.** The freeze's
closure evaluates the body at its state *after* the current batch, `ψ'`, and the simulation
theorem is proved `by (induct rule: dsim.induct)` -- an induction over the derivation of
`dsim n cs tw φd φ`. That gives the statement for the operands' own derivations, so for `ψd`
and `ψ`; it does not give it for `ψd'` and `ψ'`, which are related by a *new* derivation the
body's induction hypothesis produces, not by a sub-derivation. So the freeze case cannot be
closed in that induction at all.

The fix is the same one the kernel needed for the same reason: induct on the formula's `size`
rather than on the derivation, with `size ψ' = size ψ < size (MFrz …)` supplying the decrease.
Concretely, `dsim_deval` takes an extra premise `size φ ≤ N`, every one of its fifteen
induction-hypothesis uses gains that obligation, the multi-way conjunction's list lemma has to
thread it too, and an outer `less_induct` on `size φ` discharges the freeze case from the
strengthened hypothesis. That is a mechanical but wide edit of a six-hundred-line proof, so it
is its own step rather than a rider on this one; the evaluator-side work above is kept as a
patch rather than committed, since a `dformula` constructor no rule can build is worse than
none.

The same induction principle is what the recursive let needs, and its size guard
(`letpast_meval0` gives up when evaluation changes the formula's size) needs `psize_snd_deval`
a second time, to show the dense and sparse recursions take the same branch. So the two nodes
share both pieces of work, which is the argument for doing them together and after the
induction is converted.

### 20.29 The freeze, in the verified fragment

20.28 said the freeze's obstacle was the simulation proof's induction principle, not the
evaluator. Both halves are now done, and the diagnosis held.

**The induction.** `dsim_deval` was proved `by (induct rule: dsim.induct)`, which hands out the
statement for an operand's *own* derivation. The freeze evaluates its body once per frozen
tuple at the body's state after the current batch, related by a derivation the body's induction
hypothesis produces rather than a sub-derivation, so that case could not be closed at all. The
theorem now carries a bound and an assumption below it,

```isabelle
theorem dsim_deval_N:
  assumes Q: "⋀… χ …. size χ < NN ⟹ dsim … χ ⟹ … ⟹ ∃φd''. …"
  shows "dsim n cs tw φd φ ⟹ … ⟹ size φ ≤ NN ⟹ ∃φd'. …"
```

`dsim_deval_bound` discharges `Q` by induction on the bound and `dsim_deval` instantiates it at
the formula's own size, so what downstream theories use is unchanged. Each of the twelve
induction-hypothesis uses gained one line, `size <operand> ≤ NN` by `order_trans` and `simp`,
and the multi-way conjunction gets it per operand from `size_list_estimation'`. This is the
same construction the kernel needs for `meval`, for the same reason.

**The evaluator.** `deval` becomes `function (sequential)`, with

```isabelle
lemma psize_snd_deval:
  "deval_deval_list_dom (Inl (j, ts, db, φ)) ⟹ size (snd (deval j ts db φ)) = size φ"
  "deval_deval_list_dom (Inr (j, ts, db, L)) ⟹
     map (size ∘ snd) (deval_list j ts db L) = map size L"
```

by partial induction, and termination by the sum measure `size` / `size_list size`.
`freeze_meval0` is polymorphic in the evaluator and in the state it throws away, so the dense
side reuses it rather than defining a counterpart; `freeze_meval0_rel` says the two runs take
the same branches -- narrowing a list preserves its length, so `drop k` is empty on one side
exactly when it is on the other -- and `freeze_meval0_sub` that the frozen tuples left over come
from the ones handed in, which is what re-establishes the node's own invariant.

The simulation case is where the size induction earns its keep: the body agreement the freeze
needs comes from `Q` at `size ψ2 < NN`, with `size ψ2 = size ψ` supplied by the kernel's own
`size_snd_meval`. `dof` emits `DFrz` unconditionally, and `dsim_dof_minit0_Frz` reads
`fv φ = {0..<nfv φ}` off the safety side condition exactly as the let's lemma does.

**What this does and does not buy.** The *verified* dense fragment now covers the freeze; the
*executable* one does not, so the fallback-node count over the cram suites is still 83 and every
remaining node is still a `FRZ`. The reason is specific and worth recording: `Dense_Imp`'s
evaluator lives in the `Heap` monad, and a `fun` termination measure cannot read the resulting
formula out of a monadic computation, so the size argument that works for the pure evaluator
does not transfer. The executable side needs `partial_function (heap)`, which does not support
mutual recursion, so `deval` and `deval_list` have to be merged into one function over a
sum-typed argument first; a fuel parameter is the alternative and is worse, because it needs an
unreachable `fuel = 0` branch that would be a silent correctness cliff in an unverified
evaluator. The monadic freeze itself is easy -- structurally recursive on the frozen-tuple list,
so a plain `fun`.

Session builds green in 2:48; `src/verified.ml` is byte-identical, as it must be, since nothing
that is code-generated changed.

### 20.30 The recursive let, in the verified fragment

`MLetPast` is the last of the three binding nodes. It evaluates its bound formula repeatedly,
feeding each round's output back under the bound predicate until the output dries up or the step
budget runs out, and then evaluates its body against the database that holds the accumulated
output. The rounds are driven by `letpast_meval0`, which -- like `freeze_meval0` before it -- is
polymorphic in the evaluator, so the dense side reuses it rather than defining a counterpart:

```isabelle
| "deval j ts db (DLetPast p m φ ψ i buf) =
     (let (xs, i, buf, φ) =
            letpast_meval0 (deval j) j i [] (list_of_option buf) (p, m) ts db φ;
          (ys, ψ) = deval j ts (Mapping.update (p, m) xs db) ψ
      in (ys, DLetPast p m φ ψ i buf))"
```

`psize_snd_deval` gains exactly one goal from this clause, and the kernel closes the identical
goal for `meval` with `size_letpast_meval`, so the same step works here.

**The rounds agree literally.** The bound formula sits at its own full frame `[0..<m]`, where
`dtabc m [0..<m]` is the identity on well-formed tables, so the dense and the sparse run do not
merely correspond -- they produce the same lists. `letpast_meval0_rel` says so by induction on
`letpast_meval0.induct` instantiated at `eval = meval j m`:

```isabelle
lemma letpast_meval0_rel:
  assumes Q: "⋀tw' χd χ ts' db' zs' χ'. size χ < NN ⟹ dsim m [0..<m] tw' χd χ ⟹ … ⟹
      meval j m ts' db' χ = (zs', χ') ⟹
      ∃χd'. deval j ts' db' χd = (zs', χd') ∧ dsim m [0..<m] (dnext tw' ts') χd' χ'"
  shows "letpast_meval0 (meval j m) J i ys xs q ts db φ = (zs, i', bo, φ') ⟹ … ⟹
    list_all (ftable m {0..<m}) zs ∧ (∀y. bo = Some y ⟶ ftable m {0..<m} y) ∧
      (∃φd'. letpast_meval0 (deval j) J i ys xs q ts db φd = (zs, i', bo, φd')
        ∧ dsim m [0..<m] (dnext tw ts) φd' φ')"
```

Two things make the induction go through. The guard that rejects a round in which the formula's
size changed never fires -- on the sparse side by `size_snd_meval`, on the dense side by
`size_snd_deval` -- so the two runs take the same branch at every round without a case analysis.
And the rounds re-enter the evaluator on the *successor* state, not on a sub-derivation, which is
exactly what the size induction of 20.29 was introduced for: `Q` at `size φ < NN` supplies the
step, and `size φ' = size φ` keeps the bound as the rounds go.

The ftable conjuncts are folded into this lemma rather than taken from `letpast_meval0_ftable`,
which proves the same invariant for the `produces` side: that lemma lives in `Dense_Let`, which
sits *after* `Dense_Eval` in the import chain. Proving it again costs three lines inside an
induction that already has the facts in hand; moving it would have meant moving `produces_mono`
with it.

**The relation and the compiler.** `DsLetPast` carries one invariant beyond the two operand
simulations: `∀y. buf = Some y ⟶ ftable m {0..<m} y`, the carried-over table from the previous
round. `letpast_meval0_rel` re-establishes it for the successor. `dof` emits `DLetPast`
unconditionally, and `dsim_dof_minit0_LetPast` reads `fv φ = {0..<nfv φ}` off the safety side
condition `{0..<nfv φ} ⊆ fv φ` exactly as the let's and the freeze's lemmas do; at `minit0` the
buffer is `None`, so the new invariant is trivial there.

**Where this leaves coverage.** All three binding nodes -- `MLet`, `MFrz`, `MLetPast` -- are now
in the verified dense fragment, and `DPure` is left for `MTrigger`/`MAndTrigger`,
`MMatchP`/`MMatchF`, and the aggregating `MSince`/`MUntil`. The *executable* fragment still stops
at `MLet`: `Dense_Imp`'s evaluator is monadic, and the size argument that licenses `DFrz` and
`DLetPast` in the pure evaluator cannot be made in the `Heap` monad, as 20.29 records. So the
measured fallback-node count over the cram suites is unchanged, and `src/verified.ml` is again
byte-identical -- nothing code-generated changed.

### 20.31 The trigger, in the verified fragment

The trigger differs from the Since and the Until in where its answer comes from. Those two
accumulate it in the structure and read it out with `result_msaux` / `eval_muaux`, so what has to
transport is an operation. The trigger's structure only stores the auxiliary list; the answer is
`trigger_results` applied to that list, so what has to transport is a set comprehension:

```isabelle
theorem dtabc_trigger_results:
  assumes … and ne: "0 < length (filter (λ(t, _, _). mem (args_ivl args) (cur - t)) auxlist)"
  shows "snd (trigger_results (dargs cs cs_L args) cur (dauxlist3 n cs auxlist))
       = dtabc n cs (snd (trigger_results args cur auxlist))"
```

Its *then* branch puts two conditions on a tuple of the right operand's frame -- membership in a
stored right table, and a join against a stored left table -- and those are exactly the two the
join at `cpos` already transports. `proj_tuple_dproj`, `dproj_inj_sub` and `table_dtabc_cpos`
supply them *pointwise* rather than as an image, which is the only new thing needed;
`wf_tuple_dproj_full` and `wf_tuple_dwiden_full` make the frames correspond in both directions, so
the comprehension can be matched term for term.

The *else* branch transports too, and needs no side condition. I first got this wrong and
assumed it away: it answers `unit_table (args_n args)`, and I read that as a table over no columns
that cannot be narrowed at a non-empty column list. It can. The unit table narrows to the unit
table of the dense frame,

```isabelle
lemma dtabc_unit_table_cols:
  "sorted_wrt (<) cs ⟹ ∀c ∈ set cs. c < n ⟹ dtabc n cs (unit_table n) = unit_table (length cs)"
```

which at the empty column list is `{[]}`. The filter that chooses the branch reads only the
timestamps, and `dauxlist3` leaves those alone, so the two frames take the same branch; the
theorem therefore holds unconditionally, and `dtaux_result` and `dtrigger_step_rel` lost an
assumption each.

On top of that, `dtaux` relates the two structures through the abstract auxiliary list they are
both valid for, with `dtaux_init`, `dtaux_update` and `dtaux_result` in the shape `dsaux` and
`duaux` already have, and `mbuf2T_take_rel` steps the node's buffer traversal at both frames at
once -- `mbuf2t_take_rel_ts` without the timestamp bookkeeping, since the two runs consume the
same number of entries.

**The node.** `DTrigger` carries the arguments at `dargs`, the embedding plan, the two operands,
the two buffers and the pending timestamps; `deval`'s clause is the kernel's, with the left
operand's tables embedded by `dembedp` as the Since's are. `dtrigger_rel` and `dtrigger_step_rel`
give the simulation case its step; `dof` emits `DTrigger` when the arguments fit the frame and the
interval contains zero, and `dsim_dof_minit0_Trigger_fml` covers both shapes the safe trigger's
left operand can take -- safe itself, or a negation whose body is safe. The two differ only in the
polarity recorded in the arguments, which the relation never reads.

That last lemma is used twice: by the `Trigger_0` case of the compiler's induction, and by the
`mem I 0` branch of `And_Trigger`, where `minit0` builds a plain `MAnd` whose right operand *is* a
standalone trigger node. That branch previously went to `DPure`; it does not any more.

**What is left.** `MAndTrigger` -- the fused node `minit0` builds when the interval does not
contain zero -- is still `DPure`, and so are `MMatchP`/`MMatchF` and the aggregating
`MSince`/`MUntil`. The *executable* fragment is untouched, so the measured fallback-node count over
the cram suites is still 83 and `src/verified.ml` is byte-identical. Session builds green in 3:07;
both cram suites pass.

### 20.32 The dense Since and Until, with the probe at the left operand's frame

Section 20.24 established that the `option` in the dense tuple is load-bearing because of the
Since and the Until: their nodes run the verified structure at `dargs` (20.6), where a stored
tuple is hole-free at the right operand's width, but the *probe* -- the left operand's table --
arrives at the left operand's own, narrower frame and is widened tuple by tuple with `dembedp`
before the structure sees it. That is one allocation per left tuple per time point, and it is the
last place where the dense representation puts a hole back into a tuple.

This section builds both structures with the probe taken at the frame the left operand already
produces it at. Everything lives in a new theory, `thys/MFODL_Monitor_Imp/Dense_Aux.thy`, which
imports only `Dense`, so editing it does not make the dense evaluator or the descending compiler
reprocess.

**The mask.** The dense projection the structures need is the one 20.8's dense semi-join already
uses, `mrg_left_key (mrg_plan cs_R cs_L)`. Two lemmas tie it to the frame:

```isabelle
lemma dmsk_args_dargs:
  "sorted_wrt (<) cs_R ⟹ sorted_wrt (<) cs_L ⟹ set cs_L ⊆ set cs_R ⟹
     dmsk_args (dargs cs_R cs_L args) (mrg_left_key (mrg_plan cs_R cs_L))"

lemma dwiden_image_dtabc:
  assumes … and "table n (set cs_L) T"
  shows "dwiden (mrg_left_key (mrg_plan cs_R cs_L)) ` dtabc n cs_L T = dtabc n cs_R T"
```

`dmsk_args args msk` says the mask describes the frame: it has width `args_n args`, every column
belongs to the right operand, and the left operand's columns are the mask's true positions. The
second says that widening the left operand's *own* table by that mask gives exactly the
`cs_R`-narrowing of the wide table -- which is what the evaluator computes today, per tuple, by
embedding. So the two runs are looking at the same table; only one of them materialises it.

**The Since structure.** The state is the verified one with the two indexed tables replaced by
`didx` indices keyed by the dense projection, and the two masks collapsed into the dense mask (at
the dense frame every column is a column of the right operand, so `maskR` is all-true). It is a
ten-tuple rather than eleven:

```isabelle
type_synonym 'a dmsaux = "ts × ts × bool list ×
  (ts × 'a table) queue × (ts × 'a table) queue × 'a table ×
  ('a tuple, 'a table) mapping × ('a tuple, ts) mapping ×
  ('a tuple, 'a table) mapping × ('a tuple, ts) mapping"
```

What makes the whole thing cheap to verify is that *validity is not re-proved*. `dmsaux_of`
recovers the verified state a dense state stands for -- it rebuilds the two indexed tables from
the keys of the mappings they index, which the generated code never does -- and `mmsaux_norm`
does the same to a verified state. `valid_mmsaux` cannot tell the two apart:

```isabelle
lemma valid_mmsaux_norm: "valid_mmsaux args cur aux ys ⟹
    valid_mmsaux args cur (mmsaux_norm args aux) ys"
```

so for every operation but the join it is enough to check that the dense operation agrees with the
verified one on the fields that are not indexed tables, and that the index invariant survives:

```isabelle
lemma dmsaux_of_dshift_end:
  "dmsaux_of args (dshift_end args nt daux)
   = mmsaux_norm args (shift_end args nt (dmsaux_of args daux))"
```

and then `valid_dshift_end` is the verified lemma. The index is maintained incrementally --
`didx_union` for the tables that enter the window, `didx_minus` for the ones that leave -- and,
exactly as the verified structure does with its indexed tables (7d, 13), not maintained at all
where the join will not consult it.

**The join.** This is the point of the exercise. Its three branches are the verified ones read
densely. Without join columns the mask is all-false, the dense projection of every stored tuple is
the empty tuple, and the whole window stands or falls with `[] ∈ Y`. When every column is a join
column the mask is all-true, the dense projection is the identity, and the probe table meets the
stored tables directly. Otherwise the index decides -- and densely a bucket's key *is* what the
probe table holds, so the surviving tuples are the union of the surviving buckets:

```isabelle
      else
        (let in_del = dsemi_idx msk (¬ pos) idx_in Y;
         since_del = dsemi_idx msk (¬ pos) idx_since Y in
         (t, gc, msk, data_prev, data_in, table_in - in_del,
          Mapping.filter (λk _. (k ∈ Y) = pos) idx_in, mapping_delete_set tuple_in in_del,
          Mapping.filter (λk _. (k ∈ Y) = pos) idx_since,
          mapping_delete_set tuple_since since_del))
```

No tuple of `Y` is touched and none is widened; where the verified structure builds a `wf_idx` of
the probe table, the dense one builds nothing, because the probe table already *is* the key set.
That branch also keeps the invariant, by `didx_filter`: restricting the index to the surviving
keys is the index of the surviving tuples, since a bucket passes or fails as a whole.

The correctness statement is that the dense join is the verified join against the widened probe
table:

```isabelle
lemma djoin_msaux_abs:
  assumes "valid_dmsaux args msk cur (…) ys" and "dmsk_args args msk"
    and "⋀y. y ∈ Y ⟹ dwf_tuple (length (filter id msk)) y"
  shows "dmsaux_of args (djoin_msaux args Y (…))
     = join_mmsaux_abs args (dwiden msk ` Y) (dmsaux_of args (…))"
```

`join_mmsaux_abs` is the verified join's own abstract form, which the kernel proves equal to the
optimised one under validity, and which is already in normalised shape. The pointwise step is

```isabelle
lemma proj_tuple_in_join_dense:
  "length as = length msk ⟹ (⋀y. y ∈ Y ⟹ dwf_tuple (length (filter id msk)) y) ⟹
     proj_tuple_in_join pos msk as (dwiden msk ` Y) = ((dproj msk as ∈ Y) = pos)"
```

-- the kernel's projection and the dense one classify a stored tuple identically, the first
against the widened table and the second against the narrow one.

`dsaux'` then repeats 20.6's five lemmas for the dense structure; only `dsaux'_join` reads
differently, taking `dtabc n cs_L rel1` where `dsaux_join` takes `dtabc n cs_R rel1`. And
`deval_since` is the kernel's `eval_since` with the dense operations put in.

**The Until structure.** The Until needed far less. Of its eleven components only two mention the
left operand at all: the mask, and the mapping `a1_map` that records, for each tuple of the left
operand's frame, the last time point at which it held. The window's step, the shift, the
evaluation and the length carry both through untouched -- which is not obvious from the code but
is what the commutation lemmas say:

```isabelle
lemma shift_dq_wkeys: "shift_dq args nt (wkeys_uaux msk d) = wkeys_uaux msk (shift_dq args nt d)"
```

where `wkeys msk` widens a mapping's keys and `nkeys msk` narrows them back. (`eval_step_dq`
itself only commutes when the timestamp queue is non-empty -- it is underdefined otherwise -- so
the commutation for the shift goes through a fold whose length is bounded by that queue, which
`takeWhile_queue_rep_fst` supplies.)

So the dense Until *is* the verified structure, with `a1_map` keyed by the left operand's own
tuples, and only the one operation that reads the left operand changes: where the verified one
looks a stored tuple up by `proj_tuple maskL as`, the dense one looks it up by `dproj msk as`, and
it takes the left operand's table at that frame. The theorem is again that it is the verified
operation against the widened table:

```isabelle
theorem wkeys_uaux_dadd_new_dq:
  assumes "finite rel2" and "fst (lft_of aux) = msk"
    and "⋀k. k ∈ Mapping.keys (snd (lft_of aux)) ⟹ dnarrow_tuple msk k"
    and "⋀y. y ∈ rel1 ⟹ dnarrow_tuple msk y" and "⋀as. as ∈ rel2 ⟹ length as = length msk"
  shows "wkeys_uaux msk (dadd_new_dq args msk rel1 rel2 nt aux)
       = add_new_dq args (dwiden msk ` rel1) rel2 nt (wkeys_uaux msk aux)"
```

`dvalid_muaux` collects the three conditions a dense state has to meet -- the verified state it
stands for is valid, the mask it carries is the frame's, and every key of `a1_map` is a tuple of
the left operand's own width -- and the four operations preserve it. The finiteness hypothesis is
the only one that is not free: `add_new_dq` has a fallback for infinite right-hand tables that the
evaluator never reaches, since the operand tables are `ftable`, i.e. finite by construction.

**The rewiring.** Both structures are now wired into the verified dense evaluator. The two
`dformula` constructors lost their embedding plan and gained the dense state:

```isabelle
  | DSince args dformula dformula "event_data mbuf2S" "event_data dmsaux"
  | DUntil args "bool list" dformula dformula "event_data mbuf2" "ts list" ts
      "event_data mmuaux_dq"
```

The `nat option list` the `DUntil` node used to carry was the embedding plan, applied to every
table the left operand produced; the `bool list` that replaced it is the frame mask `dmsk cs cs_L`,
which is not applied to anything -- it is read by `dadd_new_dq` to key one lookup. `deval`'s two
clauses drop `dembedp` entirely and run `deval_since` and `dadd_new_dq`/`eval_dq` instead of
`meval_since` and `add_new_muaux_dq`/`eval_muaux_dq`, and `dof`'s two clauses build the dense
states with `dinit_msaux (dargs cs cs_L args) (dmsk cs cs_L)` and `init_dq (dargs cs cs_L args)`.

On the relation side the change is that the left buffer stays narrow. Where `dsince_rel` and
`duntil_rel` used to demand

```isabelle
    dbuf = (map (dtabc n cs_R) (fst buf), map (dtabc n cs_R) (snd buf))
```

they now demand `map (dtabc n cs_L)` on the left component: the left operand's tables never reach
the right operand's frame at all, neither in the buffer nor in the structure. What makes that
typecheck against the verified lemmas is `dwiden_image_dtabc`, which says the mask widens the left
frame to the right one exactly as `dtabc` at the wider column list would have:

```isabelle
lemma dwiden_image_dtabc:
  assumes "sorted_wrt (<) cs_R" "sorted_wrt (<) cs_L" "set cs_L ⊆ set cs_R"
    "∀c ∈ set cs_R. c < n" "table n (set cs_L) T"
  shows "dwiden (mrg_left_key (mrg_plan cs_R cs_L)) ` dtabc n cs_L T = dtabc n cs_R T"
```

so every step lemma discharges its verified counterpart's hypothesis by rewriting with it and
never actually builds the wide table. The two step lemmas -- `eval_since_rel` and `until_step_rel`
-- gained the sortedness of `cs_L` as their only new hypothesis, which the simulation supplies
from `wf_cols n cs_L` and the initialiser from `set_cols`. `duaux'` mirrors `dsaux'` for the
Until, relating a raw `event_data mmuaux_dq` at the dense frame to the narrowed auxiliary list
through `dvalid_muaux`, with `duaux'_init`, `duaux'_add_new`, `duaux'_eval` and `duaux'_length`
the four operations the evaluator needs.

The verified fragment is done: the whole dense chain -- `Dense_Eval`, `Dense_Produces`,
`Dense_Match`, `Dense_Let`, `Dense_Agg`, `Dense_Correct`, `Dense_Compile`, `Dense_Init`,
`Dense_Descend` -- reprocesses green with no embedding on either temporal node.

### 20.33 The same, in the executable evaluator

`verified.ml` is exported from `Dense_Imp.thy`, which declares its own `dformula` and its own
Since and Until structures, so the win only reaches the generated code once that file makes the
same swap. It turns out to be a small one, because of where the sum type branches.

`init_j` picks between three structures: `MSJ_evt` for a closed left operand, `MSJ_imp` -- the
heap-resident one -- when `special_args args`, and `MSJ_pure` otherwise. And

```isabelle
special_args args ⟷ args_L args = {} ∨ args_L args = args_R args
```

is *exactly* the condition under which the embedding was a no-op: the node already passed `None`
for its plan when `cs_L = cs`, and a closed left operand has nothing to widen. So the branch that
carries a real embedding is always, and only, the pure one. Swapping that branch for the dense
structure removes every embedding the evaluator ever performs, and leaves the heap-resident and
closed-left-operand structures untouched:

```isabelle
datatype msaux_d = MSD_imp msaux2 | MSD_dns "event_data dmsaux" | MSD_evt sevtaux
datatype muaux_d = MUD_imp muaux2 | MUD_dns "event_data mmuaux_dq" | MUD_evt evtaux
```

with `init_d`/`init_u_d` choosing by the same conditions and the five (resp. three) dispatch
functions calling `dadd_new_ts_msaux`, `dgc_join_msaux`, `dadd_new_table_msaux`, `dresult_msaux`
and `dadd_new_dq`, `eval_dq` in the dense branch. The nodes lose their plan:

```isabelle
  | DSince args dformula dformula "event_data mebuf2S" msaux_d
  | DUntil args "bool list" dformula dformula "event_data mebuf2" "ts mbuf_t" ts muaux_d
```

and `deval`'s two clauses drop the `case pl of None ⇒ xs | Some p ⇒ map (dembedp p) xs` that used
to sit in front of each buffer add. What the Until keeps is the mask, which is read once per
`dadd_new_dq` to key a lookup rather than applied to every tuple of every table.

`Dense_Imp.thy` carries no proofs -- it is the executable layer, and its correspondence with the
verified fragment is a matter of building the same thing, which is why that fragment was done
first.

One definition had to change to get through the code generator. `dadd_new_dq` guarded its fold
with `finite rel2` and fell back, on an infinite table, on the verified operation against the
widened one -- which renames the keys of a mapping, and `wkeys`/`nkeys` do that over the whole
function space and therefore do not execute. The guard is gone: the fold is over
`csorted_list_of_set rel2`, which is unspecified for an infinite table anyway, and every theorem
about the operation assumes the table finite. Its three consequences lose the hypothesis they no
longer use.

`isabelle build -b -e -d . MFODL_Monitor_Imp` goes through in 3:25 and `dembedp` does not occur
in the exported kernel at all. The 844 cram tests of `tests/` and the 284 of `verimon_tests/` all
pass, `dense_nodes.t` among them, so the fallback-node count is unchanged.

### 20.34 What the embedding cost (measurements)

The two kernels -- the one exported before 20.33 and the one after -- agree byte for byte on
every formula below, so this is a pure timing comparison. Synthetic log, 8000 time points, 50
tuples per predicate per time point over a domain of 200 (9.4 MB); `-verified -dense`, output to
`/dev/null`; nine repetitions, medians.

| formula | with embedding | without | speed-up |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `C(x) SINCE[0,50] B(x,y)` | 2.52s | **1.86s** | 1.35x |
| `C(x) SINCE[0,50] (B(x,y) AND A(y,z))` | 1.29s | **0.90s** | 1.43x |
| `C(x) UNTIL[0,20] B(x,y)` | 1.33s | 1.35s | 1.00x |

The ranges do not overlap on the two Since rows (2.16--2.66 against 1.74--1.93, and 1.15--1.32
against 0.77--0.92), and overlap almost entirely on the Until row.

**The Since gains and the Until does not, and the reason is where the left operand's table sits
in each operator's per-step work.** The Since's per-step work *is* the join: the left operand's
table is the probe, and the embedding built a fresh red-black tree of widened tuples for it at
every time point, so removing it removes a constant fraction of the whole operation. The Until's
per-step work is the fold over the *right* operand's table and the accumulation into the deque;
the left operand's table only feeds `upd_set a1_map`, which is one of several O(|rel1|) passes
among much larger ones, so removing its widening disappears into them. The mapping's keys are
narrower now, which makes its comparisons cheaper, and that is worth about as much as nothing.

The wide controls -- `C(z) AND (B(x,y) SINCE[0,50] A(x,y))` and
`C(z) AND (A(x,y) UNTIL[0,20] B(x,y))`, where the two frames coincide and there never was an
embedding -- are a wash, as they must be: they take the `MSD_imp`/`MUD_dns` branches unchanged.
Their runs write 55 MB of output and scatter over 7.4--9.5s on this container, which is a 25%
band and too wide to read anything finer out of. That is also why the table above uses formulas
whose output is small: at 1--3s with a 10% spread they discriminate, and the 8s output-bound rows
do not.

### 20.35 The full suite against MonPoly

20.34 measured the two kernels against each other on the formulas the embedding touches. This is
the whole suite against MonPoly, on the 8.6 MB log of 20.18
(`evaluation/synthetic/gen_log.py 8000 50 200`) and on P2/P3 with
`gen_log -event_rate 2000 -time_span 100`. Five repetitions, medians, multipliers relative to
MonPoly, lower is better. The repetitions are **interleaved across configurations** rather than
grouped, so drift in machine load hits every column equally. "dense (was)" is the kernel exported
before 20.33; every configuration agrees with MonPoly byte for byte on every row.

| formula | MonPoly | pure | dense (was) | dense | dense+h |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| P2 | 0.38s | 2.40x | 1.63x | **1.53x** | 1.69x |
| P3 | 0.42s | 1.98x | 1.39x | **1.33x** | 1.35x |
| and | 0.53s | 1.47x | 1.04x | 1.04x | **0.93x** |
| andnot | 0.53s | 0.92x | 0.95x | **0.92x** | 0.94x |
| once | 2.36s | 1.16x | **0.73x** | 0.77x | 0.78x |
| since | 0.85s | 1.25x | **1.18x** | 1.20x | 1.21x |
| eventually | 1.60s | 1.23x | 0.87x | 0.86x | **0.83x** |
| until | 0.80s | **1.43x** | 1.43x | 1.47x | 1.51x |
| agg | 0.58s | 1.50x | 1.53x | 1.51x | **1.47x** |
| complex | 2.98s | 1.86x | 1.08x | 1.08x | **1.00x** |
| sincewide | 2.98s | 1.15x | 0.98x | 0.91x | **0.88x** |
| untilwide | 3.06s | 1.16x | 1.00x | 0.99x | **0.99x** |
| aggwide | 12.03s | 1.00x | 0.79x | **0.78x** | 0.83x |
| sincesub | 0.77s | 2.68x | 2.77x | 2.13x | **2.06x** |
| untilsub | 0.67s | **1.43x** | 1.54x | 1.60x | 1.59x |
| sincesub3 | 0.62s | 2.02x | 1.63x | 1.37x | **1.28x** |

`sincesub` is `C(x) SINCE[0,50] B(x,y)`, `untilsub` is `C(x) UNTIL[0,20] B(x,y)` and `sincesub3`
is `C(x) SINCE[0,50] (B(x,y) AND A(y,z))` -- the three rows added here, where the left operand's
frame is a proper nonempty subset of the right operand's and the old code therefore embedded.

**The setup reproduces 20.18.** Its published dense figures were sincewide 0.93x, untilwide 0.99x,
aggwide 0.80x, once 0.76x, eventually 0.88x; the "dense (was)" column of the same kernel here
gives 0.98x, 1.00x, 0.79x, 0.73x, 0.87x. Only `complex` differs materially (1.25x published
against 1.08x here), which is the row whose multi-way join is most sensitive to the machine. So
the columns are comparable with 20.18's, and the container is a slower machine on which MonPoly's
absolute times are larger (once 2.36s against 2.45s, aggwide 12.03s against 12.51s).

**Where the embedding was, it is worth 1.2x to 1.3x; nowhere else does anything move.** Comparing
"dense (was)" against "dense" row by row, the five-run ranges separate on exactly the two rows
that should move and overlap on all fourteen others:

| row | was | now | ranges |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| `sincesub` | 2.12s | **1.63s** | 2.08--2.22 against 1.45--1.68, disjoint |
| `sincesub3` | 1.01s | **0.85s** | 0.89--1.05 against 0.81--0.87, disjoint |
| `sincewide` | 2.92s | 2.70s | 2.31--3.00 against 2.47--2.85, overlapping |
| `P2` | 0.61s | 0.58s | 0.56--0.64 against 0.55--0.66, overlapping |
| `once` | 1.73s | 1.82s | 1.65--1.84 against 1.76--1.92, overlapping |

That is the shape the change predicts. `sincewide`, `untilwide` and the rest take the
`MSD_imp`/`MUD_dns` branches, which 20.33 left untouched; P2 and P3 have a closed left operand, so
they take the sliding-window structure; `untilsub` does embed, and 20.34 already explained why the
Until does not gain from stopping. The per-row spread on this container runs from 5% to 36%
(`untilwide` 5%, `sincesub3` 8%, `P3` 36%), so nothing below about 15% is readable here, and the
two rows that moved are the two that moved by more than that.

**The dense monitor is at or ahead of MonPoly on seven of the sixteen rows** -- `andnot`, `once`,
`eventually`, `sincewide`, `untilwide`, `aggwide`, and `complex` with the heap index. It is behind
on the nine where the tuples have no empty columns to drop (`since`, `until`, `agg` and the three
sub-frame rows) or where the formula is a real policy with a deep join (`P2`, `P3`, `and`). That
split is 19.6's and has not changed: **short tuples pay exactly where there are columns to
shorten**, and the embedding was the last place where the dense evaluator lengthened them again.

The `-heap_maps` index is the best configuration on eight rows and never more than 5% behind the
best on any, which is a better showing than 20.18's (where it earned its keep only on `complex`);
the sub-frame Since rows are new territory for it, and it gains on both.

**What is left.** The embedding is gone from both the verified and the executable evaluator. The
remaining dense work is elsewhere: `MAndTrigger` and the two `MatchP`/`MatchF` operators still
fall back to the sparse evaluator (20.21), and the dense tables are still `event_data option
list` rather than `event_data list` (the unboxing of 20.31, which the hole-free discipline this
section establishes is the precondition for, and which 20.36 carries out).

### 20.36 The option around every field, removed

20.35 closed with what was left: "the dense tables are still `event_data option list` rather than
`event_data list`". A dense tuple has no holes -- `dwf_tuple` says so, and after 20.32--20.33 no
dense operation constructs one, since `dwiden`, the only one that ever did, does not occur in the
generated code any more. So the `option` around every field paid for a possibility that cannot
arise: one indirection on every read, two pattern tests per field in the tuple comparator, and two
words of every field's footprint. This section removes it.

**The generic layer.** `Dense_Unbox.thy` restates the dense operations over a bare element type
`'b` where the existing ones are over `'a option`, and proves each generic operation equal to the
one it generalises *at the option instance*:

```isabelle
lemma gbin_join_eq: "gbin_join pl (A :: event_data table) pos B = dbin_join pl A pos B"
lemma gadd_new_dq_eq:
  assumes "args_agg args = None"
  shows "gadd_new_dq args msk rel1 rel2 nt (d :: event_data mmuaux_dq)
       = dadd_new_dq args msk rel1 rel2 nt d"
```

Every theorem of `Dense.thy` and `Dense_Aux.thy` therefore continues to say what it said, and the
executable evaluator instantiates the same definitions at `event_data`. The layer covers the join,
the dense Since structure, the Until deque, both closed-left-operand structures and the multi-way
conjunction. The operations that *read* a field rather than move it -- the assignment, the
time-point and time-stamp terms, the match, the constraint, the aggregation -- cannot be
generalised, because they go through `meval_trm`, which reads `the (v ! x)`; they get unboxed
twins, each proved to agree with the original on hole-free tuples, e.g.

```isabelle
lemma umatch_reopt: "umatch tms xs = match tms (reopt xs)"
```

An equality at the option instance says nothing at `event_data`, so the join's *specification* had
to be redone generically as well -- the index lookup, the two probe folds and

```isabelle
lemma ggen_join_spec:
  assumes cA: "set (csorted_list_of_set A) = A" and cB: "set (csorted_list_of_set B) = B"
  shows "ggen_join p lk rk A B
       = {dmerge p a b | a b. a \<in> A \<and> b \<in> B \<and> dproj lk a = dproj rk b}"
```

which is what carries `Dense_Join_Correct.thy`'s Hoare triples over.

**The heap maps.** The imperative hash table of `Table_Imp.thy` was keyed on `event_data tuple`
throughout. Retyping the dense structures means the same table has to serve two key types, so it
is generalised: `('k, 'v) ientry`, `('k, 'v) imap`, and the key equality passed to the operations
that compare two keys as an argument rather than taken from `class equal`, which the code
generator would turn into a structural comparison assembled from dictionaries. The argument is
applied at most once per operation, behind the comparison of the two stored hashes, so it costs
nothing next to the hash itself. `ueq` and `uihash` are the two primitives at the short tuples,
the second printed as OCaml that walks one constructor less per element.

`Imap_Correct.thy` becomes a locale:

```isabelle
locale imap_ops =
  fixes khash :: "'k :: heap \<Rightarrow> integer" and keq :: "'k \<Rightarrow> 'k \<Rightarrow> bool"
  assumes keq_eq[simp]: "keq x y \<longleftrightarrow> x = y"
```

Its 1272 commands are unchanged apart from the two names: the hash is only ever required to be a
function and the equality to be the one of the key type, which is all the proofs ever used of
`ihash` and `teq`. The theory ends with the two interpretations, so every Hoare triple it proves
holds for both maps. `Since_Imp2.thy`'s heap-resident Since is generalised the same way, over the
tuple type, with the all-holes tuple of `join2`'s no-join-column branch as a parameter, since
which tuple that is depends on the representation. The Until's heap-resident branch, which
`use_heap_until` switched off, is dropped from the dense monitor rather than retyped.

**The executable evaluator.** `Dense_Imp.thy` is retyped from `event_data table` to
`event_data list set`: the formula, the evaluation function, the heap join index, the three Since
structures and the two Until ones. The subformulas the dense fragment does not cover yet (20.21:
`MAndTrigger`, `MMatchP`, `MMatchF`) still go through the evaluation function over the wide
tuples, which reads its input tables from the database itself, so `wide_db` puts the database
back into the wide representation for them and their results are narrowed and unboxed as before.
A formula with no such subformula -- which `-dense`'s `MONPOLY_DENSE_STATS` counter reports as
zero -- never reaches that conversion.

**The code setup.** The dense monitor's sets and mappings are keyed on `event_data list`, which
`Monomorphic_Code.thy` does not mention at all, so without a counterpart every set and mapping
operation of the dense evaluator would build its dictionaries per call and reach the red-black
tree through `comparator_list comparator_event_data`. `Dense_Code_Opt.thy` gains the section it
has for the wide tuples at the short ones: the direct comparator

```isabelle
fun edl_comparator :: "event_data list comparator" where
  "edl_comparator (x # xs) (y # ys) =
    (case comparator_event_data x y of Eq \<Rightarrow> edl_comparator xs ys | r \<Rightarrow> r)"
```

two frames fewer per element than the composite one; the dictionary rules; the empty table as a
value; the monomorphised set and mapping operations at the four value types the dense structures
use (`nat`, the timestamp sum, the dense table, the dense tuple list); the boxed projections out
of a mapping's tree; the tree primitives with the comparator called directly; and the boxed
dictionaries of the join index, which is entered once per indexed and once per probed tuple.

**The interface.** The front end reads and writes whichever representation the monitor in use
holds. The database and the batch of a time point are now the wide ones or the short ones, chosen
by `-dense`; the conversion of an event is the one for the wide tuples without the `Some` rather
than a second pass over its result, because that is the hottest path there is; and a verdict is
printed from whichever it is, with the fast path over the short tuples losing the
undefined-column case that could send it to the slow one.

**What holds.** `isabelle build -e -d . MFODL_Monitor_Imp` goes through in 3:00, the 844 cram
tests of `tests/` and the 284 of `verimon_tests/` all pass, and the four configurations --
MonPoly, `-verified`, `-verified -dense` and `-verified -dense -heap_maps` -- agree byte for byte
on the fourteen synthetic formulas of 20.35's table that the smaller log covers.

**What it is worth** is 20.37, which also has the one thing that had to be added before it was
worth anything.

### 20.37 What the unboxing cost, and what it is worth

**The first measurement said: nothing.** Nine interleaved repetitions of the suite, the kernel
exported before 20.36 against the one after, gave a geometric mean of 1.00x and three rows --
`sincewide`, `untilwide`, `aggwide` -- slower after. The generated code said why. 20.36's generic
layer is polymorphic in the element type, so the code generator gives every operation of it a
dictionary argument and rebuilds the composite comparator from that argument at every call:

```ocaml
let rec gprobe_step_l (_A1, _A2, _A3) p rk m y acc =
  (match lookupa ((ccompare_list _A2), (equal_list _A3)) m (dproj rk y)
   with None -> acc
   | Some l -> fold (fun x -> insert ((ceq_list _A1), (ccompare_list _A2)) (dmerge p x y)) l acc)
```

where the same function before 20.36 read `ed_lookup_tuples m (dproj rk y)` and `ed_insert`.
That is exactly what `Monomorphic_Code` and the first two sections of `Dense_Code_Opt` remove for
the wide tuples, and what the dense evaluator had for free while its operations were stated at
`event_data tuple` outright. Two words per field were saved and a dictionary build per probed
tuple was bought; on the rows where the tables are widest the second outweighed the first.

**The fix is the third section of `Dense_Code_Opt`.** It specialises the whole dense layer at
`event_data` -- the join, the two Since structures, the Until deque, the two
closed-left-operand ones -- and the heap-resident Since at both tuple types it is used at; it
boxes the operations those call that are not themselves specialised (the emptiness and
cardinality tests, the sorted list of a table, the folds, the mapping projections, the empty
mappings), and monomorphises the tree primitives that membership and insertion reach. The probe
is then

```ocaml
let rec de_gprobe_step_l p rk m y acc =
  (match edl_lookup_tuples m (dproj rk y) with None -> acc
   | Some l -> fold (fun x -> edl_insert (dmerge p x y)) l acc)
```

and the join's emptiness test is `unbox edl_is_empty_v a`, as it was for the wide tuples.

**The suite.** Nine repetitions, interleaved across configurations, medians, multipliers relative
to MonPoly, lower is better. The 8.6 MB log of 20.18 and 20.35 and the same P2/P3 logs.
"dense (was)" is the kernel exported before 20.36; every configuration agrees with MonPoly byte
for byte on every row, and so does "dense (was)".

| formula | MonPoly | pure | dense (was) | dense | dense+h |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| P2 | 0.39s | 2.39x | **1.60x** | 1.60x | 1.62x |
| P3 | 0.43s | 1.93x | 1.36x | 1.33x | **1.31x** |
| and | 0.54s | 1.28x | 1.03x | 1.03x | **0.94x** |
| andnot | 0.48s | 1.02x | 1.07x | **1.01x** | 1.01x |
| once | 2.36s | 1.14x | 0.78x | **0.74x** | 0.74x |
| since | 0.85s | 1.26x | **1.10x** | 1.14x | 1.12x |
| eventually | 1.42s | 1.39x | 0.99x | **0.92x** | 0.93x |
| until | 0.80s | 1.43x | 1.55x | **1.36x** | 1.37x |
| agg | 0.59s | 1.58x | 1.58x | **1.46x** | 1.48x |
| complex | 2.91s | 1.72x | 1.01x | **0.99x** | 1.02x |
| sincewide | 2.78s | 1.20x | 1.00x | 0.95x | **0.95x** |
| untilwide | 2.87s | 1.16x | 1.07x | **0.99x** | 1.00x |
| aggwide | 11.73s | 1.03x | 0.79x | 0.76x | **0.76x** |
| sincesub | 0.80s | 2.61x | 2.12x | **1.84x** | 1.85x |
