### 21.7 Reprofiled, and the first item of the next round

Callgrind on the eight suite formulas of 21.1's profiles, `vdense` before (21.5) and after the
unboxing (21.6), instructions in billions, MonPoly alongside:

| formula | vdense 21.5 | vdense 21.6 | change | monpoly |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| since-nu50 | 4.60 | 3.87 | -16% | 1.53 |
| once-g50 | 1.70 | 1.60 | -6% | 1.45 |
| ev-n20 | 1.70 | 1.58 | -7% | 1.65 |
| until-bb50 | 1.71 | 1.66 | -3% | 1.30 |
| join-swap | 0.97 | 0.94 | -3% | 0.81 |
| aggonce-sum50 | 4.39 | 4.86 | +11% | 2.27 |

Where the instructions go now:

- `since-nu50` (`B(x,y) AND ((NOT C(x)) SINCE[0,50] A(x,y))`): the anti-join structure's
  `gc_join` is 37% of the run (the deletion from the tables of every time point the left
  operand no longer covers, `rbt_comp_del` 34%), `add_new_table` 27%, of which the index
  update `gidx_upd` is 26%.  That is the "index-driven Since anti-join" item: the index is
  rebuilt over the whole table per time point where a per-key update would do.
- `aggonce-sum50`: the fused window's result is 50% of the run (`uag_res`: the kernel's
  `result_maggaux` 21%, then the projection to the node's columns and the unboxing as two
  images, 29%), the insertions into the kernel's structure 43%.  The result read got 0.66
  billion instructions worse than 21.5's `ag_res`, because the second image now targets the
  short tables through the generic `image` -- an RBT built with the dictionary comparator --
  where 21.5's `dsan` targeted the wide ones through the boxed `ed_image`.  Fixed below.
- `once-g50`, `until-bb50`, `ev-n20`: no single operation above 8%; the comparators and the
  tree balancing are the top entries, as for MonPoly, whose instruction count these three are
  within 10-30% of.

**C1: the fused window's result in one image.** `uag_res` now reads the kernel's result
through one image, `(λx. deopt (dproj mask x)) ` R` (the code equation `uag_res_code`, an
`image_image` step from the definition), and `Set.image` between the short and the wide tables
is boxed in both directions (`edl_image_of_wide_v`, `edl_image_to_wide_v`), so that the RBT of
the result and of the widened insertions is built with the specialised comparators.
Three rounds were needed, each a rebuild and a profile, and each is a general finding:

1. Reading the result in one image did not move the instruction count (5.40 billion against
   4.86): the image's insertions still went through the generic tree insertion.  `Set.image`
   is boxed (`ed_image`) and monomorphised (`edl_image`) already, but an image passes
   `Set.insert` to the tree's fold *unapplied*, and the redirection to the specialised
   insertion (`edl_insert`, `edl_rbtc_ins`) only reaches applied occurrences (Section 21.1's
   `unfold` rules are `c x = c' x`).  So every image of the dense evaluator, the shipping
   monitor's included, built its tree with the dictionary comparator.  `edl_image_rbt`,
   `edl_image_of_wide_rbt` and `edl_image_to_wide_rbt` are code equations on a tree that name
   the specialised insertion.
2. That did not move it either (5.25 billion).  The code equation `uag_res_code` had the
   projection mask, `wide_mask (aggargs_n (dagg_args p w)) [0..<…]`, inside the image's
   function, so `dagg_args` -- a record with a column set, a replicated type list and a shifted
   term -- was rebuilt for every tuple of every result (`dagg_args` was 21% of the run).  The
   mask is now bound once per result.  The same trap is worth a look wherever a code equation
   is written by hand with a `λ` under an image or a fold.
3. 4.11 billion: 6% below 21.5's boxed fused window and 15% below 21.6's, with `uag_res` at
   40% of the run (the kernel's `result_maggaux` 26%, the one image 14%).  The next step for
   the aggregation over a window is the kernel structure's own result, which is rebuilt as a
   table at every time point; a verified incremental result would be a change to
   `Optimized_Agg_Temporal.thy`.

`ratios.svg` are this run):

|---|---|---|
| join | 1.13 | 0.99 |
| once | 1.23 | 1.00 |
| since | 1.70 | 0.99 |
| eventually | 1.31 | 1.00 |
| until | 1.59 | 1.01 |
| burst | 1.28 | 0.99 |

The verified dense evaluator is now the shipping monitor's equal on every family of the suite
(geometric mean 0.99, no family above 1.02; 8 timeouts against the shipping monitor's 7, all on

shipping, unverified monitor stands at 1.40x.  What remains to MonPoly is the same for both:
the aggregations over a window (2.0-2.3x, the kernel's structure rebuilding its result), the
Since with an anti-join left operand (1.7x, the index rebuilt per time point, 21.7's first
profile) and the policy formulas that combine them.

### 21.8 The Since index maintained by its keys (`Dense_Idx_Code.thy`)

21.7's first profile of `since-nu50` put 63% of the run into the Since structure's index: the
`gc_join` deleting the left operand's keys from the stored tables (37%) and the `add_new_table`
merging the new tuples into the index (27%, of which the index update was 26%).  The reason is
in 20.31's index algebra (`Dense_Aux.thy`): the index is a clustering of the stored table by
the join columns, and the three operations that maintain it are stated over the whole index.
The tuples that enter the window are clustered on their own and merged in (`didx_union`, a
linear merge of two trees), the tuples that leave it are clustered and subtracted
(`didx_minus`, a `map_values` and a `filter` over every bucket), and the probe by the left
operand's table restricts the index with a `Mapping.filter` over every key.  Each of these
walks the whole index at every time point, while the tuples that actually move -- one time
point's worth -- are typically a small fraction of the window.

**Key-driven forms.** `Dense_Idx_Code.thy` gives the three operations executable forms whose
cost is proportional to what moves, each an equality with the abstract operation:

- `didx_add msk X i = didx_union i (didx msk X)`, computed as a fold of `didx_add1` over `X`,
  where `didx_add1` inserts one tuple into its bucket (`lookup_didx_add`,
  `lookup_fold_didx_add1`, `didx_add_fold`; a `comp_fun_idem` instance makes the fold
  well-defined).
- `didx_del msk D i = didx_minus i (didx msk D)`, a fold of `didx_del1` over `D`, which removes
  one tuple from its bucket and drops the bucket when it empties.
- `mfilter_keys pos Y m = Mapping.filter (λk _. (k ∈ Y) = pos) m`: for the anti-join
  (`pos = False`) it is `mapping_delete_set m Y`, a fold of the deletion over `Y`; for the
  join it is a fold over `Y` that looks each key up and keeps what it finds
  (`mrestrict1`, `lookup_fold_mrestrict1`, `mfilter_keys_True_fold`).

The costs: `|X| log |i|` and `|D| log |i|` for the two index updates instead of `|i|`, and
`|Y| log |m|` for the probe instead of `|m| log |Y|` -- and since `Y` is the left operand's
table at one time point, whose construction already cost `|Y| log |Y|`, the key-driven probe is
never asymptotically worse than the work the time point costs anyway, where the walk over the
index was unbounded relative to it.

**One abstract definition changed.** `didx_minus` dropped every bucket that ended up empty.  A
fold over the subtrahend's tuples cannot reach a bucket at a key the subtrahend does not have,
so it cannot drop such a bucket if it was empty to begin with -- and an exact, invariant-free
equality was wanted, as everywhere in 21.6.  `didx_minus` now drops a bucket only when the
subtrahend touches it: `Mapping.filter (λk Y. Mapping.lookup d k = None ∨ Y ≠ {})`.  On a
clustering, which has no empty buckets, the two readings agree, and `didx_minus_eq` (the
correctness of the subtraction against `didx msk (S - D)`) and `didx_minus_rmk` (its
naturality under the unboxing) proved as before.

**Installing them, and a limit of the rewriting.** The two index updates are installed as
`code_unfold` rules on the applied patterns, `didx_union i (didx msk X) = didx_add msk X i` and
`didx_minus i (didx msk D) = didx_del msk D i`; the generated `de_gshift_end`,
`de_gadd_new_ts_msaux'` and `de_gadd_new_table_msaux` call `de_didx_del` and `de_didx_add`
without a change to the verified definitions.  The probe could not be installed the same way:
`Mapping.filter` is boxed at the monitor's types (20.33), and the boxing rewrites the bare
constant before a rule on the applied pattern with the `λ` can match.  So `dsemi_idx` and
`gjoin_msaux` get restated code equations (`dsemi_idx_code`, `gjoin_msaux_code`), proved by
unfolding `mfilter_keys`; the definitions are untouched.

**The dictionary comparator, again.** The first build with the index alone brought
`since-nu50` from 3.87 to 2.72 billion instructions (-30%), and its profile had the generic
list comparator at 12% inclusive against 3% for the specialised one: the operations around the
index -- `mapping_delete_set`, `upd_set`, `minus_keys`, `filter_set`, `upd_set_keys` and the
folds inside `mfilter_keys` -- were still generic or *boxed*, and a boxed operation (20.33)
fixes the dictionaries but still runs the generic tree code with the dictionary's comparator
inside.  Monomorphising them (21.1's `de_` copies) reaches the specialised tree primitives, with
two more of Section 21.7's unapplied-step cases on the way: `mapping_delete_set` passes
`Mapping.delete` to the tree's fold unapplied, and an eta-expanded `λk. Mapping.delete k` in
the code equation does not survive the preprocessing, so the two instances get their tree
equations by hand (`de_mapping_delete_set_nat_rbt`, `de_mapping_delete_set_table_rbt`); and
`gshift_end` folds `filter_set` over the expired time points unapplied, so the fold gets a name
(`fold_filter_set`) whose code equation applies the step, and `gshift_end` a restated code
equation.  `upd_set`'s step applies the update to the key and the value, so it stays applied
and needs only a tree equation (`upd_set_RBT_code`).

**Measured.** `since-nu50` under callgrind, instructions in billions: 3.87 (21.6) → 2.72
(the key-driven index) → 2.58 (the operations around it specialised), -33%, against MonPoly's
1.53.  What is left in the Since structure: the insertions into the index and into `tuple_in`
themselves (`didx_add` 11%, `upd_set` 11%), the deletions of the anti-join (`mapping_delete_set`
9%, `dsemi_idx` 6%) and the garbage collection (`ggc_msaux` 11%: the union of every table of
the window, taken once per window length, and the index over `tuple_since` rebuilt from it).
The suite (`evaluation/perf/report.md` and `ratios.svg` are this run; the ratios are the
measure, since the machine's absolute times drift between runs -- MonPoly itself ran 13% faster
than in 21.7's run):

| family | vdense/monpoly 21.7 | vdense/monpoly | vdense/dense |
|---|---|---|---|
| since | 1.70 | 1.41 | 0.95 |
| until | 1.59 | 1.47 | 0.99 |
| burst | 1.28 | 1.18 | 1.00 |
| policy | 1.98 | 1.82 | 0.99 |
| agg_fut | 2.03 | 1.91 | 1.03 |
| all 21 families | 1.39 | 1.36 | 0.99 |

| formula | 21.7 | now |
|---|---|---|
| since-nu5 (`B(x,y) AND ((NOT C(x)) SINCE[0,5] A(x,y))`) | 3.18 | 1.96 |
| since-nu50 | 2.94 | 2.03 |
| since-nu500 | 2.89 | 2.14 |
| since-nb20 (`C(x) AND ((NOT B(x,y)) SINCE[0,20] A(x,y))`) | 2.30 | 1.48 |
| since-u50 (`B(x,y) AND (C(x) SINCE[0,50] A(x,y))`) | 1.94 | 1.57 |
| pol-sincefutb | 4.13 | 2.62 |

(the ratios of vdense to MonPoly.)  The Since family goes from 1.70x to 1.41x MonPoly, the
suite from 1.39x to 1.36x, with 4 timeouts against 21.7's 8 (the four that remain are the
aggregations over a window, 21.7's next item), identical verdicts throughout, and the shipping
dense monitor -- which runs the same generic Since structure -- gains the same, so the two stay
at 0.99.  The Since with a negated left operand stands at 2.0x MonPoly; the next profile puts the
remaining gap in the tree insertions and deletions themselves, which MonPoly does with hash
tables.

### 21.9 The trigger structure over short tuples, the naturality half (`Dense_Unbox_Eval.thy`)

`Dense_Unbox.thy` (20.44 in the prior round) already sketched a generic short-tuple trigger
structure, `'b gmmtaux`, alongside its own operations (`gsemi_join`, `ginit_mmtaux`,
`gupdate_mmtaux'`, `gupdate_mmtaux`, `gresult_mmtaux`, `gmmtaux_empty`) and the design notes
explaining why it is possible: unlike the Since and Until, the trigger's kernel structure
(`Optimized_Trigger.thy`) keeps the left operand's tuples at their *own* frame internally --
narrow, the width of the mask's true positions -- and only widens them at the one place they are
compared against the right operand's projected frame.  What was missing, and is added here, is
the proof that this generic structure, instantiated at the short element type, is a verified
refinement of the kernel's own `event_data option mmtaux`: a widening map on the state (`tmm`)
under which the two structures step and read alike, exact equality, no invariant beyond the two
frames' widths (`dnarrow_tuple`).

The naturality chain, each a lemma proved against the kernel's real, already-committed
definitions (`update_mmtaux`, `update_mmtaux'`, `proj_tuple_in_join[_optim]`), landed in
`Dense_Unbox_Eval.thy` right after its window-naturality sections, since that is the first file
downstream of both `Dense_Unbox` (for `gmmtaux`) and `Dense_Aux` (for `wkeys`, `dmsk_args`,
`dwiden`/`dproj`, `dnarrow_tuple`) that this material needs:

- `tmm`/`tnar`: the widening map on the state and the narrowness invariant on a dense one.
- The semi-join: `gsemi_join_dsemi`, `proj_tuple_in_join_dsemi`, `proj_tuple_in_join_optim_dsemi`
  -- the kernel's three-way case split on the mask (all-true, all-false, mixed) agrees with the
  dense `dsemi` at every branch, given the two tables' widths.
- The expiry fold (`fold_filter_once_tmm`, `shift_end'_tmm`): the mapping of last occurrences and
  its key set are widened/narrowed by `wkeys`/`dwiden` throughout the same fold.
- The update (`fold_hist_tmm`, `update_mmtaux'_tmm`, `update_mmtaux_tmm`): the fold over the
  entering time points differs from the kernel's only in the semi-join, already covered above;
  `update_mmtaux_tmm` is stated at `mem (args_ivl args) 0`, the only case a *standalone* trigger
  node ever takes (its own guard in `udof` already requires it), so the other branch of
  `update_mmtaux` -- which only touches the left operand's own tuples, unwidened either side --
  needs no lemma here.

**What this is not yet.** The chain reaches `update_mmtaux`, not yet `result_mmtaux`: the
kernel's result unions the left operand's once-keys (kept *wide*, i.e. embedded into the right
operand's frame with holes) with `hist_sat`/`since_sat`, while the generic structure's own
`tuple_in_once_keys` is narrow throughout, so the correct read is *embed the once-keys, once,
right before returning them* -- a cold, once-per-time-point operation, unlike the semi-join's hot
path, which never widens at all.  `UDTrigger`'s state in `Dense_Unbox_Exec.thy` still carries the
boxed `event_data mmtaux` (the `nat option list` embedding plan, `pl`, applied to every left
tuple via `dembedp` on the way in): switching it to `event_data gmmtaux` needs this embedding
lemma at the result, then rewriting `udof`'s and `udeval`'s `UDTrigger` cases, then a
`dtrigger_step_rel`-style theorem (mirroring the one already proved for the boxed path in
`Dense_Eval.thy`) inside `Dense_Unbox_Eval.thy`'s own induction for `udeval_Rep`.  That is the
remaining, larger half of this item; nothing in `verified.ml` changes from this round (confirmed:
`src/verified.ml` came out byte-identical from the rebuild) since only lemmas, no code
equations, were added.  Differential tests unchanged (corpus 593 identical, 7 refused, 0
differing; the whole session's 23 theories check clean, 0 errors).

### 21.10 The imperative bits, in their own session

The `MFODL_Monitor_Imp` session mixed two things that do not need each other's heap image: the
dense (short-tuple) monitor -- `Dense`, `Dense_Eval`, the `Dense_Unbox*` chain and their proofs,
none of it touching `Imperative_HOL` -- and the genuinely imperative refinement of the Since and
Until structures (`Table_Imp`, `Since_Imp2`, `Until_Imp2`, `Monitor_Imp2`, `Dense_Imp`, ...),
which does.  A grep for `Heap`/`Imperative_HOL`/`Array.`/`Ref.`/`sep_auto` across the session's 32
theories gave a clean split on paper: 10 files with the pattern, 22 without, including the whole
dense chain -- so it looked, at first, like moving the 10 into their own session would be a matter
of editing the `ROOT` file.

It was not quite: three of the "pure" files (`Dense_Unbox.thy`'s Since/Until dispatch structures
for a closed left operand, `sevtaux` and `evtaux`) turned out to be *stated* with zero Heap
mentions but *housed* inside `Since_Imp2.thy` and `Until_Imp2.thy`, alongside those theories'
genuinely imperative `msaux2`/`muaux2` structures and the `msaux_j`/`muaux_j` dispatch datatypes
that select between them -- so `Dense_Unbox.thy`, at the root of the whole dense chain, imported
two Heap-heavy theories for two pure type synonyms and nine pure definitions.  Likewise
`Table_Imp.thy` carried `evt_add`/`evt_del`, the pure window-union two structures share, at the
end of an otherwise entirely imperative file.

The fix pulled those pure pieces out into three small theories -- `Table_Evt.thy` (`evt_add`,
`evt_del`), `Since_Evt.thy` (`sevtaux` and its operations) and `Until_Evt.thy` (`evtaux` and its
operations), each importing only `MFODL_Monitor_Devel.Optimized_Until_Deque` for `queue`/`args`/
`memL`/`memR` -- and rewired `Table_Imp.thy`, `Since_Imp2.thy` and `Until_Imp2.thy` to import them
back for their heap/pure dispatch, while `Dense_Unbox.thy` now imports `Since_Evt`/`Until_Evt`
directly instead of the two heap-tainted theories that used to carry them.  With that cut made,
the dense chain's dependency graph no longer touches `Imperative_HOL` anywhere, and the session
splits cleanly along the file boundary that the original grep suggested.

`MFODL_Monitor_Imp` is now two sessions: `MFODL_Monitor_Dense` (built directly on
`MFODL_Monitor_Devel`, holding `Table_Evt`, `Since_Evt`, `Until_Evt` and the entire `Dense*`/
`Dense_Unbox*`/`Dense_Idx_Code` chain -- 25 theories, no `Imperative_HOL`, no `Heap`) and
`MFODL_Monitor_Imp` (built on `MFODL_Monitor_Dense`, holding the imperative refinement and the
final `export_code` -- `Table_Imp`, `Imap_Correct`, `Optimized_Since_Imp`, `Since_Imp2`,
`Join_Imp2`, `Until_Imp2`, `Monitor_Imp2`, `Dense_Imp`, `Dense_Join_Correct`, `Dense_Code_Opt`,
`Monitor_Imp`, `Monitor_Code_Imp` -- 12 theories).  `Dense_Code_Opt.thy` stays in the imperative
session: its `heap_since_consts` monomorphisation is over genuinely `Heap`-typed constants of
`Since_Imp2.thy`, used by the plain (non-`vdense`) dense mode, and it directly imports `Dense_Imp`
for the heap-resident join index; only its `dense_elem_consts`/`edl_*` sections serve `vdense`,
and splitting those out of the file was not needed to reach the goal (the vdense evaluator itself,
`udmstep`/`udeval`, lives entirely in `MFODL_Monitor_Dense` regardless of where its code-generation
tuning lives).  `Monitor_Code_Imp.thy` -- the one place that exports both the heap-based dense
mode's symbols and `vdense`'s -- stays where it always was, so `postbuild.sh`'s
`MFODL_Monitor_Imp.Monitor_Code_Imp:code/**` export path and `src/verified.ml` location are
unchanged.

Every theory-name antiquotation moved with its theory (`@{theory MFODL_Monitor_Imp.Dense_X}`
became `@{theory MFODL_Monitor_Dense.Dense_X}` for the 25 theories that moved; a first attempt
using bare unqualified names -- `@{theory Dense_X}` -- checked the theories fine but failed
`isabelle build`'s later document/browser-info pass with "Unknown ancestor theory", so this
codebase's convention of writing the full session-qualified name even for a same-session
antiquotation is load-bearing, not decorative).  The new session also needed adding to
`thys/ROOTS`, which lists session directories explicitly rather than having `isabelle build -d`
discover them by recursive scan.

Both sessions check clean (0 errors, 0 sorries: `MFODL_Monitor_Dense`'s 25 theories then
`MFODL_Monitor_Imp`'s 12, `4:32`/`2:15` elapsed respectively).  `src/verified.ml` came out
byte-for-byte identical to the pre-split file -- expected, since nothing was proved or defined
differently, only moved and re-qualified -- and the differential corpus is unchanged (593
identical, 7 refused, 0 differing).

### 21.11 Two exports, one per session, and the flags that measured nothing

A worry surfaced after the split: with the shipping `src/verified.ml` built from one combined
`export_code` naming both the imperative dense mode's entry points and `vdense`'s, could something
about that -- shared code, a shared runtime flag -- make one mode's measurement depend on the
other's? The way to settle it is to export each mode from its own session, with nothing else in
the request list, and read what actually comes out.

`MFODL_Monitor_Dense.Monitor_Code_Dense` is a new theory, entirely within that session, exporting
`udminit_safe`/`udmstep`/`uvdfail_nodes`/`udmstate_m` plus the front end and the short-tuple
database helpers (its own copies of `utable_of_list`/`uempty_db`/`uinsert_table_into_db`/
`urbt_fold`, since these were previously only defined in `Dense_Code_Opt.thy` on the imperative
side; duplicating four pure one-line definitions across two sessions is simpler than the
extraction `Table_Evt`/`Since_Evt`/`Until_Evt` needed, and there is no `Heap` dependency to cut
here). `MFODL_Monitor_Imp.Monitor_Code_Imp_Only` is a second, sibling theory next to the existing
`Monitor_Code_Imp` (which the shipping binary still builds from, unchanged), exporting only
`minit_safe_d`/`mstep_d`/`dfail_nodes` and the same database helpers, with no `vdense` symbol
named at all. Both produce self-contained files (`thys/MFODL_Monitor_Dense/verified.ocaml` and
`thys/MFODL_Monitor_Imp/verified_imp.ocaml`); each compiles standalone with
`ocamlfind ocamlopt -package zarith`, confirming each is independently well-formed.

The two files are not, and cannot be, linked into one running binary as a drop-in replacement for
the combined `src/verified.ml`: every `export_code` command regenerates its own copy of every
datatype it touches -- `event_data`, `Formula.formula`, `mapping`, `set`, all of it -- from
scratch, so `Monitor.event_data` in one file and `Monitor.event_data` in the other are two
distinct OCaml types with identical shapes, and a value built by one file's functions cannot be
handed to the other's without a conversion layer neither file provides. Isabelle's code generator
has no notion of one export reusing another's generated module. Turning this pair into an actual
dual-module (or dual-binary) shipping build -- functorising `verified_adapter.ml`'s ~560 lines of
formula/term/table conversions over the two structurally-identical-but-nominally-distinct
`Monitor` signatures -- is a separate, larger piece of work not done here.

What reading the two files answered directly:

- **No cross-contamination.** `grep` for `dense_heap_join`/`heap_join_min`/`ConfigImp`/
  `heap_join_ok` against `MFODL_Monitor_Dense`'s export returns nothing: none of the imperative
  refinement's machinery is reachable from `vdense`'s own symbols, confirming on the generated
  code -- not just on the import graph -- that the two are independent.

- **A genuine, previously live footgun.** `dense_heap_join` (whether the short-tuple join indexes
  on the heap) and `heap_join_min` (the size threshold for doing so) were mutable OCaml refs in a
  `ConfigImp` module, written on every run from `-dense`/`-heap_maps`/`-heap_join_min`. But
  `-dense` and `-heap_maps` were *already* documented as "deprecated, accepted and ignored" --
  meaning `dense_heap_join` was `!Misc.dense_cols && !Misc.heap_all`, which is `false` unless a
  user passed two flags whose own help text said they did nothing. So every benchmark that never
  passed those two flags together ran with the heap-resident dense join permanently off, silently:
  `Dense_Imp.thy`'s `dbin_join_h` always fell back to the pure `gbin_join`. Whatever the
  imperative session's dense join has been measured at, it was measured with its own heap index
  never exercised. `ConfigImp` (both fields) is now gone; `dense_heap_join` and `heap_join_min`
  are fixed HOL constants (`False` and `0`, i.e. the values every real run already had), with no
  `code_printing` override, so they compile to plain immutable OCaml `let`s -- confirmed in both
  new exports (`let dense_heap_join : bool = false;; let heap_join_min : Z.t = Z.zero;;`) and, via
  a rebuild, in the shipping `verified.ml` too. The dead branch its guard was skipping stays in
  the generated code (Isabelle's code generator does not eliminate `if` branches on constant
  conditions), so `heap_join_ok` and the heap-indexed path of `dbin_join_h` are still present --
  just no longer switchable by anything, and unchanged from what every run already computed.

- **A second flag that has measured nothing for longer.** `Config.generic_join`,
  `Config.imperative_join` and `Config.bulk_min` -- three more mutable refs, from the base
  session's `Code_Opt.thy`, written from `-generic_join`/`-imperative_join`/`-bulk_min` on every
  run -- appear in *every* export checked, old and new: declared, and never once read anywhere in
  14000-plus lines of generated code. `-generic_join`/`-imperative_join` gate `use_generic_join`
  inside `Join_Imp2.thy`'s multi-way join dispatcher, which belongs to the wide-tuple imperative
  monitor (`Monitor_Imp`/`Monitor_Imp2`); that monitor's own top-level step function has not been
  in the export's request list since the switch to dense-only exports (`Monitor_Code_Imp.thy`
  already documented these evaluators as unreachable), and `Dense_Imp.thy`'s own multi-way
  conjunction is a separate, un-gated implementation that never calls into it. `bulk_min` (the
  build-in-one-pass-from-a-sorted-list threshold) is equally unreferenced everywhere checked. The
  three CLI flags' own help text claimed an effect on "the verified kernel" that, empirically, it
  has not had since dense-only export became the shipping default. The OCaml-side wiring
  (`Verified.Config.* := ...` in `algorithm_verified.ml`, run unconditionally on every verified-
  kernel invocation) is removed; the three flags are still accepted, now marked deprecated and
  ignored like `-dense`/`-heap_maps` already were, rather than silently doing nothing while
  claiming otherwise. The base session's own `Config`/`bulk_min` definitions in `Code_Opt.thy` are
  untouched, since that theory is shared infrastructure this change did not audit for other
  consumers.

None of this changes what the shipping binary computes: every fixed constant above is fixed at
the value every real invocation already had, and the differential corpus and `dune build` are
unchanged (593 identical, 7 refused, 0 differing; the pre-existing `matchp` `difftest.sh`
mismatch, present before any of this round's changes, is unaffected). What changes is that the
five flags this section describes can no longer be mistaken for switches that do something.

### 21.12 The trigger node over short tuples, the wiring half (`Dense_Trigger_Nar.thy`)

21.9 proved the naturality and stopped there: `UDTrigger` still carried the kernel's own
`event_data mmtaux`, with the left operand embedded into the right one's frame by `dembedp` on
the way into the buffer, and the answer taken out of the option by `deopt_table` on the way out,
once per time point. This round wires the node onto the short structure. It took two steps,
because the two halves of the change have very different characters.

**Where the conditional step belongs.** The gap 21.9 described is real but it is not where that
section guessed. `update_mmtaux_tmm` is *conditional* -- it needs `dmsk_args args msk`, the
interval containing zero, and narrowness of everything the state holds -- while `udeval_Rep` is
*unconditional*: it relates the short evaluator to the boxed one on every input, with no
invariant threaded through its induction. Hanging a conditional naturality off `Rep_udformula`
would have forced an invariant onto all thirty-odd constructors of `udformula` and onto every
case of that induction.

The way out is that the two halves of the trigger's change are at different levels, and only one
of them is conditional:

- **Narrowing the frame** (the left operand's tuples stay at their own column list rather than
  being embedded into the right one's) is conditional, and belongs at the *boxed* level, in
  `Dense_Eval.thy`, where `dsim`'s `DsTrigger` rule already carries `mem (args_ivl args) 0`,
  `wf_cols n cs_L`, `set cs_L ⊆ set cs` and the whole `dtrigger_rel`. There the hypotheses are
  free.
- **Removing the option** is unconditional -- it is a relabelling, exactly like every other
  structure in `Dense_Unbox_Eval.thy` -- and belongs at the short level, where `udeval_Rep` needs
  it.

So the wiring is: `DTrigger`/`EDTrigger` carry `event_data option gmmtaux` (boxed, narrow left
frame), `UDTrigger` carries `event_data gmmtaux` (short, narrow left frame), and the two maps
compose, `tmm ∘ bmm`, with the conditional one applied a level below the unconditional one.

**`Dense_Trigger_Nar.thy`** is a new theory holding the conditional half. It has to sit upstream
of `Dense_Eval` (which now carries the narrow structure) and downstream of `Dense_Unbox` (which
defines it), so the `tmm` package moved there out of `Dense_Unbox_Eval`, together with the
`map_queue` functor it is stated over and `dtaux`, moved out of `Dense.thy` and restated. What
it adds beyond 21.9:

- `tvoid`: the invariant the standalone trigger keeps. With `mem (args_ivl args) 0` the update
  never takes the branch that records a left tuple, so the queue of the time points still outside
  the interval, the mapping of last occurrences and its key set stay at their initial *empty*
  values for the node's whole life (`gupdate_mmtaux_tvoid`). This is what makes the widening map's
  hypotheses free (`tvoid_tnar`, `update_mmtaux_tmm'`) -- and it is also what closes the gap 21.9
  left open at the result: the kernel unions the (wide) once-keys into its answer, the short
  structure unions the (narrow) ones, and on a state where that set is empty the two agree with no
  embedding at all (`result_mmtaux_tmm`). The "cold, once-per-time-point embedding" 21.9 expected
  to need turns out not to be needed.
- `init_mmtaux_tmm`, `gupdate_mmtaux_not_empty` (with the interval containing zero the window is
  never empty after a step, so the kernel's empty-window answer -- the wildcard tuple -- is never
  the one being read), and `dtaux`/`dtaux_init`/`dtaux_update`/`dtaux_result` restated over the
  narrow structure.

**`Dense_Unbox_Eval.thy`** gains the unconditional half: `bmm`, the relabelling from the short
element type to the boxed one, and its naturality through the whole update -- the queue scans
(`takedropWhile_queue_rq3`, `append_queue_rq3`), the expiry fold over the left operand's records
(`fold_filter_once_bmm`, `shift_end'_bmm`), the semi-join (`gsemi_join_reopt`), the fold over the
entering time points (`fold_hist_bmm`), and the read of `hist_sat` off the head of the in-window
queue (`hist_sat_bmm`), ending at `gupdate_mmtaux_bmm`, `gresult_mmtaux_bmm`,
`gmmtaux_empty_bmm`, `ginit_mmtaux_bmm`. No invariant anywhere.

**What the node does now.** `deval`/`edeval`'s `DTrigger`/`EDTrigger` case calls
`gupdate_mmtaux`/`gresult_mmtaux` and adds the left operand's table to the buffer *unembedded*;
`dtrigger_rel` accordingly holds `map (dtabc n cs_L) (fst buf)` where it held
`map (dtabc n cs_R) (fst buf)`, and `dtrigger_step_rel` runs `mbuf2T_take_rel` with
`h = dtabc n cs_L`. `udeval`'s `UDTrigger` case runs the same two operations on raw short tables
through a new `uebuf2T_take`, with no `reopt_table` on either operand and no `deopt_table` on the
answer; `Rep_udformula` applies `rbuf2` and `bmm`, and the correspondence is
`mebuf2T_take_rbuf`/`mebuf2T_take_trigger`. The embedding plan `pl` is now dead in the node --
kept as a field, since the compiler still computes it once and it costs nothing at run time.

**What this is worth.** Per time point the trigger no longer pays for: embedding every left tuple
into the wider frame (`dembedp`, a per-field list walk), putting the option back on both
operands' tables (`reopt_table`, two full table rebuilds), or taking it off the answer
(`deopt_table`, a third). What is left is the semi-join and the window bookkeeping, on tuples of
the width each operand actually binds. The win is largest where the left operand is strictly
narrower than the right one -- `andtrigger` (`A(x,y) AND (C(x) TRIGGER[0,10] A(x,y))`), whose
left operand binds one of the two columns -- and is the unboxing alone where the two frames
coincide (`trigger`, `A(x,y) TRIGGER[0,10] B(x,y)`).

**Measured.** The benchmark logs are 8000 time points of `syn.log` (50 `A`, 50 `B`, 13 `C` tuples
per time point), the binaries are the one at the previous commit and the one built from this
export, run interleaved, 15 repetitions; the table gives the median (the minimum agrees to within
a percent of the ratios).

| formula | left / right frame | vdense before | after | ratio | dense before | after | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `trigger` `A(x,y) TRIGGER[0,10] B(x,y)` | `{x,y}` / `{x,y}` | 0.896 | 0.598 | **0.667** | 0.618 | 0.614 | 0.993 |
| `trignar` `C(x) TRIGGER[0,10] A(x,y)` | `{x}` / `{x,y}` | 0.774 | 0.594 | **0.768** | 0.622 | 0.618 | 0.994 |
| `andtrigger` `A(x,y) AND (C(x) TRIGGER[0,10] A(x,y))` | `{x}` / `{x,y}` | 0.758 | 0.617 | **0.814** | 0.619 | 0.616 | 0.996 |
| `since` (control) | | 0.988 | 0.987 | 0.999 | 0.962 | 0.967 | 1.005 |
| `and` (control) | | 0.538 | 0.536 | 0.996 | 0.552 | 0.547 | 0.993 |
| `until` (control) | | 1.148 | 1.093 | 0.952 | 1.129 | 1.111 | 0.984 |

Two things in that table are worth saying plainly.

**The frame narrowing, by itself, measures nothing.** The `dense` columns -- the boxed evaluator,
which is where `DTrigger` moved from the kernel's structure to the narrow one -- are 0.993, 0.994
and 0.996, i.e. inside the noise, on all three trigger formulas, including the two whose left
operand is strictly narrower than their right. Dropping `dembedp` from the hot path buys nothing
you can see at that level: it is one list walk per left table per time point, against a semi-join
and a window update on fifty tuples. The narrowing earns its place not by being faster but by
being what makes the *next* step possible: with the left operand already at its own frame, the
short-tuple node is a plain relabelling of the boxed one, and the relabelling is what
`udeval_Rep` can carry unconditionally.

**The trigger was the node where the short-tuple evaluator was losing.** Before this round the
`vdense` column ran *slower* than the boxed `dense` one on every trigger formula -- 0.896 against
0.618 on `trigger`, 45% worse -- because it paid for two `reopt_table`s and a `deopt_table` per
time point that the boxed evaluator, already holding boxed tables, did not. It is the only node
where that was true after 21.6 unboxed the rest. It is no longer true: 0.598 against 0.614, now
marginally ahead, which is what the other nodes look like. The gain is largest on `trigger`
(0.667), where both operands carry fifty two-column tuples per time point and so there is the
most table material to stop relabelling, and smallest on `andtrigger` (0.814), where the trigger
shares its work with a conjunction that this change does not touch.

**Correctness.** Both sessions build clean (`MFODL_Monitor_Dense` 4:09, `MFODL_Monitor_Imp` 2:13,
0 errors), the differential corpus is unchanged at 593 identical / 7 refused / 0 differing, and
`difftest.sh` has the short and boxed evaluators agreeing on `trigger`, `trignar` and
`andtrigger` (the one `matchp` mismatch it reports predates this round and is unaffected). The
regenerated `src/verified.ml` is 419 lines shorter: with `UDTrigger` no longer mentioning
`update_mmtaux`/`result_mmtaux`, the kernel's wide-tuple trigger structure drops out of the
export's reachable set entirely.

### 21.13 The fused window's result and the Until deque's read (`Dense_Unbox_Eval.thy`, `Dense_Until_Gen.thy`)

21.7 named the next two items from its profiles and guessed at their shape. Reprofiled on the
binary this round starts from (callgrind, `base1500.log`, 1500 time points), the guesses needed
correcting in both directions, so this section records the measurements that decided each one
rather than only what was built.

**The result, read in one pass.** On `aggonce-sum50` (`r <- SUM y; x ONCE[0,50] A(x,y)`,
4.085 Gi) the fused window's result was 39.2% of the run, split 25.6% for the kernel's
`result_maggaux` and 13.6% for the node's own read of it. The kernel answers with a table of the
node's frame, one tuple per group; the node then reads that table again at its own frame and
without the option. Two tables of the same size, built one after the other, once per time point.
On top of that the kernel's read goes through `get_map_result`, which walks the *keys* of
its mapping and looks each one up -- a second descent of the tree per group.

`ures_maggaux h args a = h ` result_maggaux args a` is the composite, true by definition, so no
correctness proof changed; the fusion lives entirely in the code equations. `ugmr` walks
`RBT_Mapping2.entries` instead, where the key and its value arrive together, and folds the
node's own short tuple straight into the answer. One tree, no lookups.

| formula | before | after | change |
|---|---|---|---|
| `aggonce-sum50` | 4.085 Gi | 3.038 Gi | **-25.6%** |
| `agg-cnt` | 1.2534 Gi | 1.2535 Gi | 0.0% |

`uag_res` itself went from 39.2% (1602 Mi) to 18.0% (546 Mi), and `result_maggaux`,
`ed_get_map_result` and `ed_image` left the profile entirely. `agg-cnt` is unchanged and should
be: it is a plain aggregation with no window, so it never reaches the fused result at all.

Two things worth keeping from this one. `edl_insert` at 18.2% of the new profile confirms the
fold reaches the monomorphised short insertion: 21.7's trap -- an image hands `Set.insert` to the
tree's fold *unapplied*, where the redirection does not reach it -- does not bite here, because
the fold applies it to an argument. And the equation had to keep `h ` result_maggaux args a` on
the branches the aggregation type makes unreachable: `result_maggaux`'s inner case is partial, so
both sides are `undefined` there, at different types, and pushing the image inside would have
demanded `h ` undefined = undefined`.

**Not the incremental result.** 21.7 proposed instead that the kernel's structure maintain its
result incrementally, as a change to `Optimized_Agg_Temporal.thy`. Measured, that is the worse
trade on this workload: the log has about 50 `A` tuples per time point over 200 distinct groups
and a window of 51 time points, so roughly 50 groups gain a tuple and 50 lose one per step --
about half the groups change, and incrementality would save about half of the result's cost where
the fusion saves two thirds of it, in exchange for a datatype change to `aggaux` and its
correctness proofs in the shared verified kernel. It stays unbuilt, with the numbers recorded.

**The Until deque's indexed read.** 21.6 flagged this as a cost this refinement had added:
`dq_nth_l` reads an entry off the deque's lists rather than by the kernel's counters, so that a
map over the entries commutes with the read on every deque and not only on a consistent one. It
was written as `if k < length (dq_f d) then dq_f d ! k else z`, and the generated code does
exactly that -- `size_lista` walks the list to its end, then `nth` walks it again to the index --
where the kernel's own `dq_nth` does only the index. The deques the Until structure indexes are
as long as its window.

`nth_def` walks once and returns the default when it runs out, and `dq_nth_l_code` is
*unconditional*: the default past the end is the same one, so the naturality `dq_nth_l` exists
for is untouched and no invariant is needed.

| formula | before | after | change |
|---|---|---|---|
| `until-bb50` | 1.6398 Gi | 1.5391 Gi | **-6.1%** |
| `ev-n20` | 1.5810 Gi | 1.5808 Gi | 0.0% |

`dq_nth_l` was 7.6% of `until-bb50` and has left the profile; what remains of the deque's reads
is `dq_nth_s` at 1.3% and `dq_nth` at 1.1%. `ev-n20` is unchanged, as an eventually with no
nested deque should be.

**Not the fused insertions.** The other half of 21.6's note -- the insertions widen the short
tuples into the kernel's boxed ones through a third image -- was measured and declined. The
headline looked large (`usa_add_new_table` 22.1% and `usa_add_new_ts`/`usa_expire` 19.7% of the
post-fusion `aggonce-sum50`), but most of that is the window's own bookkeeping, and what fusing
the widening into the kernel's fold would remove is the boxed *tree build*, `ed_insert`, at
118 Mi of 3038 Mi -- 3.9%. The per-tuple widening stays either way. And it is not a code
equation: `Finite_Set.fold f z (g ` T) = Finite_Set.fold (f ∘ g) z T` needs `inj_on g T`, and
`udwiden` is not injective on arbitrary lists (`udwiden [True] [x] = udwiden [True] [x,y]
= [Some x]`) -- only on tuples of the node's own width, which is an invariant the evaluator
maintains and a code equation cannot state. It would need the two-level treatment 21.12 gave the
trigger, threading the width invariant through `usa_move`, `usa_add_new_table` and `usa_expire`
and their naturality lemmas, for 3.9% of one family. Recorded, not built.

Differential tests unchanged throughout: corpus 593 identical, 7 refused, 0 differing after each
of the two changes, and `difftest.sh` agreeing on every benchmark formula (the `matchp` mismatch
it reports predates this round).

The 185 formulas once more, all three systems in one run (`evaluation/perf/report.md` and

changes, only the Since index (21.8) is in code it shares, so its numbers are the machine's

by a fair amount: `dense` moved from 1.37 to 1.39 against MonPoly on formulas it runs the same

| family | vdense/monpoly 21.8 | vdense/monpoly | vdense/dense |
|---|---|---|---|

| agg_fut | 1.91 | 1.77 | 0.90 |
| long | 1.31 | 1.31 | 0.92 |
| policy | 1.82 | 1.74 | 0.93 |
| once_scale | 1.21 | 1.15 | 0.95 |
| eventually | 1.29 | 1.35 | 0.96 |

The boxed monitor times out on two as well.  Verdicts identical on every formula, for both

What is left between the verified evaluator and MonPoly is unchanged in kind from 21.7's list

MonPoly does with hash tables) and the policy formulas that combine the two.

### 21.15 The average over a window, which was never incremental (`Optimized_Agg.thy`)

21.14's suite left two vdense timeouts, both aggregations over a window, and 21.7 had put the
whole `agg_once` family down to "the kernel structure rebuilding its result at every time
point".  Profiling the family formula by formula says otherwise.  Where `uag_res` goes, on
`base1500.log`:

| formula | reads the aggregate through | share of the run | total | MonPoly |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `aggonce-sum50` | `ures_maggaux` | 17.9% | 3.038 Gi | 2.270 Gi |
| `aggonce-cnt50` | `ures_maggaux` | 20.5% | 3.068 Gi | 2.268 Gi |
| `aggonce-min50` | `ures_maggaux` | 20.4% | 3.708 Gi | 1.937 Gi |
| `aggonce-avg50` | **`uagg`**, the generic `eval_agg` | **85.7%** | **21.955 Gi** | 3.259 Gi |

`AVG` was not using the incremental structure at all.  It was clustering the whole window's
table into groups at every time point, for 6.7x MonPoly and a timeout, while the neighbouring
aggregations were at 1.3x.

**Why.**  `init_maggaux` matched `(Agg_Avg, TInt)`.  The second component of `aggargs_ω` is the
aggregation's *result* type, not the type of the values: for `Agg_Min`, `Agg_Max` and `Agg_Sum`
the two coincide, but `t_res Agg_Avg _ = TFloat` (`Typing.thy`, and `rewriting.ml`'s
`type_check_aggregation (TCst TFloat) exp_num_typ` agrees), so an `Agg_Avg` node never arrives
with `TInt` and that branch was unreachable.  `AVG` fell through to the catch-all and got no
structure.  `Agg_Med` is typed `TFloat` the same way and *is* listed at `TFloat`, so this reads
as a line that was corrected for the result-type slot once and missed the other case.

Everything the incremental version needs was already there and already proved:
`result_maggaux`'s `SumAux` branch handles `Agg_Avg` (sum over count, converted once), and
`valid_maggaux` admits `aggtype = Agg_Sum ∨ aggtype = Agg_Avg` for `SumAux` without mentioning
the result type at all -- so `valid_insert_maggaux`, `valid_delete_maggaux` and
`valid_result_maggaux`, which are stated over `valid_maggaux`, cover the combination as they
stand.  The change is one branch in `init_maggaux` and one in `dinit_maggaux`, and
`valid_init_maggaux_unfolded`'s blanket `cases; cases; auto` discharges the new case exactly as
it discharged the unreachable one.

**Why this is sound over floats.**  Addition of floats is not associative, so an incremental sum
must never see one.  It does not: `SumAux` accumulates an `integer`, `insert_sum` and
`delete_sum` match `EInt` alone and answer `(False, Mapping.empty)` on anything else, and
`insert_maggaux'` turns that into `None`, which is a one-way latch -- the node then evaluates
the aggregation generically for the rest of the run.  So `AVG` over floats keeps the behaviour
it has today (at the cost of one build and discard at the first tuple), and `AVG` over integers
computes the reference's own value: `eval_agg_op (Agg_Avg, ty)` folds `plus` over the flattened
multiset, which on `EInt` elements is exact integer addition, then converts once and divides
once -- and `double_of_event_data (EInt x) = double_of_event_data_agg (EInt x) =
double_of_integer x`.  The same argument is what already carries `(Agg_Med, TFloat)` over
integer values.

**Measured.**  `aggonce-avg50` under callgrind: 21.955 Gi → 4.594 Gi, **-79%**, against MonPoly's
3.259 Gi -- from 6.7x to 1.41x, in line with the rest of the family.  `uagg` has left its
profile; `uag_res` now reaches `ures_maggaux` like the others.  On the suite's own log both
evaluators stop timing out: `aggonce-avg50` 2.80 s under vdense and 3.65 s under dense against
MonPoly's 1.36 s, where both were over the 5 s budget; `aggonce-avg5` 1.71 s against 2.55 s in
21.14's run.  The differential corpus is unchanged at 593 identical / 7 refused / 0 differing,
and `difftest.sh` has vdense, dense and MonPoly agreeing on `agg`, `aggwide` and `aggonce` (the
`matchp` mismatch it reports predates this round).

The remaining vdense timeout, `aggev-sum20`, is an aggregation over a *future* window: it runs
5.3 s under vdense but 2.8 s under dense, so it is the unboxed refinement of the Until-side
structure that costs there, not the aggregation.  That is its own item.

### 21.16 The rest of the generated code, monomorphised (`Dense_Mono_Sweep.thy`)

20.x and 21.6's `Monomorphic_Code.thy` and `Dense_Code_Opt.thy` monomorphised what the profiles
charged for -- the containers at `event_data tuple` and at `event_data list`.  This section is
about the rest: every operation the export still reaches with a type class dictionary in hand,
at a type that is closed and known.  It is not a performance item.  The measured payoff of the
largest single case, `insert_treelist`, is 0.03% of `aggonce-min50` (1.25 Mi of 3708 Mi, with
the whole rank path under 0.6%), and the sweep changes no benchmark measurably.  It is here
because the exported code is meant to be monomorphic throughout: a dictionary that survives is
a dictionary a reader of the export has to account for.

**What the count of 245 dictionary-taking definitions actually contains.**  Three kinds, and
only two are work:

- *Operations at one closed type* -- 70 of them, headed by `Set.member` at `nat` (29 call
  sites), `List.superset`, `List.member`, `comparator_of`, `sum_list`, `Max`,
  `atLeastLessThan`, `filter_set`, `wf_table_of_set_args`, `set_of_idx`, the `mregex` set
  operations of the match automata and the `string8 × nat` mapping of the predicate table.
- *Operations at several closed types* -- the subset test (`nat` and `mregex`) and the treelists
  of the rank aggregations (`integer` and `string8`); one copy each.
- *Dictionary constructors* -- `ccompare_list`, `ceq_option`, `equal_prod`, `heap_list`,
  `set_empty`, `mapping_empty`, and so on.  These are not targets at all.  They exist only to
  feed a generic consumer and disappear as the consumers are specialised, and they are the bulk
  of both the multi-instantiation list (`ccompare_list` alone is 168 call sites) and of the 133
  definitions that never see a concrete dictionary because their caller forwards its own.

So the actionable set is the first two kinds, and the third is consequence.

**How.**  No new mechanism: `monomorphize.ML`'s two commands, which 20.x introduced.  Each
instance family needs its class operations as explicit equations first (`nat_dict_simps`,
`ed_dict_simps`, `mregex_dict_simps`, `s8_dict_simps`, mirroring `etuple_dict_simps` and
`edl_dict_simps`), and then the specs are a list.  Thirty-two landed in this pass.

**What did not go through.**  Four constants -- `all_interval`, `these`,
`gadd_new_ts_msaux_add` and `safe_regex` -- fail to derive: `Code.read_const` resolves each to a
constant whose type variable is fixed rather than schematic, so `monomorphize` has nothing to
instantiate.  Lifting that is a change to `monomorphize.ML`, not a new spec.  And the set
operations of the recursion check must stay generic: specialising `Regex.regex.atms`
at `rec_safety` makes the code generator demand `rec_safety :: {finite_UNIV, ceq, ccompare}`,
arities that type does not have, and the export fails outright.  Both are recorded rather than
worked around.

**Measured.**  Outputs are unchanged by construction and in fact: every hash of `difftest.sh` is
identical to the pre-sweep binary's, and the differential corpus stays at 593 identical, 7
refused, 0 differing.  On the export itself: `src/verified.ml` 13901 → 13549 lines, the
dictionary-taking definitions 248 → 223, and the applications that pass a concrete dictionary
1214 → 1066, **-12.2%**, over two passes (32 specs and then 19 more).  The suite is unchanged
within its noise.

**What is left, and why the count moved less than the specs suggest.**  Removing the dominant
instance of a generic function does not remove the function while another instance still reaches
it, and it promotes the next instance into view: `Set.member` left the list at `nat` and
re-entered it at `mregex`, and `Mapping.map_values` at a second value type.  The sweep is
therefore iterative rather than one-shot: the second pass here took a layer that the first
had made visible.  After both, 223 dictionary-taking definitions remain, of which 58 are reached
at one closed type.  What is left, in order of call sites:
`equal_option`/`cproper_interval_list`/`set_impl_proda` (dictionary constructors -- they go when
their consumers do), `equal_set` at a set of tables, the Containers distinct-list and
red-black-tree set implementations (`filterd`, `filterc`, `filtere`, `memberc`,
`list_of_dlist`), the three heap-index operations `im_new`/`im_lookup_h`/`im_update_h` at a
second value type, `upd_cfi`, `set_fold_cfc`, and the four that need the `monomorphize.ML`
change above.  None of them needs a new technique, only the next pass.

|---|---|---|

| dense/monpoly | 1.39 | 1.38 |
| vdense/dense | 0.96 | 0.96 |
| vdense timeouts | 2 | **1** |
| dense timeouts | 2 | **0** |
| vdense formulas above 4x MonPoly | 0 | 0 |
| dense formulas above 4x MonPoly | 3 | 3 |

`aggonce-avg50` is the change: 2.66 s under vdense and 3.38 s under dense against MonPoly's

The monomorphisation sweep is invisible here, as expected: every difftest hash is identical to
the pre-sweep binary's and no family moves beyond the machine's own drift (the families that

which is the size of that drift and the reason the within-run `vdense/dense` column is the one
to read).

The `agg_once` family is now 1.94x MonPoly and 0.84 of the boxed evaluator.  What is left in it
is the space item 21.15's profiling turned up and did not touch: instruction counts across the
family are flat in the window length at about 1.35x MonPoly, but wall time is not (1.74x at
window 5, 2.34x at window 500) and it tracks peak resident memory almost exactly (1.50x to
2.29x), with the garbage collector's share of the run going 11.5% -> 23.3% where MonPoly's goes
11.4% -> 13.6%.  The Since/Once window stores each in-window tuple three times -- once per
occurrence in `data_in`, once in `table_in`, and once as a key of `tuple_in`, with `table_in` an
exact parallel copy of `Mapping.keys tuple_in` kept so that the result read is O(1).  That is
`mmsaux` in the shared kernel, so it is a datatype change to `Optimized_Since.thy` and a larger
piece of work than anything in 21.8-21.16; it is recorded here rather than started.

### 21.18 The aggregation over a future window, fused (`Dense_Evt_UAgg.thy`, `Dense_Unbox_UAgg.thy`)

21.17's suite left one vdense timeout, `aggev-sum20` (`r <- SUM y; x EVENTUALLY[0,20] A(x,y)`),
and the reason was visible in the source rather than only in the profile.  `udof`'s `MSince`
case with an aggregation has two branches -- the fused `udsx_init_agg` when the left operand is
closed at the node's frame, the generic `UDAgg` over a plain `UDSince` otherwise -- and the
`MUntil` case thirty lines below has only the second.  There was no `udux_init_agg` anywhere.
So every aggregation over a future window clustered the whole window's union into groups once
per time point: on `aggev-sum20`, 81.5% of the run in `uagg`, 8.763 Gi against the boxed
evaluator's 4.575 Gi.

**The structure.**  `uwagg` pairs the Until window with the kernel's incremental aggregation, as
`swagg` does for the Since window, but the Until forces two differences.  Its answers are
settled late -- a time point's answer is fixed only once the window has run past it -- so
`uw_step` pushes a snapshot of the union onto a list of finished answers that `uw_eval` hands
over later.  Fusing therefore means, first, that the aggregate is maintained alongside the count
set through the scan that extends the window (`ua_extend`) and through the deletion in
`ua_step`, and second, that what is pushed is the aggregate's own reading at that moment rather
than the raw union.  The list of finished answers holds aggregated tables from then on, and that
is the whole saving.

Nothing of the aggregation had to be rebuilt: `ag_init`, `ag_ins`, `ag_del`, `ag_res` and the
invariant `ag_inv` of 21.x are stated over a count set, not over the Since window, so the Until
reuses them and their preservation lemmas as they stand.

**The proof** is a simulation against the plain window.  `ua_rel` says the seven components
agree except that the finished answers are the plain ones under `dagg_p`, and that the aggregate
is valid for the current union.  `ua_extend_proj` gives the tracking, `ua_extend_rep` the
invariant across the scan's insertions (mirroring `sa_move_rep`), `ua_step_rel` the step -- where
`ag_res_inv` turns the aggregate's reading into `dagg_p` of the union and `ag_inv_del` carries
the deletion -- and `ua_shift_rel` the recursion over them.  `valid_ua_eval` states exactly the
property the fusion is for: the answers handed over are the plain window's under `dagg_p`.

**The wiring** follows the Since's, rule for rule.  `duaux_x` gains `DUX_wagg` and the
`dux_post`/`dux_agg` pair that names what a structure reports; `duxaux'` gains its case; the
Until node's result is stated through `dsresh ... (dux_post daux)` and the two plain `dsim`
cases record `dux_agg daux = None`; `DsUntilFused` is the new case, a bare `DUntil` whose
structure reports the aggregate.  `UDUX_wagg` and `rua` carry it to the short tuples, and `dof`,
`edof` and `udof` all emit the fused node when the window applies -- the left operand closed at
the node's frame and the interval containing zero.

**Measured.**  `aggev-sum20` under callgrind: 8.763 Gi → 2.846 Gi, **-68%**, with `uagg` and its
clustering gone from the profile and `uag_res` reaching `ures_maggaux` at 18%, like every other
aggregation.  On the suite's own log it runs 1.73 s against MonPoly's 1.70 s, where it was over
the 5 s budget.  The `agg_fut` family goes to 0.76 of the boxed evaluator and 1.50x MonPoly,
from 1.75x.  Outputs are unchanged: every `difftest.sh` hash matches and the differential corpus
stays at 593 identical / 7 refused / 0 differing.

### 21.19 The suite: no timeouts left

| | 21.14 | 21.17 | now |
|---|---|---|---|
| vdense/monpoly, all 21 families | 1.34 | 1.33 | 1.35 |
| vdense/dense | 0.96 | 0.96 | **0.96** |
| vdense timeouts | 2 | 1 | **0** |
| dense timeouts | 2 | 0 | 2 |
| vdense formulas above 4x MonPoly | 0 | 0 | **0** |

The verified unboxed evaluator now finishes all 185 formulas inside the 5 s budget, with
identical verdicts throughout.  The `vdense/monpoly` column moved the wrong way between the last
two runs by more than the change warrants -- the families this round did not touch move by up to
0.07 in it -- which is the machine's drift again, and the reason the within-run `vdense/dense`
column is the one to read.  There, the two families this round and 21.15 touched are the ones
that moved: `agg_fut` 0.90 → **0.76** and `agg_once` 0.84 → **0.83**, with everything else within
0.02 of where it was.

What separates the verified evaluator from MonPoly is now one thing rather than three.  The
aggregations over a window stand at 1.5-2.0x, and 21.15's profiling says why: their instruction
counts are flat in the window length at about 1.35x MonPoly, but wall time is not, and it tracks
peak resident memory (1.50x at window 5, 2.29x at window 500) with the garbage collector's share
going 11.5% → 23.3% where MonPoly's goes 11.4% → 13.6%.  The Since/Once window stores each
in-window tuple three times -- once per occurrence in `data_in`, once in `table_in`, and once as
a key of `tuple_in`, with `table_in` an exact parallel copy of `Mapping.keys tuple_in` kept so
that the result read is O(1).  That is `mmsaux` in the shared kernel, and a datatype change to
`Optimized_Since.thy`: the next item, and a larger one than anything in 21.8-21.18.

### 21.20 The three slowest formulas, and what is actually left

`pol-threshold` (3.85x MonPoly), `aggonce-min50` (3.29x) and `aggonce-max50` (3.24x) are the
suite's worst, and `aggonce-cnt50` is the right control: the same shape as the other two, at
2.1x.  Under callgrind on `base1500.log`:

| formula | vdense | monpoly | Ir ratio | wall ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `pol-threshold` | 2.995 Gi | 1.267 Gi | 2.36 | 3.85 |
| `aggonce-min50` | 3.711 Gi | 1.937 Gi | 1.92 | 3.29 |
| `aggonce-cnt50` | 3.092 Gi | 2.267 Gi | 1.36 | ~2.1 |

**The ratios flatter us.**  Where the instructions go:

| | tree and comparators | GC | output | hashing |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `pol-threshold` vdense | **33.1%** | 19.2% | 0.0% | -- |
| `pol-threshold` monpoly | 0% | 23.0% | 0.0% | 26.9% |
| `aggonce-min50` vdense | **24.6%** | 23.4% | 0.7% | -- |
| `aggonce-min50` monpoly | 0% | 8.1% | **20.9%** | 6.2% |
| `aggonce-cnt50` vdense | **30.9%** | 18.9% | 0.7% | -- |
| `aggonce-cnt50` monpoly | 0% | 12.2% | **17.8%** | 15.0% |

MonPoly spends a fifth of the two printing formulas in `printf` and `memcpy`, which our front end
does in 0.7%.  Net of output its monitoring work on `aggonce-min50` is about 1.53 Gi, so the
honest ratio there is **2.4x**, not 1.92 -- and the three then agree at about 2.4x.  A quarter to
a third of each of our runs is red-black tree balancing, insertion and comparison, against
MonPoly's nothing: it does the same job with hash tables.  That is the whole structural gap, and
it is the same root as the space finding of 21.15.

**`pol-threshold` computes an answer it then throws away.**  Over 1500 time points it emits
\<^bold>‹zero› verdicts: with a window of 60 and 40 events per time point over a domain of 200,
a group holds about twelve tuples and `40 < r` never fires.  We nevertheless build the full
~200-row aggregate table at every time point -- `uag_res` into `ures_maggaux`, **21.2%** of the
run -- and the constraint node then filters all of it away.  MonPoly's computation is
correspondingly cheaper than its own on `aggonce-cnt50` (1.27 Gi against 1.9 Gi net of output),
which is the whole of its advantage here.

**MIN and MAX: 21.15 was wrong about the rank structure.**  That section read the treelists off
the \<^emph>‹exclusive› profile, where `insert_treelist` is 0.03%, and concluded the rank path was
cold.  Inclusively it is not:

| | `aggonce-cnt50` | `aggonce-min50` |
|---|---|---|
| `insert_maggaux` | 127.1 Mi (4.1%) | 296.8 Mi (8.0%) |
| `delete_maggaux` | 129.7 Mi (4.2%) | 295.6 Mi (8.0%) |
| `set_fold_cfc` over them | 256.5 Mi | 592.2 Mi |
| GC | 18.9% | 23.4% |

The rank insert and delete cost **2.3x** the count's, and that +336 Mi is more than half of
`aggonce-min50`'s +619 Mi over `aggonce-cnt50`; the rest is the bigger per-group state
(`usa_add_new_table` +279 Mi, `usa_expire` +192 Mi) and the GC that state costs.  Per tuple the
rank path does the same map lookup and update as the count, plus a treelist insertion and a fresh
`LInt` wrapper -- allocation the count avoids by storing a `nat`.

**Three items, priced.**

1. *Push a result-column constraint into the aggregate's read.*  On `pol-threshold` this turns a
   21.2% table build, and the filter pass after it, into almost nothing, and it is the whole
   `policy` family's shape -- an aggregate compared against a threshold.  It is C3's fold with a
   guard, plus a `udof` branch recognising a constraint that mentions only the result column, and
   a `dsim` case: about the size of 21.18.
2. *An incremental result for the window's aggregate.*  Measured on this log, **36 of 200 groups
   change per time point**, so rebuilding only those would cut the 21% result build by about five
   times, on every windowed aggregation.  21.13 declined this on an estimate of "about half the
   groups"; the measurement says 18%, which makes it materially better than recorded.  It still
   costs a datatype change to `aggaux` in the shared kernel.
3. *Drop the count set's second half where the aggregation is fused.*  `gcnt_add1` and `gcnt_del1`
   keep a mapping from tuple to count \<^emph>‹and› the set of tuples with a positive count, but with
   the aggregation fused `ag_res` reads `result_maggaux` and never `snd cc`.  That is one set
   insertion and one set removal per tuple of dead work -- about 80 tree operations per time point
   against the result's 200, so on the order of 5%.  Self-contained, and the same redundancy as
   `table_in` against `tuple_in` in `mmsaux`, one level down.

## 21.21 The guard and the count structure: two of the three, measured

**F1, the guard.**  `(r <- CNT y; x ONCE[0,60] A(x,y)) AND 40 < r` built the full ~200-row
aggregate table at every time point and then filtered all of it away.  The constraint is now the
node's *guard*: the fused aggregating Since carries it, and `ugmr`'s fold inserts only the groups
that pass, so the rows that fail are never built.

The guard is the short evaluator's alone, which is what keeps the change small.  `UDSX_wagg`
carries it, `rdsx` drops it, and `Rep_udformula` of a guarded Since is `EDAndRel` over the boxed
node -- exactly what `edof` emits for `MAndRel`.  So the boxed evaluator, the three compilers'
boxed halves and the simulation `dsim` are untouched; `udof` sets the guard through `uset_q` when
the node it just compiled is a fused aggregating Since, and the correctness of that is one line
(`Rep_uset_q`).  21.20's plan put the guard into `daggp` and so into `dsim`; going through
`Rep_udformula` instead saves two `dsim` constructors and their proof cases.

The wrap costs one lemma: `size (Rep_udformula φ) = size φ` is no longer true, and it was needed
only to make `letpast_meval0`'s size guard take the same branch on the two sides.  It is replaced
by the fact that *both* evaluators return a formula of the size they were given
(`psize_snd_udeval`, `psize_snd_edeval`, both already proved), so that guard now never fires on
either side -- a premise that says what is actually going on.

**F3, the count structure.**  The count structure kept every tuple's count in a mapping *and* the
tuples themselves in a set beside it, so that the plain window could hand out its union in one
step.  The fused windows never hand out that union.  They now keep the mapping alone: what enters
is what the mapping does not hold yet and what leaves is what it holds exactly once, both one
lookup per tuple where they were a set difference and a lookup.  `ucc` reads the old structure off
the mapping -- its key set is the mapping's domain, which is what the second half always held --
so the naturality against the boxed window goes through unchanged and neither the boxed window nor
the aggregate's invariant moves.  Both fused windows, Since and Until, keep the counts this way.

| | 21.20 | + F1 | + F3 | total | MonPoly | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `pol-threshold` | 2.995 Gi | 2.502 Gi | **2.104 Gi** | **-29.8%** | 1.264 Gi | 2.37x → **1.66x** |
| `aggonce-min50` | 3.711 Gi | 3.695 Gi | **3.209 Gi** | **-13.5%** | 1.937 Gi | 1.92x → **1.66x** |
| `aggonce-cnt50` | 3.092 Gi | 3.080 Gi | **2.684 Gi** | **-13.2%** | 2.265 Gi | 1.37x → **1.18x** |

F1 is worth -16.5% where a constraint is there to push and nothing at all where there is none
(-0.4% on the two `aggonce`, which is noise): the `None` guard is one match per group.  F3 is
worth -13% to -16% on all three.  The 21.20 estimate for F3 was "on the order of 5%"; it counted
the set operations against the result build's 200 insertions and forgot that the set operations
run over *every* tuple while the result build runs over every *group*.

What is left is the third item.  `ures_maggaux` is still **22.5%** of `aggonce-min50` and
**22.9%** of `aggonce-cnt50`, and on `pol-threshold`, where the guard now keeps almost all of it
from being built, **7.7%**.  The measurement that motivates it stands: 36 of 200 groups change per
time point, and the read rebuilds all 200.

## 21.22 The incremental result, priced properly and declined

21.20 priced the third item -- keeping the window's aggregate result across time points instead of
rebuilding it -- at "about five times" the result build, from the measurement that *36 of 200
groups change per time point*.  Both halves of that estimate were wrong, and in the same
direction.

**The counting.**  The 36 is the number of distinct groups in the tuples that *enter* the window.
As many leave it, from a different draw, so the groups whose row changes between two reads is the
union of the two:

```
$ python3  # over evaluation/perf/logs, the log the profiles run on
A-events per time point   40
distinct x per time point 36.42        (x is the group column)
distinct x overall        200
```

With a window of 50 and 40 events a time point the leaving draw is independent of the entering
one, so the union is 36.4 + 36.4 - 36.4²/199 ≈ **66 of 199 groups, a third**, not 18%.

**The unit cost.**  The estimate also assumed that patching a group costs what building its row
costs.  It does not.  Building a row is one structure operation -- read the value, insert the row
into the answer.  Patching one is four: the group's value before the change, its value after,
the removal of the old row from the answer and the insertion of the new one.  Neither of the two
reads can be dropped: the answer is keyed by the whole row, so the old row cannot be found from
the group alone, and the kernel's insertion does not report what it overwrote.

The two costs are measurable and they are the same.  On `aggonce-cnt50`, over 1500 time points:

| | inclusive | elements | per element |
|---|---|---|---|
| `insert_maggaux` (one mapping update per tuple) | 4.84% of 2.684 Gi | 1500 × 40 tuples | **2165 instr** |
| `ures_maggaux` (one row built and inserted per group) | 22.80% of 2.684 Gi | 1500 × 199 groups | **2050 instr** |

So the incremental read would cost 66 × 4 = **264** structure operations a time point where the
rebuild costs **199**.  It is a 1.3x *loss*, and the best conceivable variant -- if the kernel's
insertion and deletion were changed to report each group's value before and after, so that the two
reads came free -- would be 66 × 2 = 132, a 34% saving on a 22.8% line, about 8% overall, for a
change to the shared kernel's aggregation structure.  Declined, again, and this time with the
arithmetic.

It would pay where the touched groups are under a quarter of the groups present: a window that
moves slowly against its group domain, or a domain much larger than the per-time-point event
count.  That is a property of the workload, not of the formula, so it cannot be decided at compile
time; it would have to be a runtime choice between two representations, which is a larger change
than the 8% is worth.

**What the read actually costs.**  It is not the tuple building.  `ures_maggaux` is 22.8% and the
insertion into the answer set is 18.6% of the run; what is left, the row's construction, is under
a fifth of the line.  We also checked whether the answer could be built from a sorted list in
linear time instead of by insertion -- the rows come out of the mapping in group order, so they
would already be sorted if the result column sorted after the group columns.  It does not:

```
PROBE calls=1000 sorted=0 avg_groups=199 y=0
```

The result column is **column 0** on all three formulas, so the rows come out ordered by group and
have to go in ordered by (value, group); not one of the thousand reads produced a sorted list.
Sorting them first and building in linear time trades the insertions' comparisons for a sort's --
the same count -- and saves only the rebalancing, so it is not worth its own code path either.

The remaining gap to MonPoly on these three formulas is what 21.20 measured: it does this with
hash tables and we do it with red-black trees, and Containers offers no hashed set.

## 21.23 The suite after the two

The 185-formula suite, re-run in the configuration the published report was produced in (three
systems, three repetitions, two workers; 866 s of wall time against the earlier 869 s, so the
contention is the same):

| | before 21.21 | after |
|---|---|---|
| geometric mean vdense/monpoly | 1.35 | **1.31** |
| median | 1.26 | 1.26 |
| vdense faster | 20 | **25** |
| within 10% | 45 | **50** |
| more than 2x slower | 22 | **16** |
| worst ratio | 3.85 | **2.85** |

The two families the work was aimed at move, and nothing else does:

| family | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| `agg_once` (22) | 1.98 | **1.70** |
| `policy` (10) | 1.79 | **1.67** |
| `agg_fut` (5) | 1.50 | **1.30** |
| `since` (13) | 1.45 | 1.46 |
| `join` (9) | 1.13 | 1.11 |
| `once` (16) | 1.21 | 1.21 |

`pol-threshold`, the suite's worst formula before, is no longer in the first eight: 3.85x and
1.98 s became **2.37x and 1.28 s**.  `aggonce-min50` and `aggonce-max50` went from 3.29x and 3.24x
to 2.66x and 2.64x.  The slowest four are now `onceex-50`, `pol-sincefutb`, `pol-unmatched` and
`aggonce-min50`, all between 2.66x and 2.85x -- the first three are the `EXISTS`-under-`ONCE`
shape, which is a different item from this section's three, and no formula in the suite is more
than 2.9x.

Verdicts agree with MonPoly on all 185, as before, and the differential tests and the 593-formula
corpus are unchanged (593 same, 7 refused, 0 differing).

## 21.24 The temporal regex nodes, in the verified dense evaluator

`MATCHP` and `MATCHF` were the last two operators the dense compiler refused: `dof` answered
`DFail` at them, so `-vdense` refused every formula that contained one, and the shipping monitor
(`-verified`, the heap-resident dense monitor of `Dense_Imp.thy`) was the only one that ran them.
They are now dense nodes like the rest.

### The node

The kernel's match nodes keep a window of tabulated regex derivatives -- `mrδaux` for the past,
`mlδaux` for the future -- over tuples of the kernel's width, and hand each operand's table to
`update_matchP`/`update_matchF` unchanged.  The dense node keeps that window at its own frame:
`cs` is the regex's free variables, the window runs at width `length cs`, and each operand's
table, which sits at the operand's own narrower frame, is widened into the node's frame on the
way in by `dembedp` through the plan `emb_plan cs (css ! i)` -- the device the trigger node
already used.  `Dense_Aux.thy` already had the narrowing layer for both windows (`dmrd_rel`,
`dmld_rel`, the two locales and `dtabc_update_matchP`/`dtabc_update_matchF`/`dtabc_eval_matchF`),
written for exactly this and unused until now; the node is those lemmas plus the buffer.

`dsim` gains `DsMatchP` and `DsMatchF`, and the simulation theorem their two cases.  The buffer
side is `dbufn_nt_take`, the `dbufn`-level wrapper of a new `mbufnt_take_rel` in `Dense.thy`:
stepping the kernel's buffered traversal and the dense one in lockstep preserves any relation the
step function preserves, which is how the trigger's and the multi-way join's buffers were already
handled.

### What the compiler needed

Every other dense node recovers its operands' frames from what the kernel node stores -- `MAnd`
from `A`/`B`, `MAnds` from `A_pos`/`A_neg`, the temporal nodes from `args_L`/`args_R`, `MAgg` from
`aggargs_cols`.  A match node stores nothing of the kind: its operand list is just the atoms of
the regex.  So the compiler now carries `mfcols`, which descends an `mformula` and answers the
columns its result has, or `None` where it cannot tell.  It has to descend rather than read a
stored set, because `mok n A tw (MRel {})` holds for every `A`: a node that produces the empty
table is well formed at any frame, so the criterion alone does not pin the frame down.  `mfcols`
answers `None` at `MRel` and at a nested match node, and `dof` then answers `DFail` there, which
the caller's own criterion (`dofz`, the count of fail nodes) rejects -- sound, if not complete.

### The sanitiser

The unboxed evaluator (`Dense_Unbox_Exec.thy`) runs on tuples without the option around the
fields.  The match nodes are the one place where it does not have its own structure: they keep
the kernel's window, so the tables inside stay boxed, and the node widens its operands on the way
in and strips the option off the result on the way out.  Stripping and restoring is `dsan`, the
identity exactly on hole-free tables, so the boxed node had to apply it too -- as the trigger node
already did.  It is the identity there: the node's frame is the regex's free variables, so the
table the window hands back is full, which the two simulation cases show from the tables the
kernel's own node produces (`produces_tables` at the node, then `dsan_dtabc`).

### The suite

Eleven formulas, one group: the two operators with one test, with a choice, with a concatenation
and with a star; the future one on the bursty log; an operand narrower than the node's frame; and
an aggregate over a past regex.

| geomean over the 11 | ratio |
|---|---|
| vdense / monpoly | **1.55** |
| dense / monpoly | 1.01 |
| vdense / dense | **1.54** (1.12 to 2.62) |

MonPoly monitors the temporal regex operators after all, and on this group the shipping monitor
is level with it.  The verified dense evaluator pays 1.55x, which is the suite's second-worst
family after `once_exists` -- the same ratio as `policy`.  The gap is the window itself: the node
runs the kernel's `update_matchP`/`update_matchF` over the kernel's boxed tuples, so a match node
is the one place where the short-tuple evaluator widens its operands on the way in and strips the
option off on the way out, and the tabulated derivatives are the kernel's structure rather than
one written for the dense frame.  Nothing here narrows that: the point of the item is that the
operators are in the fragment at all.  A dense window over short tuples is the obvious next item
for this family, in the shape of the Since and Until structures of sections 13 and 16.

The whole suite, now 196 formulas in 22 families, is unchanged where it was not touched: geomean
vdense/monpoly 1.29 against 1.31 before, median 1.26, 33 formulas where the verified evaluator is
faster, 16 more than 2x slower, none more than 4x.

`match-pcnt` (`r <- CNT x MATCHP[0,20] C(x)?`) is the suite's one verdict disagreement, and it is
not the regex: both verified monitors print a final `@MaxTS (time point 6000): (0)` that MonPoly
does not, which is the end-of-stream behaviour of a count over an empty group.  The two verified
evaluators agree on it tuple for tuple, as they do on the other ten.

## 21.25 The fused And-Trigger, in the verified dense evaluator

After 21.24 the dense compiler refused exactly one kernel node: `MAndTrigger`, the kernel's
fusion of `α AND (φ TRIGGER I ψ)`.  `dof` answered `DFail` there, so `-vdense` refused every
formula containing one; the seven formulas of the 600-formula corpus it refused were all of
this shape.  It is now a dense node, and with it every constructor of the kernel's `mformula`
has one: the catch-all `DFail` equations of `dof`, `edof` and `udof` are gone.

### Which And-Trigger reaches the node

When `I` contains 0, the kernel initialises the conjunction as an ordinary `MAnd` over a
standalone `MTrigger`, and both already had dense nodes.  So `MAndTrigger` arises only for an
interval that misses 0, and then the safety of the trigger forces its two operands onto the
same free variables and the trigger onto the positive side.  The compiler's guard says exactly
this, as `tok cs_R cs_L args ⟷ mem I 0 ∨ (cs_L = cs_R ∧ args_pos args)`, together with the
frames the other nodes check: the conjunct at the node's frame `cs`, the trigger at
`cs_R ⊆ cs`, its left operand at `cs_L ⊆ cs_R`.

### The node

`DAndTrigger` runs two stages.  The first is the standalone trigger node's: the two operands'
tables go through a buffer into the narrowed trigger structure (`gupdate_mmtaux`), and each time
point's read comes out as an option: `None` while the window is empty, `Some` of the trigger's
answer at its own frame `cs_R` otherwise.  The second stage buffers the conjunct's tables
against those reads and joins: `None` lets the conjunct's table through unchanged, and `Some z`
joins it with `z` through the plan `mk_djoin cs cs_R`.

The option is the one real difference from the kernel.  The kernel's fused node reads the
trigger structure as a pair of a column set and a table, and on an empty window that pair is the
full table over no columns (`({}, unit_table n)`), which its `bin_join` then lets through.  A
dense table over no columns has no such reading at the trigger's frame, so the dense node keeps
the case apart, and `dtres` relates the two reads; `dtres_join` shows that the two joins agree.

### The invariant

The standalone trigger node never keeps left tuples keyed in its structure, so the narrowing
invariant of 21.9 and 21.12 (`tnar`) was the whole story there.  The fused node's
structure does keep them, because the interval misses 0 and the left operand's tuples have to
wait.  Its read then unions in their widening rather than the tuples themselves, and the two
agree exactly when the widening is the identity on what is stored.  That is the second
invariant, `tfix`.  It holds vacuously while nothing is stored, which covers the standalone
case, and outright when `cs_L = cs_R` (the mask is all true, `dmsk_all`), which the guard
guarantees.  `dtaux` is now `tnar ∧ tfix`, and its initialisation, update and read lemmas take
`tok` in place of `mem I 0`, so the standalone trigger node keeps its proofs unchanged.

`dsim` gains `DsAndTrigger`, and the simulation theorem gains its case.  The two buffers are
